CAMLPR Hematology Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Case CHB1

A 19-year-old woman of Mediterranean descent is found to have a mild anemia on pre-employment screening and has no symptoms. Her mother has a similar blood picture. Hemoglobin 112 g/L, RBC 5.60 x10^12/L, MCV 66 fL, RDW-CV 13.8 percent, ferritin 95 ug/L (reference 15 to 200). The film shows microcytes, hypochromic cells, target cells and occasional basophilic stippling.

  1. The correct answer is C (11.8, below 13, which favours thalassemia trait over iron deficiency in this patient): The index is 66 / 5.60 = 11.8, which is below 13 and favours thalassemia trait, supported by the high RBC count and normal ferritin. Dividing hemoglobin by RBC gives the MCH (20 pg), not the Mentzer index. Inverting the ratio gives 0.08.
  2. The correct answer is A (Suggest hemoglobin analysis by HPLC or electrophoresis before iron is given, because stores appear adequate): With normal ferritin, a low Mentzer index and target cells, a hemoglobin disorder is likely and iron would not help. Hemoglobin analysis by HPLC or electrophoresis is the standard follow-up. A marrow examination is excessive at this stage.
  3. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin A2 raised, usually above 3.5 percent, with hemoglobin A still the dominant fraction): Beta thalassemia trait is characterized by a raised hemoglobin A2, typically 3.5 to 6 percent, while hemoglobin A remains the main fraction. Hemoglobin S would indicate sickle trait, and hemoglobin H is found in alpha thalassemia, not here.
  4. The correct answer is B (Week 10, when the count is about 395 x10^9/L, which is already within the interval): Reading the plotted values, the count is 430 x10^9/L at week 8 and 395 x10^9/L at week 10, so week 10 is when the count reaches 400 or lower at the earliest point. Week 8 has fallen by more than half but is still higher than the limit. Week 12 is lower still, but the limit was already met two weeks earlier.
  5. The correct answer is C (It checks that the analyzer count agrees with the film before release, because discrepancies need resolving): A film estimate is a plausibility check: a few platelets per field is not consistent with a count of 410, so the discrepancy must be resolved before release. It does not replace the instrument count, report platelet volume or diagnose a disease. Common causes of such a mismatch include interference from small particles or red cell fragments.
  6. The correct answer is D (A helminth parasite infection, because tissue-invasive worms stimulate IL-5 driven eosinophil production in the marrow): Tissue-invasive helminths trigger IL-5 mediated eosinophil production, and a rural exposure with a stool ova and parasite request supports this cause. Acute bacterial and viral infections usually suppress or leave eosinophils unchanged, and chronic myeloid leukemia more typically shows basophilia and immature granulocytes rather than isolated eosinophilia.
  7. The correct answer is C (Mostly lymphocytes with fewer monocytes, because neutrophils are rare in a normal CSF): Normal adult CSF contains mostly small lymphocytes and a smaller share of monocytes, so a count dominated by either neutrophils or macrophages is abnormal. Neutrophils are not part of the normal pattern, and the CSF does not mirror the blood differential. Any granulocyte excess prompts a call to the supervisor or physician.
  8. The correct answer is A (16 x10^9/L, because 8 cells per field multiplied by 2000 gives 16,000 cells per uL): The estimate is the average count per field times the laboratory factor. Eight cells times 2000 gives 16,000 cells per uL, which converts to 16 x10^9/L because dividing cells per uL by 1000 gives the SI unit. The other values reflect a wrong factor or a wrong unit conversion.
  9. The correct answer is D (Calculate 0.32 x10^9/L neutrophils, which meets the flag, and report it urgently to the physician): The absolute neutrophil count is 0.80 x 0.40 = 0.32 x10^9/L, which is above the 0.25 x10^9/L flag that suggests spontaneous bacterial peritonitis. The flag is applied to the neutrophil count, not the total. Waiting for culture would delay urgent treatment.
  10. The correct answer is A (It clears fluid from the lumen so the sample reflects circulating blood): Fluid and medication fill the dead space of a line, so the first blood withdrawn is diluted or contaminated. Discarding it clears the lumen so the sample reflects the patient's circulating blood. The purpose is not to warm the line, test patency alone or remove platelets.
  11. The correct answer is B (2 mL, because 200 mL of total volume multiplied by 0.01 gives 2 mL): Total blood volume is 2.5 kg multiplied by 80 mL/kg, which equals 200 mL. One percent of 200 mL is 2 mL, the largest volume permitted for a single collection. Using ten percent or the per kilogram figure alone gives volumes that are wrong by a factor of ten or more.
  12. The correct answer is D (4.4 x10^9/L, because 88 x 20 divided by 0.4 uL gives 4400 per uL): Cells per uL equals cells counted x dilution factor divided by the counted volume. Four 1 mm2 squares at 0.1 mm deep hold 0.4 uL, so 88 x 20 / 0.4 equals 4400 cells per uL, or 4.4 x10^9/L. Leaving out the area or dividing by the dilution gives values that are far too high or too low.
  13. The correct answer is D (2-2s, indicating systematic error, so reject the run, hold patient results, investigate and log it): On days 10 and 11 two consecutive results exceed the same plus 2 SD limit, which is the 2-2s rule and points to systematic error. The run is rejected, patient results are held, and the cause is investigated and logged. Repeating until a pass or continuing to test hides the problem.
  14. The correct answer is C (About 4 hours after injection, where activity of 0.90 IU/mL lies within the target range): Peak anti-Xa after subcutaneous enoxaparin is reached about 4 hours after the dose, and the curve in the figure is highest there at roughly 0.90 IU/mL, inside the 0.6 to 1.0 range. Earlier and later times lie on the rising and falling limbs, and the 12 hour value is a trough, not a peak.
  15. The correct answer is C (Schistocytes, which indicate mechanical fragmentation of red cells by fibrin strands within small blood vessels): Schistocytes are red cell fragments sheared by fibrin strands in small vessels, which is why they appear in DIC with low platelets and fibrinogen. Spherocytes arise from membrane loss in immune hemolysis, target cells from excess membrane, and teardrop cells from marrow stress or fibrosis. None of these explains the coagulation picture.
  16. The correct answer is D (IgG, with or without complement, bound to the surface of the patient's red cells): Warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia is usually caused by IgG, sometimes with complement, coating the red cells. Macrophages in the spleen remove part of the membrane, which creates spherocytes and gives a positive DAT. Cold agglutinin disease instead shows complement C3d from IgM, and fibrin strands cause schistocytes, not spherocytes.
  17. The correct answer is B (No result needs confirmation, since the peak of 3.9 on day 17 is under 4.5): The highest plotted value is 3.9 on day 17, which is below the 4.5 trigger, so the rule does not require venous confirmation for any point. Values above the therapeutic band prompt dose review, but they are not the confirmation trigger in this procedure.
  18. The correct answer is C (Lavender tube, because EDTA binds calcium and preserves cell morphology and counts): EDTA removes calcium and keeps red cell, white cell and platelet size and shape stable for routine counting. Heparin works through antithrombin, not by binding calcium, and can distort white cell staining. Citrate dilutes the sample and fluoride is meant for glucose, so neither suits a standard CBC.
  19. The correct answer is D (Welcome the family member after explaining the procedure, because support respects the patient's wishes): Culturally safe care respects the patient's wishes and comfort when safety allows. Because the room is safe, welcoming the family member after explaining the procedure follows that principle. Excluding the family or requiring written approval treats a reasonable request as a problem.
  20. The correct answer is D (Let it bleed gently, wash with soap and running water, then report the exposure promptly): Immediate first aid is to let the wound bleed gently and wash it with soap and running water, then report so that a risk assessment can start. Squeezing hard or using bleach can damage tissue and does not improve outcomes. Delaying a report until symptoms or test results appear loses the time window in which prophylaxis works best.
  21. The correct answer is C (300 g/L, because hemoglobin divided by hematocrit gives the cell hemoglobin concentration): MCHC (g/L) = hemoglobin (g/L) / hematocrit (L/L) = 108 / 0.36 = 300 g/L, which equals the printed 30.0 g/dL. Keeping the numeral understates the value tenfold. Multiplying hemoglobin by hematocrit does not give a concentration.
  22. The correct answer is A (A markedly prolonged aPTT and thrombin time with only a mildly prolonged PT): Heparin strongly prolongs the aPTT and thrombin time because it acts through antithrombin on thrombin and factor Xa, whereas many PT reagents contain neutralizers that blunt its effect. A pattern with only a mild PT change fits contamination. Short clotting times or a prolonged PT with normal aPTT and thrombin time do not match heparin.
  23. The correct answer is A (Keep tubes at room temperature and deliver promptly, because cooling activates platelets and alters aggregation): Chilling activates platelets and changes their response, so aggregation specimens are kept at room temperature and tested promptly. Ice, refrigeration and freezing all damage or activate platelets and make the results unreliable.
  24. The correct answer is C (Hold the panel, document the failure, and send it to a validated backup laboratory): When the daily bead check fails and cleaning does not correct it, the instrument is out of control and patient results cannot be trusted. The panel is held, the failure is documented, and the specimen goes to a validated backup site within its stability window. Adjusting voltage without a documented procedure, or reporting percentages only, does not repair a sensitivity fault.
  25. The correct answer is D (165, above the reference interval, which favours a leukemoid reaction over chronic myeloid leukemia): Each score is multiplied by the number of cells: 0 + 30 + 50 + 45 + 40 = 165. This is above the reference interval, which is typical of a leukemoid reaction. Chronic myeloid leukemia usually gives a low LAP score.
  26. The correct answer is C (Macrocytic anemia with an inadequate reticulocyte response, pointing to impaired production not blood loss): An MCV of 112 fL is macrocytic and an absolute reticulocyte count of 20 x10^9/L is below the interval, so the marrow is not responding to the anemia. That points to a production defect such as B12 or folate deficiency. Hemolysis and bleeding would raise the reticulocyte count.
  27. The correct answer is A (A high plasma protein level, because excess protein takes up stain and tints the background): Excess plasma protein, as in paraprotein states, binds stain and gives a blue-gray background, and it often accompanies rouleaux. Because the control slide stained normally, the buffer, stain and timing are not at fault. Contaminated water would leave precipitate, not an even background tint.
  28. The correct answer is B (Immune thrombocytopenia, because isolated low platelets, normal coagulation results and a high MPV suggest destruction): Isolated severe thrombocytopenia, normal INR, aPTT and fibrinogen, a normal hemoglobin and a high MPV fit immune destruction, where the marrow releases young large platelets. TTP needs red cell fragmentation, DIC needs abnormal clotting tests, and aplastic anemia would lower the other cell lines and not raise the MPV.
  29. The correct answer is B (Distributions differ by sex, so one range misclassifies people, because androgens raise red cell mass): Mean hemoglobin is higher in adult males because androgens stimulate erythropoiesis, so the two groups form distinct distributions. A combined interval would label some healthy females as low and some anemic males as normal. Collection time and calibration do not explain a consistent biological difference.
  30. The correct answer is A (Centrifuge, remove the plasma, replace it with an equal volume of saline, remix and recount): Lipid turbidity falsely raises the absorbance used for hemoglobin, so the plasma blank must be removed. Spinning the tube, replacing the plasma with an equal volume of saline and recounting removes the lipid but keeps the cells at the same concentration. Heat does not clear lipid, and the hematocrit is not changed by the plasma swap.
  31. The correct answer is D (The draw was past peak, so the low value cannot be judged against the target): Anti-Xa is measured at the expected peak of about 4 hours after injection, and a sample 10 hours later reads low simply because the drug has been cleared. Treating the value as underdosing would lead to an unnecessary increase. Chromogenic anti-Xa is the standard test for LMWH, and an aPTT is insensitive to it.
  32. The correct answer is D (Decline to release it, directing him to the physician or health information office for consent): Release of health information needs the patient's consent or a legal authority, and being a caregiver does not by itself provide either. The technologist should decline and point the son to the physician or the health information office. Checking a birth date or faxing the report still discloses information improperly.
  33. The correct answer is A (All readings stayed within 2 to 8 C, so no corrective action was needed): Every reading lies between 3.9 and 4.4 C, inside the 2 to 8 C acceptance range, so the unit passed and no action is needed. The 0.5 C spread is ordinary variation, not a failure. A mid-range mean is acceptable, and there is no requirement to sit near either limit.
  34. The correct answer is C (A 40 percent decrease, which meets the limit and needs review before release): The change is the difference of 84 divided by the starting value of 210, which equals 0.40, or 40 percent, so the review limit is met. Dividing by the later value gives 67 percent, and 60 percent is the share that remains, so both are miscalculated. A 25 percent change does not match the numbers.
  35. The correct answer is A (The post could allow a patient to be identified, which would breach confidentiality): A post that shows an unusual blood film with the hospital and date could let someone link it to a patient, which breaches confidentiality. Image ownership, caption wording and incorrect comments are secondary issues. Public comments from friends increase the chance of identification.
  36. The correct answer is B (Test another 20 healthy people and reassess, because three outliers is a borderline result): With three or four outliers in the first twenty, the usual approach is to test a second group of twenty and judge the combined result. Accepting the interval outright ignores the guideline, and a full new study is premature after a borderline result. Deleting volunteers only because their values are extreme biases the limits.
  37. The correct answer is C (The man's result is flagged low, and the woman's falls within her interval): Adult male hemoglobin is higher than adult female hemoglobin, so 128 g/L is below the male interval but inside the female one. Using one combined interval would mislabel one of the two patients. Flags are only meaningful when the sex-specific range matches the patient.
  38. The correct answer is C (Excluding venous thromboembolism, because the assay has high sensitivity and a high negative predictive value): A D-dimer below the cut-off in a patient with low pretest probability makes venous thromboembolism very unlikely, because the assay is highly sensitive. It is not specific, so a raised value cannot confirm a clot, and the test neither excludes disseminated intravascular coagulation nor monitors anticoagulant therapy.
  39. The correct answer is A (Test a positive control and re-read the slide later, because stale reagent can give false negatives): A sickle preparation relies on a fresh reducing agent such as sodium metabisulfite, which deteriorates in an opened bottle and can give a false negative. A known sickle-positive control proves the reagent is working, and the slide should be re-read after a longer interval because deoxygenation takes time. A normal film does not mean trait and cannot be reported as such.
  40. The correct answer is D (Plasma trapped between packed red cells adds to the column, so spun values read slightly higher): Even a well-spun microhematocrit retains a small amount of plasma between red cells, about 1 to 3 percent of the column, which makes manual values slightly higher than values calculated from the red cell count and MCV. The buffy coat is not a factor in the calculated value, and speed or tube coating do not account for a consistent offset.
  41. The correct answer is B (329 g/L, inside the 320 to 360 g/L interval, so the warmed result is biologically plausible): MCHC is hemoglobin divided by hematocrit, so 92 g/L divided by 0.28 L/L gives about 329 g/L, which is physiologic. The original 541 g/L is impossible because clumped cells distorted the count and hematocrit. Dividing by the RBC count gives MCH in pg, not MCHC.
  42. The correct answer is A (7.5 percent, which exceeds the goal and requires investigation and corrective action): The rate is 3 divided by 40, or 0.075, which is 7.5 percent and is above the 5 percent goal. The goal covers all uncorrected errors, not only those causing harm, so the laboratory must investigate and correct. The 0.75 percent and 12.5 percent values come from misplacing the decimal or using the wrong denominator.
  43. The correct answer is D (The laboratory oversees training, QC, records and method verification within its quality system): Accreditation expects point-of-care testing to sit within the laboratory's quality system, so the laboratory manages operator training, QC, documentation and method checks. Vendors supply devices but do not take over the laboratory's accountability for results.
  44. The correct answer is C (Fix only the thin film in methanol, leaving the thick film unfixed so cells lyse): Methanol fixation preserves red cell shape on the thin film, while the thick film must stay unfixed so that water in the stain lyses the red cells and exposes the parasites. Fixing the thick film would stop that lysis and obscure the parasites. Heat fixing would distort cells and is not part of the standard method.
Case CHB2

A technologist stains eight wedge films from different inpatients in one run using methanolic Wright stain and fresh buffer. Every slide shows blue-grey red cells, dark blue nuclei with no visible chromatin detail and a deep blue background. The recorded buffer pH was 7.6 against a laboratory specification of 6.8. The patients' hematocrits ranged from 0.28 to 0.44 L/L, and all films were made by the same technologist.

  1. The correct answer is D (A buffer pH that was too high, because alkaline conditions weaken eosin and favour blue): A buffer pH of 7.6 is above the specification of 6.8, and alkaline conditions weaken eosin staining while favouring blue-staining components, which gives blue-grey red cells and dark nuclei. Hematocrit differences and spreader technique affect film thickness, not the colour of every slide, and fresh buffer does not carry extra dye.
  2. The correct answer is D (Remake the buffer to pH 6.8 and restain the films, because blue colour distorts morphology): The blue colour comes from a buffer that was too alkaline, so the specification must be restored before the slides are reliable. Remaking the buffer and restaining brings colours back to the expected balance. Reporting from blue slides, rinsing again or adding more stain does not correct the pH problem and could make the nuclei darker.
  3. The correct answer is C (Measure buffer pH and stain a control film each run, because drift is caught early): Measuring the buffer pH and staining a known control film in every run provides an objective check on colour balance before patient slides are read. Lamp changes, thicker films and shorter staining times do not detect or prevent chemical drift in the buffer, and some of them would only mask a colour problem.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reflex testing, which follows preapproved criteria so abnormal results are confirmed without delaying patient care): Reflex testing means a preapproved rule automatically triggers a second test when a first result meets stated criteria, here a film review for a low platelet count. It is approved by the medical director so the technologist is not acting on personal judgment. Add-on testing is a separate ordering request made after collection, and repeat testing reruns the same method.
  5. The correct answer is B (Dam the edge with absorbent, work inward, and place the used material in labelled waste): A spill is contained by placing absorbent around the outer edge first so that it cannot spread, then working inward and collecting the saturated material into labelled hazardous waste. Adding water enlarges the spill and sends formalin to the drain. Letting formalin evaporate exposes staff to vapour, and starting at the centre lets the edges run.
  6. The correct answer is D (Hematocrit 0.38 L/L, hemoglobin 124 g/L and platelet count 250 x10^9/L, after SI unit conversion): Canadian reports give hematocrit as a fraction in L/L, so 38 percent becomes 0.38, and hemoglobin in g/L, so 12.4 g/dL becomes 124 g/L by multiplying by 10. The platelet count is already in x10^9/L. Entering unconverted or mis-scaled numbers would produce values that look like extreme abnormal results.
  7. The correct answer is A (Have the infusion paused two minutes, then draw below the IV site, with a discard sample): If the only available arm has an infusion, the nurse should stop it for at least two minutes and the draw should be made below the catheter so infusion fluid is not drawn into the tube. A discard volume removes residual fluid, and the arm should be noted on the report. Drawing above the line or beside it risks dilution with the infusion.
  8. The correct answer is A (State the value again clearly, have it read back until it matches, and document it): The read-back exposed a transposition, so the technologist must restate the value and confirm a matching read-back before the call ends. Sending a report later leaves the wrong number in the nurse's hands for now. The verbal exchange is part of the documented critical communication.
  9. The correct answer is C (Reactive lymphocytes, activated T cells with abundant cytoplasm molded around neighbouring red cells): Large lymphocytes with abundant pale blue cytoplasm that molds around neighbouring red cells are reactive (variant) lymphocytes, mostly activated T cells responding to a viral infection such as infectious mononucleosis. Lymphoblasts have fine chromatin, nucleoli and a thin cytoplasmic rim. Plasma cells have eccentric nuclei with a perinuclear clear zone, and hairy cells show fine cytoplasmic projections.
  10. The correct answer is D (20.0 x10^9/L, because the raw count is multiplied by 100 and divided by 120): The correction is raw WBC x 100 / (100 + nRBC per 100 WBC), which gives 24.0 x 100 / 120 = 20.0 x10^9/L. Subtracting 20 percent gives 19.2, which wrongly treats the nRBCs as a percentage of the raw count. Adding to the raw count moves the result in the wrong direction.
  11. The correct answer is D (Transfer the aliquots to a working backup freezer, because further warming risks stability): Frozen control material stays stable only while it remains frozen, so the priority is moving it to a verified working freezer and then documenting the event. Waiting an hour risks thawing, and a recovered display would not prove the contents stayed frozen. Using the material for QC could produce invalid control results.
  12. The correct answer is B (3.0 percent, calculated as 36 divided by 1200 and multiplied by 100 for percent): Percent parasitemia is parasitized cells divided by total red cells counted, times 100, so 36 / 1200 x 100 equals 3.0 percent. Skipping the multiplication gives a fraction, scaling to 1000 cells ignores the real denominator, and inverting the ratio gives a meaningless number.
  13. The correct answer is D (131 g/L, because Canadian reports express hemoglobin as grams per litre of blood): One litre holds ten decilitres, so 13.1 g/dL equals 131 g/L. Changing only the label would understate hemoglobin tenfold, and multiplying by 100 would overstate it tenfold. Canadian laboratories report hemoglobin in g/L.
  14. The correct answer is D (Between 10 pm and 2 am, because this species shows nocturnal periodicity in peripheral blood): Wuchereria bancrofti microfilariae in South Asia and most endemic regions circulate in peripheral blood at night, so the best yield comes from blood drawn between about 10 pm and 2 am. Daytime collection suits Loa loa, which has a diurnal pattern, and neither fasting nor exercise changes the periodicity.
  15. The correct answer is B (A rejection rule, most often indicating random error such as a pipetting or mixing problem): A single result beyond three SD violates the 1-3s rule, which is a rejection rule and is most sensitive to random error such as a sampling or mixing problem. It does not depend on the other level agreeing. Warning rules are the 1-2s type, not a three SD excursion.
  16. The correct answer is C (Hold the result and inform the supervisor, because aged specimens give false negative RNA results): RNA is fragile, so a sample held for days at room temperature can degrade and give a false negative BCR-ABL1 result, which conflicts with the morphology of myelocytes and basophilia. The technologist should hold the result and tell the supervisor so the specimen can be rejected and recollected. A negative PCR on a compromised sample does not override a typical CBC and film.
  17. The correct answer is C (Spread them at once from the first pull, because later pulls dilute particles with blood): The first small pull is the richest in marrow particles, and clotting begins within seconds so slides must be made immediately. Later pulls are diluted by blood from the sinuses, which lowers cellularity and ruins the smear. Waiting lets the clot trap the particles.
  18. The correct answer is B (Refuse to initial it and report the discrepancy to the supervisor, because records must be truthful): Signing for work that was not witnessed, or back-dating a control, falsifies a quality record. The printout shows no run, so the discrepancy must go to the supervisor who can investigate and decide on patient result review. Silence leaves unverified patient results in the system.
  19. The correct answer is D (Turn off the hot plate and other ignition sources, because methanol vapour is flammable): The main hazard of methanol is fire, so ignition sources are removed first and the spill is then contained with absorbent. Bleach does not neutralize methanol and can add a reaction hazard. A bench fan has a motor that can ignite vapour, and bagging soaked towels in ordinary waste leaves a fire risk in the bin.
  20. The correct answer is D (Place a balance tube of matching size and water volume in the opposite empty bucket): Buckets must be loaded in opposing pairs of equal mass so the rotor spins without vibration. A water filled balance tube of matching size completes the empty pair. Slower speed still leaves an unbalanced load, and neighbouring buckets do not offset one another.
  21. The correct answer is A (Reject the tube, confirm identity with the patient, then recollect and document the event): A name mismatch with different health card numbers means identity cannot be confirmed, so the tube is rejected and not relabelled. While the patient is present, identity is confirmed directly, a new sample is collected and labelled at the bedside, and the event is documented. Relabelling from the requisition or testing with a note could report one patient's blood under another person's name.
  22. The correct answer is C (Brief breaks every 30 to 60 minutes, alternating microscope work with other tasks): Frequent short breaks and rotation among different tasks give muscles and eyes recovery time and reduce static posture, which lowers the risk of strain without sacrificing total output. Long uninterrupted sessions and waiting for pain to start allow cumulative strain to build.
  23. The correct answer is B (80 percent, below the limit, so the sample is rejected and recollection is requested): The fill percentage is the measured volume divided by the nominal volume: 3.6 / 4.5 equals 0.80, or 80 percent. That is below the required 90 percent, and an underfilled citrate tube gives falsely prolonged times, so it is rejected and recollected because recollection is easy. The figure of 20 percent is the missing fraction, and 125 percent comes from inverting the division.
  24. The correct answer is C (Filter the stain and keep slides wet during staining, because drying leaves dye crystals): Dye precipitate forms when stain dries on the slide or when old stain with aggregated dye is used. Filtering the stain and keeping slides covered with liquid during the staining step prevents crystals from settling on the film. Changing buffer pH, fixation time or warming the stain does not remove particles already suspended in the reagent.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Myeloblast, promyelocyte, myelocyte, metamyelocyte, band neutrophil, segmented neutrophil, with specific granules appearing at the myelocyte stage): Granulocytes mature from myeloblast to promyelocyte, myelocyte, metamyelocyte, band and finally segmented neutrophil, with the nucleus indenting and then segmenting as the cell ages. Swapping the promyelocyte and myelocyte, or the band and segmented stages, breaks that order. Specific secondary granules first appear at the myelocyte stage.
  2. The correct answer is D (About 30 to 45 degrees, because this spreads the drop into an even feathered film): A spreader held at roughly 30 to 45 degrees pulls the drop by capillary action into a film that thins smoothly to a feathered edge. Very shallow angles make a long thin film that may be too sparse, while steep angles make short thick films with poor cell separation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Run the STAT hemoglobin first, then the coagulation panel, then routine tests, by urgency): Clinical urgency and patient risk set the order, so the STAT hemoglobin from a bleeding patient goes first. The pre-operative panel is needed by morning and ranks next, while routine tests wait. Arrival order or speed of the test does not reflect risk to the patient.
  4. The correct answer is C (Report both as measured and flag the mismatch to the clinician, per protocol): Each assay measures something different, and the aPTT can be prolonged by factors other than heparin, so a disagreement is a finding to communicate rather than to hide. Both results are reported with their flags and the mismatch is brought to the clinician. A mismatch alone does not prove a collection error, and flags must never be edited to fit the other result.
  5. The correct answer is D (Hold the result, check labelling against the requisition, and recollect, since the indices also changed): A fall of 63 g/L with a shift in MCV from 84 to 101 fL means the red cells themselves look different, not just diluted, which points toward blood from another patient. The specimen is held while labelling and requisition details are checked and a fresh sample is drawn. Repeating the same tube would reproduce the same wrong blood, and releasing it could mislead treatment.
  6. The correct answer is A (Sickle cells do not stack into rouleaux, so sedimentation is falsely slowed despite active inflammation): ESR depends on red cells forming rouleaux, which sink faster as stacks. Sickle cells and spherocytes are rigid and do not stack well, so the ESR can read falsely low even when inflammation is present. A raised fibrinogen speeds, not slows, rouleaux formation, and anemia usually raises the ESR.
  7. The correct answer is A (Heparin contamination likely prolongs the thrombin time, so recollect from a clean venipuncture before reporting): A long thrombin time with a normal reptilase time points to heparin, because reptilase is not inhibited by it. The fibrinogen is normal, which argues against a dysfibrinogenemia, and the line flush explains the source. Fibrin degradation products would prolong both tests, and a bad reagent would have been seen in the controls.
  8. The correct answer is D (Double spin to platelet poor plasma and ship frozen aliquots on dry ice, because factors degrade): Factor assays need platelet poor plasma because residual platelets release phospholipid and factors that disturb results after freezing and thawing, so the plasma is spun twice and moved to plastic tubes. Aliquots frozen and shipped on dry ice keep factor activity. Glass activates contact factors, and plasma left on cells or shipped warm loses labile factors such as factor VIII.
  9. The correct answer is A (Oxalate and fluoride shrink and damage blood cells, so a separate EDTA tube is required): Oxalate draws water out of cells and fluoride damages membranes, so red cell and white cell size and morphology become unreliable in a grey top tube. Fluoride does the opposite of raising glucose, since it blocks glycolysis, which is why grey suits glucose. The CBC needs its own EDTA tube.
  10. The correct answer is A (29.5 x10^9/L, which is markedly above the interval, because the percent is applied to the total): The absolute count is the percent multiplied by the total white cell count: 82 percent of 36.0 gives 29.5 x10^9/L. This is far above the upper limit of 4.0 x10^9/L, so it is a marked lymphocytosis. Using 18 percent gives the nonlymphocyte fraction, and adding the percent to the total has no mathematical meaning.
  11. The correct answer is D (More hemoglobin A than hemoglobin S, typically near 60 percent A and 40 percent S): A carrier inherits one beta globin gene for hemoglobin A and one for hemoglobin S, and normal beta chains are made more efficiently, so hemoglobin A is about 60 percent and hemoglobin S about 40 percent. Hemoglobin S alone suggests sickle cell anemia, and equal S and C bands suggest hemoglobin SC disease.
  12. The correct answer is B (Escalate to the pathologist now and state the urgency, because suspected APL is an emergency): Suspected acute promyelocytic leukemia with low fibrinogen and platelets is a medical emergency in which early treatment lowers the death rate from hemorrhage, so the technologist should escalate the morphology finding at once. The laboratory does not diagnose or prescribe, but it does have a duty to communicate urgent findings. Delaying for flow cytometry or a repeat test puts the patient at risk.
  13. The correct answer is C (To find system weaknesses and prevent a similar event from harming a patient): Near misses are free lessons: recording them exposes weak points in the process before a patient is harmed. A good occurrence system is about learning and prevention, not blame, billing or automatic rewriting of procedures.
  14. The correct answer is D (Cells in CSF lyse and deteriorate quickly, so delayed counting underestimates the true cell numbers): Leukocytes and especially neutrophils in the low-protein CSF lyse within a short time, so a late count underestimates the cells and can hide meningitis. Glucose in CSF actually falls with time from cellular metabolism. Clot formation and bacterial counting are not the reasons for the one-hour rule.
  15. The correct answer is D (Investigate specimen identification and integrity before release, since the change exceeds biological expectation): A rise of 46 g/L in four hours without a documented cause exceeds the delta limit and is biologically unlikely, so the specimen may be mislabelled, contaminated or mismeasured. The technologist checks identity and integrity before releasing. Changing or cancelling earlier results alters the record without any evidence.
  16. The correct answer is C (5.0 percent, because 250 divided by 5,000 and multiplied by 100 gives 5.0): Parasitemia is infected cells divided by total red cells counted, times 100, so 250 / 5,000 x 100 equals 5.0 percent. The 0.5 percent value forgets to multiply by 100, and the 20 percent value inverts the fraction. Dividing by 10,000 uses a denominator that was not counted in this film.
  17. The correct answer is D (Disseminated intravascular coagulation, because factors and fibrinogen are consumed along with platelets): In DIC, widespread clotting consumes platelets, fibrinogen and clotting factors, so the PT and aPTT are prolonged, fibrinogen is low and D-dimer is high. TTP and HUS also show schistocytes and thrombocytopenia, but their PT, aPTT and fibrinogen are usually normal. Immune thrombocytopenia has no red cell fragmentation.
  18. The correct answer is A (Reject it and recollect, because five hours exceeds the four hour room temperature limit): Red cells swell and change shape as an EDTA specimen ages, which slows sedimentation and gives falsely low results once the stability limit is passed. Reporting with a comment still releases an unreliable number. Mixing or cooling cannot undo the change, so a fresh sample is needed.
  19. The correct answer is D (A sustained rise in uniform small lymphocytes, because a single clone expands steadily): CLL is a clonal expansion, so the lymphocytes are uniform in size and persist for months without an infection or other trigger. Reactive lymphocytosis settles and shows variable cells. Blasts suggest acute leukemia, and rouleaux points toward a paraprotein rather than CLL.
  20. The correct answer is A (Report no platelet count, comment that the sample is clotted, and request a recollection): Fibrin strands show that the sample clotted, and clotting traps platelets and other cells in an unpredictable way. Any count from this tube is unreliable, so no value should be reported and a new sample is needed. Estimating the loss or warming the tube cannot restore the trapped cells.
  21. The correct answer is A (Remove the unit from service and repeat retained samples, because short spins trap plasma): An under timed spin leaves plasma trapped between the red cells, so the packed cell column reads falsely high. The unit should be taken out of service until the timer is repaired and verified, and retained samples should be repeated. Adjusting dial settings or comments do not restore reliable results.
  22. The correct answer is D (Thermometer accuracy against a reference, so drift beyond tolerance can be corrected): Comparing a working thermometer with a calibrated reference verifies the working device's accuracy and shows whether it has drifted from tolerance. The 0.8 C difference is then judged against the laboratory's allowed deviation. Door seals, compressor cycles and alarm setpoints are checked by other tests.
  23. The correct answer is C (Clean the eyepiece with lens paper, because a smudge there rotates when it is turned): A smudge that moves when the eyepiece is rotated is on the eyepiece lens, because the eyepiece is the only part that turns with it. Dirt on the film would shift with the slide, and dirt on an objective would change when objectives are swapped. The condenser lens would stay put when the eyepiece is turned.
  24. The correct answer is A (Normal for age, because young children normally have more lymphocytes than neutrophils until school age): Newborns and young children have more lymphocytes than neutrophils, and the crossover to the adult pattern occurs around school age. Applying the adult interval of 20 to 40 percent to this 3-year-old therefore creates a false flag. Here the absolute lymphocyte count is about 5.0 x10^9/L, which lies within the usual childhood interval.
  25. The correct answer is C (Refer the film with the CBC results, because unclassifiable cells meet the policy trigger for review): The policy names cells that cannot be confidently classified as a referral trigger, and these cells match nothing in the atlas. Labelling them as lymphocytes would be guessing, and omitting them would hide an abnormal population. Referral is not limited to confirmed blasts, and waiting for an audit would delay review.
  26. The correct answer is C (Refer for flow cytometry, because maternal hemoglobin F cells falsely raise the film bleed estimate): A mother with raised hemoglobin F, such as hereditary persistence of fetal hemoglobin, has cells that resist acid elution and look fetal. The film then overestimates the bleed. Flow cytometry with anti-hemoglobin F or anti-D separates true fetal cells and gives a reliable count.
  27. The correct answer is A (Add the test to the same accession, and record who requested it and when): An add-on should be attached to the original accession so the order, tube and result stay linked, and the verbal request source and time must be recorded for traceability. A new patient record would create a duplicate, and a fresh sample is unnecessary when the stored tube is within the policy window. Paper-only reporting breaks the audit trail.
  28. The correct answer is A (Report no parasites seen and request repeat films, because one negative set cannot exclude infection): Parasite density varies over time and a single negative set does not rule out malaria, so serial films are standard. Restaining or rereading the same slides cannot reveal parasites that were not on them, and closing the request would risk a missed falciparum infection.
  29. The correct answer is C (Report promptly to her supervisor and occupational health, since early action prevents lasting injury): Early symptoms of a repetitive strain injury are reported promptly to the supervisor and occupational health so the task can be changed and the injury documented before it becomes disabling. Symptoms that ease on weekends point toward work as the cause rather than away from it. Informal swaps and waiting leave the hazard unaddressed and may weaken a later workers' compensation claim.
  30. The correct answer is D (Report both findings, commenting that early heterophile tests may be negative and a repeat helps): Heterophile antibodies often have not yet appeared early in the illness, so a negative result on day 3 does not exclude infectious mononucleosis. The laboratory should report both findings and note that a repeat in 7 to 10 days, or EBV serology, may help. Calling the result final, cancelling the film review or reporting lymphoblasts without morphologic support would mislead the clinician.
  31. The correct answer is B (The index is minus 2.5, which is unacceptable and requires a formal documented investigation): The index equals the laboratory result minus the group mean, divided by the group standard deviation: 205 minus 240 is minus 35, and minus 35 divided by 14 is minus 2.5. A magnitude beyond 2.0 is unacceptable, so the laboratory must investigate and record corrective action. The sign shows the result is lower than the peers, so reversing the subtraction gives the wrong direction.
  32. The correct answer is B (Suspect wrong blood in tube, hold the result, and request a properly identified recollection): A large drop in hemoglobin together with a changed MCV in one day fails the delta check and suggests the tube holds a different person's blood, so the result is held until a correctly identified recollection is analyzed. An acute bleed would not shrink the MCV from 91 to 76 fL so quickly. Reanalyzing the same tube only repeats the same wrong blood.
  33. The correct answer is A (A light blue sodium citrate tube, with the result corrected for the 1.1 dilution factor): Some patients have antibodies that cause platelets to clump only in EDTA. Recollecting into sodium citrate avoids the calcium chelation that exposes the binding site, and the count is multiplied by 1.1 for the citrate dilution. Heparin tubes can also clump platelets, and fluoride is not used for platelet counts.
  34. The correct answer is B (The value is normal for age, so the flag reflects the wrong interval): Neonatal red cell values are higher than adult values because of the high hemoglobin at birth, so age-specific intervals must be applied. A value of 185 g/L falls inside the stated interval of 145 to 220 g/L and is normal for the first week. Reporting it as critical or polycythemia would cause unnecessary alarm and testing.
  35. The correct answer is D (Check the pH of the new buffer and restain a film using the previous lot): A recent change in buffer or stain lot is the most likely cause of a new blue background, so the buffer pH is checked and a film is restained with the previous lot to separate reagent from instrument. Yesterday's passing QC does not cover today's new reagents. Pump service or longer staining would only follow if the reagent check fails.
  36. The correct answer is D (Verify the sample identification against the requisition and collection record before releasing anything): A fall in hemoglobin together with changes in MCV and RDW that cannot occur in two days suggests the sample may belong to another patient. Identification must be verified first, since releasing the result could lead to wrong treatment. Averaging or repeating analyzer runs cannot detect a mislabelled tube.
  37. The correct answer is A (Low fibrinogen and platelets with prolonged INR and aPTT, because clotting factors are consumed): In consumptive coagulopathy clotting factors, fibrinogen and platelets are used up, so INR and aPTT lengthen, fibrinogen and platelets fall and D-dimer rises. The panel shows exactly this pattern. A normal fibrinogen, normal clotting times or shortened aPTT do not match the report, and this combination in a patient with abnormal promyelocytes is a critical communication.
  38. The correct answer is C (Repeat the tests on the mechanical analyzer, because its endpoint does not rely on turbidity): A mechanical analyzer detects the clot by movement of a bead or ball, so turbidity from lipid does not hide the endpoint. Diluting plasma changes factor concentrations and gives a wrong clotting time, and a flagged result must not be released. Freezing does not clear lipid and would delay a STAT result.
  39. The correct answer is A (Activate the shield, then discard it in a sharps container, since recapping risks injury): Needlestick injuries happen most often during recapping and while carrying exposed needles, so the shield is engaged and the device goes straight into the container. A shield reduces risk but does not make the device safe for regular waste. Collecting needles on a tray adds handling and exposure time.
  40. The correct answer is B (The result moved from the prior value beyond the set limit, so identity needs checking): A delta check compares a result with the same patient's earlier result and flags a change larger than a laboratory-defined limit. The cause may be real clinical change, a transfusion, or a wrong-blood-in-tube error, so identity and collection must be reviewed before release. It does not test instrument controls or reference intervals.
  41. The correct answer is A (Decline and refer her to the care team, because she lacks authorization to receive information): Results may be released only to the patient or to people with legal authority and a care role. A caller who cannot be verified as either gets no information, even a vague comment such as low or normal. Birth date checks do not prove the caller's right to know, and worry from relatives does not create consent.
  42. The correct answer is B (19.2 x10^9/L, because the nucleated red cells were counted as white cells and are removed): The corrected count is 24.0 x 100 / 125, which is 19.2 x10^9/L. Multiplying by 1.25 moves the value the wrong way, and subtracting 25 percent of the count uses the wrong base. Nucleated red cells are counted by the analyzer as white cells, so the correction is needed whenever they are present.
  43. The correct answer is A (Not valid, because a platelet count below about 100 x10^9/L prolongs the closure time by itself): The PFA-100 closure time is prolonged by thrombocytopenia, usually when the count is below about 100 x10^9/L, and by a low hematocrit. A prolonged result at a platelet count of 62 x10^9/L therefore cannot be used to diagnose a platelet function defect. The instrument does not correct for the count.
  44. The correct answer is B (Activate the alarm and alert others, then decide whether to use an extinguisher): The fire response sequence calls for alerting others and raising the alarm so help is coming, before anyone decides whether the fire is small enough to fight. Fighting a fire alone without an alarm delays help. Finishing a run wastes time, and opening windows feeds the flame with oxygen.
  45. The correct answer is A (Hold the result and recollect from the other arm, because infusion fluid dilutes the sample): A large unexplained fall in hemoglobin with a matching hematocrit, in a sample drawn near a running infusion, is typical of dilution by intravenous fluid. The correct step is to hold the result and obtain a fresh specimen from the arm without the infusion. Reporting it could trigger an unnecessary transfusion, and repeating the same tube would only reproduce the diluted value.
  46. The correct answer is A (A battlement track through the monolayer, because it samples all cell types fairly): A battlement track in the monolayer, where cells lie side by side without overlap, samples neutrophils, lymphocytes and monocytes in their true proportions. The feathered edge concentrates large cells and the thick head distorts shape and staining, so counting there skews the result.
  47. The correct answer is B (Reduce the citrate for the hematocrit, because extra citrate dilutes plasma and prolongs clotting times): A fixed volume of citrate is designed for blood with a normal hematocrit. When the hematocrit is above about 0.55 L/L there is less plasma, so the same citrate over-dilutes it and prolongs clotting times unless the citrate volume is reduced. Simply underfilling raises the citrate proportion even further, and EDTA is not suitable for coagulation testing because it removes the calcium needed for the reaction.
  48. The correct answer is B (Erythroid hyperplasia as a compensatory response to hemolysis, which lowers the ratio below the reference range): A reticulocyte count of 15 percent with low hemoglobin and raised indirect bilirubin points to hemolysis, and the marrow responds with erythroid hyperplasia, which pushes the M:E ratio below the 2:1 to 4:1 reference range. A hypercellular marrow argues against aplasia, and a ratio of 1:2 reflects an excess of erythroid, not myeloid, cells.
  49. The correct answer is A (Many cells with multiple small blue inclusions that give a golf ball appearance after incubation): Excess beta chains form hemoglobin H tetramers that precipitate after incubation with brilliant cresyl blue, giving cells many small blue dots like a golf ball. Heinz bodies are fewer and larger, siderocyte granules need an iron stain, and Howell-Jolly bodies are single nuclear remnants seen in asplenia.
  50. The correct answer is A (Recheck detector voltages using unstained cells, because negative controls belong in the negative region): An unstained control has no antibody, so any positivity comes from the instrument settings or autofluorescence rather than the reagents. Voltages and compensation must be reset until the control falls in the negative region. Only then can patient markers be interpreted.
  51. The correct answer is D (It compares results with peer laboratory values, so bias hidden from controls can be found): Internal controls monitor stability against their own target ranges, so a bias shared by controls and patients, or hidden by a poorly set target, can go unseen. Proficiency testing compares results with peers using the same or reference methods and exposes that bias. It does not replace internal controls, it does not prove individual patient results, and it covers many analytes, not only films.
  52. The correct answer is B (Decline, because leftover specimens need patient consent or ethics approval before use in research): Using leftover clinical specimens in research needs the patient's informed consent or approval from a research ethics board. The patient agreed only to the clinical test. Removing the label or sharing a small portion does not change that requirement.
  53. The correct answer is A (Microfilariae, a significant parasite finding that needs prompt physician notification and species identification): A sheathed, 250 micrometre worm-like larva with nuclei along its body is a microfilaria, and in a patient from West Africa it points to filarial infection that needs species identification. Trypanosomes are only 15 to 30 micrometres long with a flagellum, Borrelia are thin spirals, and fibrin strands have no nuclei.
  54. The correct answer is D (Cold agglutinins, so warm the tube and repeat, because clumped red cells count as single cells): A very high MCV and MCHC with a low RBC count is the classic pattern of cold agglutinins, where clumped red cells are counted as single large cells. Warming the specimen to 37 degrees C disperses the clumps and allows an accurate repeat. Lipemia, underfilling and clots produce different patterns and do not give an MCV of 150 fL.
  55. The correct answer is C (Release the batch, because every plotted point stays near the mean with no multirule violation): Every point on the chart lies within about one standard deviation of the mean and alternates on both sides, so no Westgard rule is violated on any day. Scatter on both sides of the mean is normal random variation, not drift. Holding the batch or recalibrating without a rule violation would delay patient care for no analytical reason.
  56. The correct answer is D (120 x10^9/L, flagged high because it is above the 100 x10^9/L upper limit): Absolute reticulocytes equal the reticulocyte percentage times the RBC count: 0.040 x 3.00 x10^12/L gives 0.12 x10^12/L, or 120 x10^9/L. This is above the upper limit of 100 x10^9/L and reflects the marrow's response to ongoing hemolysis in sickle cell disease. Units must be converted carefully, since a slip of a power of ten gives 12 or 1200.
  57. The correct answer is B (Reject the tube and request recollection, because two identifiers must be labelled at the bedside): A tube must be labelled with two patient identifiers at the time of collection so that the sample can be tied to one person. Relabelling in the laboratory or relying on a phone call removes that safeguard, and stability of EDTA blood has no bearing on identity. Because recollection is easy, rejection is the safe course.
  58. The correct answer is D (Beta thalassemia trait, because raised A2 with microcytosis and normal ferritin shows reduced beta synthesis): Beta chain shortage leaves relatively more delta chain, so hemoglobin A2 rises above the reference interval. Together with a low MCV, a high red cell count, a Mentzer index of about 11 and normal ferritin, this fits beta thalassemia trait. Iron deficiency lowers A2 and lowers ferritin, and hemoglobin E would appear as a much larger peak.
  59. The correct answer is C (Thaw it at 37 C for 3 to 5 minutes, because rapid thawing preserves labile factors): Rapid thawing at 37 C limits cryoprecipitate formation and loss of labile factors, and gentle mixing returns the plasma to a uniform state. Slow thawing in the cold allows precipitation, while very hot water can denature proteins.
  60. The correct answer is B (Directly after the blood culture, before green and lavender tubes, to limit additive carryover): CLSI places the blood culture at the start to protect sterility, then the light blue citrate tube, then serum, heparin, EDTA and grey. Citrate sits ahead of the green and lavender tubes so heparin and EDTA cannot contaminate the clotting sample. Drawing it before the culture breaks sterility, and placing it after EDTA risks calcium chelation.
  61. The correct answer is C (About 0.52 L/L is expected, so the MCHC near 458 g/L shows falsely high hemoglobin): Hemoglobin 174 g/L divided by 10 and multiplied by 3 gives about 52 percent, or 0.52 L/L, well above the reported 0.38 L/L. The calculated MCHC (174 divided by 0.38) is about 458 g/L, far above the 320 to 360 g/L interval and physiologically implausible. Milky plasma points to lipemia, which adds turbidity and falsely raises hemoglobin, so the hemoglobin should be remeasured after plasma replacement.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Fix the core in buffered formalin promptly, since delayed fixation causes autolysis of marrow cells): A trephine core is fixed first so the cells stop autolysing and the architecture is held in place. Decalcification comes only after fixation, because strong acid on unfixed tissue destroys nuclear detail and antigens. Saline or freezing does not stabilize the marrow for routine paraffin sections.
  2. The correct answer is B (Make a film from freshly drawn blood, because storage artifact will not affect a new sample): Echinocytes with evenly spaced short projections are a common storage artifact in aged EDTA blood, and a fresh sample will usually show normal discocytes. Heat does not reverse the change, and reporting the artifact as a disease pattern would mislead the clinician. Acanthocytes have irregular spicules, so that term does not fit.
  3. The correct answer is D (Bleach with hydrochloric acid, because mixing them would release toxic chlorine gas in the room): Mixing hypochlorite bleach with an acid releases chlorine gas, which is toxic to breathe, so these two must be stored apart. Neutral buffer concentrate does not react dangerously with methanol, acid or bleach. Segregation is based on the hazard class of each chemical, and acids and bleach are a standard incompatible pair.
  4. The correct answer is C (Review the method comparison study and add a cumulative comment noting the analyzer change): A small shift seen across many patients at the time of an instrument change points to method bias, not disease. The method comparison study documents the expected difference, and a comment helps clinicians read the trend. Re-calibrating to match old values or hiding history would damage both accuracy and continuity.
  5. The correct answer is D (Serum vitamin B12 and folate, because the film points to impaired DNA synthesis in developing cells): Large oval red cells together with a neutrophil showing six lobes are the hallmark of megaloblastic change, so vitamin B12 and folate levels are the logical next tests. Iron studies suit microcytic cells, electrophoresis suits target cells, and a DAT suits spherocytes.
  6. The correct answer is A (17.9 x10^9/L, because the analyzer count is multiplied by 100 and divided by 140): The corrected WBC equals the uncorrected count multiplied by 100 and divided by 100 plus the nucleated red cells per 100 white cells. Here 25.0 x 100 divided by 140 gives 17.9 x10^9/L. Subtracting a straight 40 percent or multiplying the count up gives wrong values.
  7. The correct answer is C (Collect now and repeat every 12 to 24 hours, because parasite numbers fluctuate): Parasitemia rises and falls, but parasites can be found at any time, so waiting for a fever wastes time in a potentially urgent illness. Serial films over 24 to 48 hours increase detection because a single negative film does not exclude malaria.
  8. The correct answer is B (Postpone the dispensing until splash goggles are obtained, and tell the supervisor about the shortage): Required eye protection cannot be replaced by ordinary glasses, because splashes can arrive from the sides and above. Holding the container at a distance does not prevent a splash, and moving the exposure to a colleague only moves the risk. Delaying a non-urgent transfer and reporting the shortage keeps staff safe and lets the equipment be replaced.
  9. The correct answer is D (A raised erythropoietin level, because chronic hypoxia stimulates the kidney to release more hormone): In chronic hypoxia the kidney senses low oxygen delivery and releases more erythropoietin, so red cell mass rises as an appropriate response. A clonal disorder such as polycythemia vera would instead show a low erythropoietin, often with raised white cells or platelets. The normal white cell and platelet counts here fit a secondary cause.
  10. The correct answer is B (The lipemic tube, cleared by high-speed spinning or run on a mechanical analyzer): Lipemia produces turbidity that scatters light, so optical clot detection can fail or give unreliable times. Ultracentrifugation to remove the lipid layer, or a mechanical clot-detection method, avoids the problem. Diluting an icteric sample or rewarming a hemolyzed one does not address light scattering.
  11. The correct answer is D (Negative against the age-adjusted cut-off of 0.78, because 78 multiplied by 0.01 equals 0.78): The age-adjusted cut-off is 78 multiplied by 0.01, which equals 0.78 mg/L FEU, and 0.70 is below that value. The conventional cut-off no longer applies once the validated age-adjusted threshold is used. The result is not invalid because of age and is not borderline, since it sits under the applicable threshold.
  12. The correct answer is A (The blasts lack myeloid enzyme activity, so a lymphoid origin is favoured pending flow cytometry): Strongly positive neutrophils prove the reagents worked, so the negative blasts are truly negative. Myeloblasts normally show myeloperoxidase, so a negative result favours a lymphoid origin, although monoblasts can also be negative. Flow cytometry confirms the lineage.
  13. The correct answer is C (Notify the physician at once, because intracellular ring forms suggest Plasmodium and need urgent confirmation): Delicate rings with one chromatin dot inside red cells are the classic early trophozoite of Plasmodium, and malaria is treated as a critical result that needs immediate telephone notification. Species identification and parasitemia follow but must not delay the call. Platelets and stain deposit lie on the surface or outside cells rather than within them.
  14. The correct answer is D (Reactive thrombocytosis driven by iron deficiency, because low iron stores stimulate megakaryocytes and platelet production): Iron deficiency commonly causes a reactive thrombocytosis, and the hypochromic microcytic film points to low iron stores as the driver. Essential thrombocythemia is a diagnosis of exclusion that needs clonal marrow features, which are not described here. Microcytes are not mistaken for platelets, and an anemia does not make the spleen release stored platelets.
  15. The correct answer is B (Draw from the arm opposite any infusion, and fill the citrate tube to the marked line): A new venipuncture from the arm opposite any infusion removes the line as a source of heparin, and a correctly filled citrate tube avoids a second error. Reusing the line risks the same contamination because flushing does not reliably clear the lumen. Heparin tubes are unsuitable for coagulation testing, and EDTA does not neutralize heparin and would itself invalidate the test.
  16. The correct answer is A (Use the correction function so the original value stays in the audit trail): Quality systems require that the original released value stays traceable, so errors are corrected through a function that records the old value, the new value, who changed it and why. Deleting or silently overwriting destroys the audit trail. A phone call alone leaves the permanent record wrong.
  17. The correct answer is B (Ask the nurse to read back the patient identifiers and the result for confirmation): A read-back of the patient identifiers and the value lets the caller confirm that the receiver heard the right patient and number. Repeating the value oneself does not verify what the receiver understood. Recording the receiver's identity is needed but does not confirm accuracy.
  18. The correct answer is C (Report with a comment that warfarin lowers both proteins, so deficiency cannot be confirmed during therapy): Warfarin lowers the vitamin K dependent proteins C and S, so a deficiency cannot be confirmed while the patient is anticoagulated at an INR of 2.4. The assays can be run on citrated plasma, so rejecting the request is not required. A comment prevents a false diagnosis of inherited deficiency.
  19. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge a document control gap and complete the overdue review with proper sign-off): A missed review is a document control nonconformity even when the content is correct, so it should be admitted, recorded and corrected with a signed review. The SOP remains valid for use while this is done, and altering the system date would falsify the record.
  20. The correct answer is D (Exhausted and evaporated buffer, because depleted buffer raises resistance and slows migration of every band): When all lanes including controls fail to migrate and the current is low, the problem lies in the system rather than one sample. Old or evaporated buffer raises resistance and reduces migration, so the buffer should be replaced with fresh solution. A dilute sample would affect patient lanes only, and a short stain would not leave bands stuck at the origin.
  21. The correct answer is C (Hold the slides, have the collecting physician identify and relabel them, and document the correction): A marrow aspirate cannot be repeated casually, so an identification problem is resolved through the person who witnessed the collection. Relabelling by inference from a tube is unsafe and reporting partly labelled slides risks a mix-up. Discarding irreplaceable material without trying to verify it is not justified.
  22. The correct answer is D (3.6 percent, found by multiplying 6.0 by 0.27 and dividing by 0.45): The corrected reticulocyte percentage is the observed percentage times the patient hematocrit over the normal hematocrit: 6.0 x 0.27 / 0.45 = 3.6 percent. Inverting the ratio gives 10.0 percent, which overstates the marrow response. A raw percentage is not adjusted for the lower number of red cells in anemia, and skipping the division leaves the value too low.
  23. The correct answer is C (An oversized drop or too steep an angle, because both leave a short thick film): A large drop or a steep spreader angle forces a short film whose body is too thick to separate cells, so they overlap and stain dense. Staining buffer pH changes colour balance, not cell crowding, and delayed fixation or grease cause distortion or holes rather than a uniformly thick film.
  24. The correct answer is D (2.1 x10^9/L, from 17 percent multiplied by 12.5, which exceeds the reference interval): Absolute count equals the percentage multiplied by the total white cell count: 17 percent of 12.5 x10^9/L is 2.1 x10^9/L, well above the 0.5 x10^9/L limit. Reporting the percentage as a count, shifting the decimal, or dividing instead of multiplying all give wrong values.
  25. The correct answer is C (Report to the supervisor promptly, because impaired work with specimens already risks patient safety): A colleague who appears impaired while handling patient specimens is a patient safety risk, so the supervisor must be told promptly. Waiting for an error, a private talk alone or covering the work all delay protection. Patient safety takes priority over friendship or reputation.
  26. The correct answer is C (Increased basophils with a low LAP score, because clonal neutrophils make little alkaline phosphatase): In CML the neoplastic granulocytes make little alkaline phosphatase, so the LAP score is low, and basophils are typically increased. Toxic granulation, Dohle bodies and high LAP scores are features of reactive neutrophilia. Bands and immature forms occur in both conditions, so they do not separate them.
  27. The correct answer is A (Test 20 or more healthy adults on the new lot and use the geometric mean): The mean normal prothrombin time is lot and instrument specific, so it is determined from fresh plasma of at least 20 healthy individuals and expressed as the geometric mean. Patients on warfarin would bias the value, and the old lot's mean cannot be reused because the new reagent clots plasma at a different speed.
  28. The correct answer is D (Millimetres of clear plasma above the settled red cell column after one hour of standing): The Westergren method reads how far the red cell column has fallen in 60 minutes, expressed as the height of clear plasma above it in mm/h. It is not a packed cell volume and does not depend on centrifugation. A vertical tube standing undisturbed is essential, because tilting changes the fall rate.
  29. The correct answer is D (Hold the result and recollect in citrate, because satellitism falsely lowers the EDTA count): Platelet satellitism is an EDTA-dependent artifact in which platelets adhere to neutrophils, so the instrument misses them and the count is falsely low. Recollecting in sodium citrate gives a true count before anything is released. Rerunning the same tube repeats the artifact, and a critical call on a spurious value would mislead the clinician.
  30. The correct answer is B (Prolonged EDTA contact causes in vitro artifacts, so films should be made from fresh blood): EDTA gradually swells cells, degrades neutrophils, vacuolates cytoplasm and crenates red cells when a tube stands for hours. Films should be made promptly, within a few hours of collection. A wrong stain pH changes colour rather than producing vacuoles and swelling.
  31. The correct answer is D (Exclude her result, because predefined health criteria rule out donors currently treated for anemia): Reference individuals are selected by health criteria set before collection, not by whether their values look normal. A donor treated for anemia does not meet those criteria even when her current value is acceptable. Judging inclusion by the value itself would narrow the interval artificially.
  32. The correct answer is A (Positive for hemoglobin S, with a comment that trait cannot be distinguished from disease): Turbidity that hides the ruled lines, with acceptable controls, is a positive solubility result because deoxygenated hemoglobin S is insoluble in the reagent. The test detects hemoglobin S only and cannot separate trait from disease or measure its percentage. Electrophoresis or HPLC is needed for that distinction, so the report must say so rather than name a diagnosis.
Case CHB3

A 34-year-old woman attends a community clinic. Her CBC shows hemoglobin 100 g/L, RBC 5.60 x10^12/L, hematocrit 0.32 L/L, WBC 6.5 x10^9/L and platelets 290 x10^9/L. The analyzer reports RDW-CV 13.8 percent. The film shows small pale red cells with only mild size variation. The technologist calculates the indices by hand to check the printout. Iron studies are not yet available.

  1. The correct answer is B (57 fL, because hematocrit multiplied by 1000 and divided by the red cell count gives volume): MCV equals hematocrit x 1000 / RBC, so 0.32 x 1000 / 5.60 = 57.1 fL, which is microcytic. The 18 value is a near miss that is actually the MCH calculation, and 313 comes from the MCHC calculation. Failing to scale the percent hematocrit gives 5.7, which is not a possible red cell volume.
  2. The correct answer is B (17.9 pg, because hemoglobin divided by the red cell count gives the hemoglobin mass per cell): MCH equals hemoglobin (g/L) divided by RBC (x10^12/L), so 100 / 5.60 = 17.9 pg, which is low and fits hypochromia. Dividing hemoglobin by hematocrit gives the MCHC, not the MCH, and 57.1 is the MCV. Using the percent hematocrit gives an impossible fraction of a picogram.
  3. The correct answer is D (Index near 10 favours thalassemia trait, so hemoglobin pattern testing precedes iron therapy): The index is 57.1 / 5.60, about 10.2, and a value below 13 favours thalassemia trait, which typically shows a high red cell count with a low MCV and a normal RDW. Confirmation by iron studies and hemoglobin pattern testing avoids unnecessary iron therapy. Megaloblastic anemia is macrocytic, and the index is not a normal range.
  4. The correct answer is C (Into the flammable solvent waste container, because methanol is flammable, then close the cap tightly): Used methanol-based stain is still flammable, so it goes into the labelled flammable solvent waste container, which is kept capped between additions. Diluting it in a buffer container or mixing it with bleach waste creates a mixed hazard and a possible reaction. Pouring solvent down a sink breaks waste rules and sends flammable vapour into the drain.
  5. The correct answer is B (Issue a corrected report, phone the physician and document the error and call): A transcription error that has reached the physician must be corrected formally, and the physician must be told by phone, because 89 g/L may signal worse bleeding than 98 g/L. The error and the call are documented. Quiet correction, doing nothing or waiting for a trend all leave the clinician uninformed.
  6. The correct answer is D (Verify the number by calling the office's listed line, then fax with a cover sheet): A mismatched fax number is a warning that results could reach the wrong person. Calling the office on its known listed line confirms the destination quickly, and a cover sheet limits harm if a misdirection still occurs. Sending to two numbers doubles the exposure, and urgency does not remove the duty to protect the information.
  7. The correct answer is C (Larger bluish-grey red cells, because residual RNA takes up the basic dye in young cells): Polychromatophilic cells are young red cells still holding ribosomal RNA, so they look larger and bluish-grey on a Romanowsky stain and match the reticulocyte rise. Pencil cells indicate iron deficiency, rouleaux reflect plasma protein changes, and dense spherocytes reflect membrane loss, none of which show marrow response.
  8. The correct answer is C (Use deionized water and acid-washed glassware, because trace iron in tap water would stain blue): The Prussian blue reaction detects any ferric iron, including contamination from tap water or metal containers. Deionized water and acid-washed glass prevent false positive blue. Methanol fixation is part of the method, and drying does not leach iron from the cells.
  9. The correct answer is D (Stop reporting patient results and notify the supervisor, because the system is not in control): A control outside its limit means patient results from that run cannot be trusted until the cause is found. Reporting an urgent sample anyway puts a patient at risk, and the supervisor decides on troubleshooting, service and alternate testing. Yesterday's controls say nothing about today's run.
  10. The correct answer is B (A weakening laser or detector, so the instrument needs service before patient runs resume): A slow, steady downward drift over many days is typical of a laser or detector losing output with age, which needs service. A single contaminated vial or a pipetting difference would give a sudden or erratic change, not a smooth trend. Sheath fluid is colourless and does not absorb light.
  11. The correct answer is A (Time of call, name and role of the person notified, results given and read-back confirmation): An auditable record shows when the call was made, who received it and in what role, exactly what was communicated, and that the recipient read the result back correctly. Initials and dates alone cannot prove the right person received the right information. The report timestamp records release, not the conversation.
  12. The correct answer is D (3.6 percent, calculated as 6.0 multiplied by 0.27 and divided by 0.45): Corrected reticulocyte percentage equals the observed percentage multiplied by the patient's hematocrit and divided by the normal hematocrit: 6.0 x 0.27 / 0.45 equals 3.6 percent. The correction is needed because anemia leaves fewer red cells in the denominator, so the raw percentage overstates production. Inverting the ratio or skipping the division gives values of 10.0 or 1.6 percent.
  13. The correct answer is C (The gel forms a barrier between serum and cells, and full clotting prevents later fibrin): The thixotropic gel has a density between serum and cells, so it moves and sets as a barrier during the spin. Incomplete clotting lets fibrin form after centrifugation, which can clog analyzer probes. The gel neither starts clotting nor removes lipids, and serum tubes are not anticoagulated.
  14. The correct answer is B (A trend from gradual deterioration, so investigate reagent, optics or pipette before limits are exceeded): A steady daily rise in one direction is a trend, which usually points to progressive problems such as reagent degradation or a failing optical component. It is worth investigating before a rejection rule is broken. Waiting for a limit violation or repeating until a good value appears hides the developing fault.
  15. The correct answer is C (Raised red cells, white cells and platelets, because one mutated marrow clone drives all three lines): In polycythemia vera a mutated marrow clone drives red cells, and frequently white cells and platelets as well, while erythropoietin is suppressed. Secondary polycythemia shows raised red cells alone with a high erythropoietin from hypoxia. Hemoconcentration from fluid loss raises hemoglobin and hematocrit but does not raise white cells or platelets.
  16. The correct answer is D (Close the field diaphragm, then focus the condenser until the diaphragm edges look sharp): Kohler illumination is set by closing the field diaphragm and moving the condenser until the diaphragm edge is in sharp focus, then centring it. Raising lamp voltage only changes brightness and does not focus the light. Changing the objective or lowering the condenser does not correct the focus of the field diaphragm image.
  17. The correct answer is C (Review flag thresholds with the vendor and test abnormal specimens, because missed blasts expose weakness): Flags are the safety net that sends abnormal specimens to manual review, so a missed blast population is a serious gap even when the normal differential agrees well. Flag settings should be examined and more abnormal specimens tested before approval. Precision on normal cells does not cover abnormal cells, and repeating a specimen until a flag appears does not fix sensitivity.
  18. The correct answer is B (Recollect in sodium citrate, repeat the count, and correct the result for citrate dilution): EDTA induced platelet clumping falsely lowers automated counts because clumps are not counted as single platelets. Recollecting into sodium citrate prevents this clumping, and the result is corrected for the small dilution from the citrate. The analyzer does not exclude clumps reliably, extra EDTA worsens the effect, and estimating from clumps is not valid.
  19.
