The correct answer is B (Suspect in vitro hemolysis, withhold the RBC, hematocrit and indices, and request a new sample): An MCHC of 473 g/L is far beyond what real red cells can contain, so the red cell count and hematocrit are invalid. In vitro hemolysis lowers the RBC count and hematocrit while free hemoglobin is still measured, which makes the indices meaningless. The sample should be recollected rather than diluted or reported with a comment.
  20. The correct answer is D (Blast cells, because immature cells express less CD45 than mature lymphocytes and have little granularity): Blasts typically show dim CD45 with low to intermediate side scatter, which places them below the lymphocyte cluster. Neutrophils have high side scatter, and monocytes show brighter CD45 with higher scatter. Nucleated red cells are CD45 negative.
  21. The correct answer is A (Have the collector add the missing identifier, sign and date it, and document the correction): Marrow aspirates cannot be repeated easily, so the specimen is rescued by an attested correction rather than rejected. The person who collected the material must add the missing identifier, sign, date and document the change so the record is traceable. Adding the identifier from paperwork alone, or proceeding without documentation, leaves identity unproven.
  22. The correct answer is B (Stop patient testing, allow warm up and recheck, and call for service if still out): Clotting reactions depend on a temperature near 37 C, and a block below the acceptance range can lengthen clotting times. Testing stops until the block is rechecked after warm up and corrected or serviced. Changing the set point or relying on controls alone does not fix the reading.
  23. The correct answer is A (0.54 x10^9/L, analyzer higher, because 6.30 minus 5.76 equals 0.54 for the neutrophils): Absolute neutrophils equal percent times WBC divided by 100, giving 6.30 x10^9/L by analyzer and 5.76 x10^9/L by manual count. The analyzer is therefore higher by 0.54 x10^9/L. Applying 6 percent to a WBC of 9.0 gives the same figure, while using 10.0 or no WBC at all gives wrong values.
  24. The correct answer is D (The ring finger, off centre of the pad, because its skin is smoother and thinner): Capillary sampling is done on the middle or ring finger, slightly to the side of the pad, where the tissue is deep enough to protect the bone. Calloused skin yields poor flow and the index finger and thumb are avoided for these reasons. The very centre of the tip lies close to the bone and is more painful.
  25. The correct answer is C (It protects calcium dependent clotting tests from EDTA contamination that would falsely prolong the aPTT): EDTA removes the calcium that the aPTT depends on, so it must follow the citrate tube in the draw. The order on the requisition does not set the draw sequence, because the CLSI order does. Draw order is about additive carryover, not about which test matters most.
  26. The correct answer is A (Factor VII, because tissue factor complexes with it in the PT, while the aPTT bypasses it): The PT reagent supplies tissue factor and calcium, so it depends on factor VII together with the common pathway, while the aPTT uses a contact activator and does not need factor VII. Factors VIII, XI and XII belong to the intrinsic pathway and are measured by the aPTT instead. A prolonged PT with a normal aPTT therefore points to factor VII.
  27. The correct answer is A (The left arm, because lymphedema risk on the surgical side makes venipuncture there unsafe): After removal of axillary nodes, lymph drainage from the arm on that side is impaired, so a puncture can lead to swelling and infection. The opposite arm should be used for the lifetime of the patient unless the physician advises otherwise. The hand on the same side is still part of the affected limb.
  28. The correct answer is B (A comment that transfused donor cells may mask deficiency, so repeat testing after months is advised): Transfused donor cells with normal enzyme activity dominate the sample for weeks to months and can hide a true deficiency. The laboratory should report the result with that caution and advise retesting once the donor cells have been replaced. A normal result cannot be treated as final, and donor cells survive far longer than one day.
  29. The correct answer is C (Show Tuesday's value together with its clumping comment so the trend is read correctly): The comment explains why Tuesday's count is falsely low, so keeping it attached stops a reader from seeing a sudden drop and recovery as real. Deleting a released result removes part of the legal record. Inventing an average would replace a measured result with a number that was never tested.
  30. The correct answer is D (Level 1, because its CV of 6.0 percent is double the 3.0 percent of Level 3): The CV for Level 1 is 0.18 divided by 3.0, which is 6.0 percent, and for Level 3 it is 0.54 divided by 18.0, which is 3.0 percent. A larger standard deviation does not mean greater imprecision when the mean is also larger. Comparing CV puts both levels on the same relative scale.
  31. The correct answer is B (The on-call pathologist or hematologist at once by direct contact, because suspected blasts are critical): Circulating blasts can signal acute leukemia and are treated as a critical morphology finding that needs prompt physician-level review and direct communication. Waiting for routine rounds or an end-of-shift email delays diagnosis. Infection control has no role in a possible hematologic malignancy.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Gloves first, then coat, then eye protection, finishing with hand hygiene after removing the last item): The most contaminated item, the gloves, comes off first so that it does not touch the coat or the face as those are removed. The coat follows, then eye protection handled by its clean straps, and hand hygiene finishes the sequence. Removing gloves last leaves contaminated hands touching clean items and the face during removal.
  2. The correct answer is A (EDTA-induced platelet clumping, because clumps are not counted individually and citrate does not provoke it): A count of 31 on EDTA against 262 on citrate, with a normal hemoglobin and no bleeding, points to EDTA-induced clumping, since clumped platelets are not counted as single cells. Citrate does not provoke the clumping, so the recount is reliable once corrected for dilution. A true low count would appear in both tubes, and drift or hemolysis would not split two tubes drawn together.
  3. The correct answer is C (Macro-ovalocytes and a hypersegmented neutrophil, pointing to impaired DNA synthesis from B12 or folate deficiency): Macro-ovalocytes with a hypersegmented neutrophil, a raised MCV and mild cytopenias reflect megaloblastic maturation from impaired DNA synthesis. Iron deficiency causes microcytosis, immune hemolysis gives spherocytes and reticulocytosis, and target cells fit liver disease or hemoglobinopathy. The numb feet add support for vitamin B12 deficiency.
  4. The correct answer is A (Record the gap as an occurrence, review QC data, and perform maintenance now): Missing records cannot be recreated from memory or copied, because that falsifies a quality record. The honest response is to document the gap, use QC data as evidence of performance, and complete the overdue maintenance. Assessors can review records from any period within the retention time.
  5. The correct answer is A (The triangular fragments with pointed ends, because they are true fragmented red cells): Schistocytes are true red cell fragments, such as helmet cells and small triangular pieces with sharp points and no central pallor. Crenated cells (echinocytes) are an artifact or storage change with evenly spaced spicules, and target cells reflect a membrane surface-to-volume change, so neither is counted. Counting the wrong cells would overestimate fragmentation.
  6. The correct answer is B (Acanthocytes, because irregular spicules of unequal length reflect altered membrane lipids in liver disease): Acanthocytes have a few irregular spicules of unequal length and appear in liver disease and abetalipoproteinemia. Echinocytes have many evenly spaced short projections and are commonly an artifact of storage or slow drying, which is why the aged specimen differs. Teardrop cells and stomatocytes have different shapes altogether.
  7. The correct answer is A (A chipped or dirty edge on the spreader slide, because it drags through the film): Lines that run along the direction of spreading in the same place on every slide point to a defect on the spreader edge, which drags through the film as it moves. Fibrin strands and stain deposits would not repeat at fixed positions, and a slow push produces ridges across the film instead.
  8. The correct answer is B (Pause and calm the child, because repeated failed punctures raise distress and reduce sample quality): After two unsuccessful attempts the patient is distressed, and further rushed punctures cause more crying, vasoconstriction and poorer flow. Pausing, calming the child and involving the nurse or a colleague is the safest approach. Squeezing distorts the counts and an inadequate partial sample should not be sent as if it were complete.
  9. The correct answer is C (Recollect into a tube with the citrate volume reduced for the high hematocrit, then retest): A high hematocrit leaves less plasma in the tube, so the fixed amount of citrate is too concentrated and falsely prolongs the clotting times. The citrate volume must be reduced for such patients, and a recollection with an adjusted tube is the correct step. Adding citrate or diluting the plasma makes the error worse or changes the sample.
  10. The correct answer is A (Relocate away from the vent, close the shield doors, let it warm, then repeat the check): Air currents, an unlevel surface and incomplete warm up cause unstable readings, so the environment is corrected first. Drifting values cannot be fixed by calibrating or by adding an offset, because the cause is unstable conditions. After relocating and warming, the weight check is repeated.
  11. The correct answer is D (Test the rule on sample cases, record medical director approval, and train staff): A new automatic comment is validated on known cases, approved by the medical director and communicated to staff before it reaches patients. A manufacturer's testing does not cover this laboratory's flags and workflow. Trialling it on live reports exposes patients to errors, and physician signatures on individual reports are not the approval step.
  12. The correct answer is B (TRAP positive, because the lymphocyte acid phosphatase resists inhibition by the added tartrate): Most acid phosphatase is inhibited by tartrate, but the enzyme in hairy cells is tartrate resistant. Persisting strong positivity on the tartrate slide is reported as TRAP positive and supports hairy cell leukemia. Alkaline phosphatase and esterase are separate reactions.
  13. The correct answer is A (MCV 89 fL and MCHC 336 g/L, both of which fall within the normal intervals): MCV equals the hematocrit in L/L times 1000 divided by the RBC, so 0.50 x 1000 / 5.6 gives about 89 fL. MCHC equals the hemoglobin divided by the hematocrit, so 168 / 0.50 gives 336 g/L. Both are normal, so the indices are consistent and the raised values reflect hemoconcentration from dehydration rather than an abnormal red cell.
  14. The correct answer is C (Request a recollection and tell the nurse why, because hemolysis can interfere with the reading): Hemolysis can interfere with a chromogenic reading, so a low value from a grossly hemolyzed sample may not reflect true heparin activity. The laboratory asks for a fresh sample and tells the nurse the reason so no dose is changed on a doubtful value. Releasing it or advising a dose change acts on an unreliable result, and dilution does not remove the pigment.
  15. The correct answer is C (8.0 x10^9/L, because 12.0 multiplied by 100 and divided by 150 removes nucleated red cells): The corrected WBC equals the reported WBC times 100 divided by 100 plus the number of nucleated red cells per 100 WBC, so 12.0 x 100 / 150 = 8.0 x10^9/L. Subtracting half of the count gives 6.0, which wrongly assumes the nucleated cells are 50 percent of all counted cells. Adding them increases the count instead of correcting it.
  16. The correct answer is D (Macrocytic indices with hypersegmented neutrophils observed, clinical correlation advised, because the comment describes findings only): A technologist may describe what the indices and film show and advise correlation with the clinical picture. Naming a probable disease, prescribing a treatment or attributing a cause moves into diagnosis, which belongs to the physician. Macrocytosis has several possible causes, so a cause cannot be assigned from the film.
  17. The correct answer is A (Avoid the fistula arm and consult the nurse for another site, protecting the access): A dialysis fistula is a lifeline and is accessed only by trained dialysis staff, so it is not used for routine blood collection. Needle damage, infection or clotting of the access could cost the patient their dialysis route. A hematoma area should also be avoided, so the nurse should help find a suitable alternate site.
  18. The correct answer is D (Relative polycythemia from fluid loss, because red cell mass is normal and rehydration lowers values): Vomiting and diarrhea shrink the plasma volume, so a normal red cell mass sits in less plasma and hemoglobin and hematocrit rise together with normal indices. Normal white cell and platelet counts and clinical dehydration argue against a clonal marrow disorder. Repeating the count after fluid replacement should bring the values back toward the reference interval.
  19. The correct answer is B (Rest him seated for fifteen minutes and offer water, because exertion and dehydration concentrate blood): Hard exercise and fluid restriction shrink plasma volume, so the hemoglobin concentration rises without any increase in red cell mass. Resting and rehydrating before a repeat draw removes these preanalytical effects. Prolonged stasis would raise, not lower, the value, and the additive concentration in a correctly filled tube does not explain a 32 g/L rise.
  20. The correct answer is C (Withhold the results, request recollection of both patients, and file an occurrence report today): A mislabelled pair of specimens cannot be released, so the results are withheld and both patients are recollected. An occurrence report records the event so it can be learned from and corrected. Swapping labels, releasing with a comment or hiding the error all risk attributing results to the wrong patient.
  21. The correct answer is A (Excess citrate relative to blood can falsely prolong the PT, so the tube is rejected): The tube is set up for nine parts blood to one part citrate, so an underfilled tube holds too much citrate for the volume of blood. The extra citrate binds calcium added by the reagent and prolongs the clotting time, so the specimen is rejected. A short draw is a volume problem, not a hematocrit problem.
  22. The correct answer is B (Consumptive coagulopathy, because platelets and fibrinogen are low while PT, aPTT and D-dimer are high): Low platelets and fibrinogen with prolonged PT and aPTT and a markedly raised D-dimer show consumption of platelets and clotting factors with active fibrin formation and lysis, which fits sepsis-related DIC. Vitamin K deficiency would not lower platelets or fibrinogen. Primary fibrinolysis does not lower platelets, and immune thrombocytopenia leaves the coagulation tests normal.
  23. The correct answer is A (2.3, from the ratio of 2.0 raised to the power of the ISI): INR is the patient to mean normal PT ratio raised to the power of the ISI, so (24.0 / 12.0) to the power 1.2 equals about 2.3. Using the bare ratio ignores reagent sensitivity, and multiplying by or inverting the ISI applies it incorrectly.
  24. The correct answer is A (3.1 percent, because the 112 cells in B represent 1008 cells in the large square): Square B is one ninth of square A, so 112 red cells in B stand for 112 x 9 equals 1008 red cells in A. The reticulocyte percentage is 31 / 1008 x 100, which is 3.1 percent. Dividing by 112 alone ignores the area difference, and multiplying the reticulocytes by 9 scales the wrong number.
  25. The correct answer is C (2.3, calculated as the time ratio of 2.0 raised to the power of 1.2): The prothrombin time ratio is 24.0 divided by 12.0, which is 2.0. The INR is that ratio raised to the power of the ISI, so 2.0 to the 1.2 equals about 2.3. Multiplying by the ISI gives 2.4 and dividing the exponent gives 1.8, and neither follows the defined formula.
  26. The correct answer is D (Stay calm, acknowledge his worry, give the expected wait and say who will report the results): A calm tone, acknowledgment of the concern and an honest time estimate reduce conflict without exceeding the technologist's role. Technical detail overwhelms a frightened parent, and a false promise damages trust when it fails. Sending him away ignores his legitimate worry.
  27. The correct answer is A (Ask about aspirin use and repeat off the drug, because COX inhibition blocks that response): An isolated loss of the arachidonic acid response with normal ADP, collagen and ristocetin is the typical pattern of cyclooxygenase inhibition by aspirin or a similar drug. A medication history and a repeat off the drug are the sensible next steps. Glanzmann disease would abolish the ADP and collagen responses as well.
  28. The correct answer is A (A 1-3s violation, so reject the run and review day 9 patient results): A single control beyond 3 SD from the mean is a 1-3s violation, which rejects the run. Because patients were tested that day before the review, their results need to be reviewed and repeated where needed. Repeating the control until it passes or recalibrating without review hides the problem.
  29. The correct answer is D (Secure the tube, notify the supervisor, and hold it untested until custody is clarified): Without a transfer form the history of the tube before arrival cannot be verified, so it cannot support a legal case. The sample should be kept secure and untested while the supervisor clarifies custody with the police. Testing or discarding would damage evidence that may not be replaceable, and writing the officer's name does not create a record of earlier handlers.
  30. The correct answer is B (Moderate hemophilia A, because only factor VIII is low, at 3 percent, with normal factor IX): Factor VIII of 3 percent falls in the 1 to 5 percent band, which is moderate, and the normal factor IX, VWF antigen, platelet count and PT point to hemophilia A rather than hemophilia B or von Willebrand disease. A value below 1 percent would be severe, and mild disease requires activity above 5 percent.
  31. The correct answer is D (Describe the backlog and agree a realistic time, so priorities stay clear): Stating the current workload and agreeing on a realistic time keeps priorities visible and supports the colleague without hiding delays. Silent acceptance or flat refusal damages communication, and pushing the audit back a week ignores a shared patient care goal.
  32. The correct answer is D (Report counts, describe blast-like cells, and urgently alert the pathologist without stating a diagnosis): The technologist should release the measured counts, describe the abnormal cells and bring the film to the pathologist urgently, because the findings are serious. Naming the diagnosis is outside scope. Holding the whole CBC delays care, and leaving out the morphology removes the very finding that makes the case urgent.
  33. The correct answer is A (Hemoglobin S, because deoxygenated hemoglobin S is insoluble in the buffer and scatters light): In the solubility test, a strong reducing agent deoxygenates the hemoglobin and the high-molarity phosphate buffer makes deoxy hemoglobin S insoluble, so the solution turns turbid. Hemoglobin A and most other variants stay soluble and the solution remains clear enough to read print. The test does not separate trait from disease.
  34. The correct answer is C (Report the eosinophilia with a comment, because the film confirms a true reactive increase): The count is confirmed by the film, with normal morphology and no blasts, so it is a true reactive eosinophilia and should be reported with an explanatory comment. Drug reactions are a common cause. Rejecting a verified sample or implying leukemia without morphologic evidence is inappropriate, and withholding the differential delays care.
  35. The correct answer is D (Resistant red cells stay intact and are counted as white cells, because their size overlaps): The white cell channel relies on complete lysis of red cells, and a minority of specimens, including some neonatal samples, contain red cells that resist the reagent. Those intact cells generate pulses within the white cell range and falsely raise the count. Fetal hemoglobin, bilirubin and platelet size do not explain a lyse-resistance flag.
  36. The correct answer is B (Free serum IgG remained after washing and neutralized the antihuman globulin reagent): Serum proteins left after inadequate washing, mainly free IgG, bind and neutralize the antihuman globulin, which gives a false negative. Washing the cells at least three to four times with saline removes them. The spherocytes and reticulocytosis suggest immune hemolysis, so a properly washed repeat is needed rather than accepting the negative.
  37. The correct answer is D (Withhold the report and promptly inform the clinician of the low value, who arranges follow-up): Without written authorization the partner is not a valid recipient, so the report is held. However, a hemoglobin of 68 g/L is clinically important, so the clinician must still be told so the patient can be reached. Doing nothing leaves a possibly unwell patient unreviewed, and a partial disclosure is still a disclosure.
  38. The correct answer is A (Report 1 mm/h with a comment that high hematocrit can falsely lower the ESR, limiting aggregation): A high hematocrit leaves little plasma for red cell aggregation, so the ESR can read falsely low even in active inflammation. The result is reported as measured with an explanatory comment so the clinician does not rule out disease. Heparin is not an accepted anticoagulant for this method, and a low ESR is not a critical result.
  39. The correct answer is C (File the incident report with the supervisor now, because review can prevent a repeat injury): Incidents are reported even when the injury is minor, so that the committee can find the cause and prevent a repeat with a worse outcome. Reporting duties do not depend on lost time, a diagnosis or a colleague's opinion. A prompt written report also protects the worker if symptoms appear later.
  40. The correct answer is A (Mostly small mature lymphocytes with no blasts, nucleated red cells or immature granulocytes): In aplastic anemia the film is sparse, and the few leukocytes seen are mostly mature lymphocytes because granulocytes are depleted, with no blasts or immature forms. Blasts point to acute leukemia, nucleated red cells and teardrops suggest marrow infiltration, and hypersegmented neutrophils suggest B12 or folate deficiency.
  41. The correct answer is B (The INR is calibrated on warfarin-treated plasma, so it poorly reflects direct oral anticoagulant effect): The INR and its ISI were designed to standardize results for vitamin K antagonists, and direct oral anticoagulants inhibit factor Xa or thrombin in ways the INR does not scale to. Apixaban does not remove calcium or act on platelets, and kidney function affects drug clearance rather than test validity.
  42. The correct answer is C (Treat the check as failed because 4600 is below 4680, then troubleshoot before running patients): Ten percent of 5200 is 520, so the acceptable range is 4680 to 5720. A reading of 4600 is outside it, so the daily check fails and the instrument is troubleshot before patient testing. Forcing the peak with voltage outside a documented procedure, or quoting a wider limit, is not permitted.
  43. The correct answer is B (Only current approved versions should be available where work is done, because staff follow them): Document control ensures that staff work from the current approved version, so obsolete copies must be removed from the point of use. Old revisions are retained in an archive for reference rather than being simply destroyed, and approval comes from authorized reviewers, not the bench user.
  44. The correct answer is D (Primaquine, because oxidant drugs overwhelm the reduced glutathione that protects enzyme-deficient red cells): G6PD feeds the pentose phosphate pathway that makes NADPH and keeps glutathione reduced, so oxidant drugs such as primaquine, sulfonamides and nitrofurantoin, or fava beans, cause hemolysis in deficient cells. Iron, vitamin B12 and folic acid are standard treatments for deficiencies and are not oxidant triggers.
  45. The correct answer is C (40 cells per uL, found by dividing 36 by the 0.9 uL counted across nine squares): Nine large squares at 0.1 mm depth hold 0.9 uL, so 36 cells divided by 0.9 uL gives 40 cells per uL. Ignoring the depth underestimates the count tenfold, and multiplying by the number of squares moves the answer in the wrong direction.
  46. The correct answer is B (Run the test, because capped citrate blood stays acceptable for PT up to twenty four hours): A capped citrate tube kept at room temperature is acceptable for PT and INR for up to twenty four hours, and nine hours is well within that window. The shorter four hour limit applies to aPTT on uncentrifuged whole blood, and heparin monitoring samples must be centrifuged within one hour. Warming the tube is not part of the PT procedure.
  47. The correct answer is C (Examine a film for clumps and recollect in sodium citrate if clumping is confirmed before reporting): A platelet clump flag with a count far below the patient's baseline and no bleeding suggests EDTA-induced clumping, which falsely lowers the automated count. The film confirms clumps, and a citrate recollection with a correction for dilution gives a true value. Reporting or phoning the artefact is unsafe, and neither multiplying nor diluting recovers an accurate count.
  48. The correct answer is B (Report with a transfusion comment and advise repeat testing in about three months): Donor red cells contain HbA, which raises the HbA fraction and can mimic trait or hide the patient's own variant pattern. The result is still reportable when it carries a comment that transfusion occurred, since HbA will fall as donor cells clear. Repeat testing after roughly three months gives a cleaner picture of the patient's own cells.
  49. The correct answer is D (Deficient glycoprotein IIb-IIIa, because platelets cannot bind fibrinogen to form aggregates despite normal adhesion): Failure to aggregate with ADP, collagen and epinephrine with normal ristocetin clumping is the pattern of Glanzmann thrombasthenia, caused by deficient GPIIb-IIIa. Platelets still adhere through GPIb, so ristocetin works, but they cannot bridge through fibrinogen. A GPIb defect or low VWF would impair the ristocetin response instead.
  50. The correct answer is D (Red cells did not pack fully, so extra plasma remained and the reading is falsely high): Packing is time and speed dependent. A 2 minute spin leaves more plasma trapped between cells, so the red cell column is taller and the hematocrit reads high. A short spin does not heat the tube enough to cause hemolysis, and clay or buffy coat positions do not explain a time-dependent error.
  51. The correct answer is A (Clotting of the aspirate, because it has no anticoagulant and fibrin traps cells and spicules): Marrow aspirate has no anticoagulant when it is first drawn, so it clots within seconds and fibrin traps the cells and spicules. That is why smears are spread immediately from the first pull, which is also the richest in marrow. Dilution with peripheral blood rises with later pulls, not the first, and cooling or hemolysis are not the main concern.
  52. The correct answer is B (135, above the reference interval, from adding each grade multiplied by its cell count): The score is the sum of each grade multiplied by the number of cells at that grade: 0 plus 30 plus 40 plus 45 plus 20 equals 135. Adding only cell numbers, grade values or the cell total ignores the weighting. A score of 135 is high, which supports a reactive process rather than CML.
  53. The correct answer is B (20 uL of stock suspension plus 980 uL of saline, for 1000 uL total): A 1 in 50 dilution means 1 part stock in 50 parts total, so 1000 uL / 50 = 20 uL of stock and 980 uL of saline. Adding 50 uL gives 1 in 20. Adding 1000 uL of saline on top of 20 uL of stock makes 1020 uL, which is a 1 in 51 dilution.
  54. The correct answer is C (Run the new lot beside the old lot, then calculate the laboratory's mean and SD): Running the new lot in parallel with the old lot keeps the old lot as the active quality check while data accumulate for the new lot. After enough results the laboratory calculates its own mean and standard deviation. Starting on insert ranges alone or using a single day's value gives limits too wide or too unreliable.
  55. The correct answer is C (Use a qualified interpreter or language line, then confirm understanding before the draw): A nod does not prove understanding, and consent must be informed, so a qualified interpreter or telephone language service should be used and understanding checked before the draw. A friend may not be neutral and has not been authorized to hear the patient's health information, which also matters under provincial privacy law. Speaking louder does not solve a language barrier.
  56. The correct answer is D (9.6 x10^9/L, because the reported count is multiplied by 100 and divided by 100 plus 25): Nucleated red cells are counted as white cells by the analyzer, so the true count is WBC x 100 / (100 + nRBC per 100 WBC), which is 12.0 x 100 / 125 = 9.6 x10^9/L. Adding 25 percent gives 15.0 and subtracting 25 percent gives 9.0, which are both wrong operations. Analyzers that flag nRBCs usually still need this correction unless they subtract them automatically.
  57. The correct answer is B (Hold the films, correct the buffer and restain them, because acidic stain colours mislead morphology): A buffer pH of 6.0 is too acidic, which gives pink-orange red cells, weak nuclear colour and strongly red eosinophil granules. Because those colours can hide or alter morphology, the patient films should not be read until the buffer is corrected and the films are restained. Lighting adjustments cannot repair a chemical staining error, and releasing the results risks misleading morphology reports.
  58. The correct answer is D (Use forceps to put it in a sharps container, then report the unattended sharp): An unattended sharp is a hazard to everyone, so it is removed immediately using a mechanical aid such as forceps and reported so the source can be reviewed. Gloves do not stop punctures, and paper towels hide the hazard. Waiting to find an owner delays removal and leaves staff exposed.
  59. The correct answer is A (A myeloproliferative neoplasm such as CML, because clonal myeloid disease often expands basophils): Persistent basophilia is a hallmark of chronic myeloid leukemia and other myeloproliferative neoplasms, which arise from a clonal myeloid stem cell. Sepsis and corticosteroids lower rather than raise basophil counts, and acute allergic reactions cause transient basopenia from degranulation.
  60. The correct answer is A (Normocellular, because expected cellularity at age 70 is about 30 percent of the marrow space): The age-based rule gives an expected cellularity of about 30 percent at age 70, so a core with 30 percent cells is normocellular for that age. Using a single adult value of 50 percent would wrongly call the marrow hypocellular. Aplasia would need a marked fall well below the age-expected value.
  61. The correct answer is D (Advise a venipuncture recollection before treatment, since flush heparin may have contaminated the sample): An aPTT of 168 s against 31 s the day before, from a line kept open with heparinized flush, suggests contamination rather than true anticoagulation in a patient with no heparin orders. Giving protamine on a doubtful value could harm the patient, and rerunning the same tube repeats the error. Recollection by venipuncture gives a result that can guide treatment.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Leave the record closed, because access requires a work duty and every access is audited): Access to health information is allowed only for a legitimate work purpose, and the laboratory information system logs every viewing. Family ties and curiosity are not work reasons, and viewing alone counts as unauthorized access. Having a coworker look for her simply passes the improper request along.
  2. The correct answer is B (The Class II biological safety cabinet, because HEPA filtered airflow contains infectious aerosols): A Class II biological safety cabinet draws room air in, passes the exhaust through HEPA filters and protects the worker, the specimen and the environment from infectious aerosols. A chemical fume hood does not filter air through HEPA media and does not protect the specimen. Masks and open benches give no aerosol containment.
  3. The correct answer is A (Hard squeezing added tissue fluid and activated platelets, which then clumped and lowered the count): Forceful milking dilutes the sample with tissue fluid and damages and activates platelets, which stick together and are then not counted individually. The result is a falsely low platelet count that does not reflect the patient. A gentle, steady pressure with a deep enough puncture avoids this problem.
  4. The correct answer is D (Recollect in sodium citrate, count platelets, and multiply by 1.1 to correct for dilution): Clumps in EDTA blood lower the platelet count without any real drop in platelets. A citrate sample avoids this effect, and because citrate dilutes the blood by one part in ten the result is multiplied by 1.1. More EDTA would not break up the clumps, and reporting the false count could cause needless treatment.
  5. The correct answer is B (RDW, because it measures the spread of cell volumes and rises with anisocytosis): RDW is calculated from the red cell volume histogram and describes anisocytosis. MCV is only the average volume, so a population of mixed sizes can still have a normal MCV. MCH and MCHC describe hemoglobin content and concentration, not size spread.
  6. The correct answer is C (The five whys technique, which traces a problem back through successive causes to a process fault): Repeatedly asking why moves the analysis from the visible error to the underlying process cause, which is the five whys technique. Levey-Jennings charts monitor control results, Pareto charts rank categories by frequency, and Gantt charts schedule tasks. None of them trace causes in this way.
  7. The correct answer is A (Report the result, because the film confirms the count and inflammation can raise platelets): The film confirms that the platelets are truly increased, with no giant forms or clumps to suggest an artifact. A rise from 305 to 710 in five days fits a reactive process driven by active inflammatory bowel disease. Repeating on citrate adds nothing when the film agrees, and naming a clonal disorder from one count is outside a technologist's role and unsupported.
  8. The correct answer is B (It narrows sampling error in each percentage, which matters most for rare cell types): Percentages from a count of 100 carry wide sampling error, and the error is largest for cell types that make up only a few percent, such as eosinophils and basophils. Doubling the count tightens that range. It does not replace a proper counting track and it does not calibrate the analyzer.
  9. The correct answer is C (9.8 x10^9/L, a marked basophilia that supports a myeloproliferative neoplasm such as CML): Absolute basophils equal 6 percent of 164 x10^9/L, which is 9.8 x10^9/L and far above the usual limit near 0.2 x10^9/L. Persistent marked basophilia with immature granulocytes and thrombocytosis is characteristic of chronic myeloid leukemia. Decimal slips or quoting the percentage as a count lead to incorrect interpretations.
  10. The correct answer is B (Cell volume, because larger cells displace more conductive diluent and so generate a taller pulse): In the Coulter principle a cell is a poor conductor, so each cell crossing the aperture briefly raises resistance, and pulse height is proportional to cell volume. Hemoglobin, nuclear density and surface charge do not set the amplitude. This is why the analyzer can derive MCV and size histograms from pulse heights alone.
  11. The correct answer is C (Explain that the ordering practitioner interprets results with her history, and encourage her to ask): Interpreting a result and giving a diagnosis belong to the practitioner who knows the history. The technologist can still be courteous, explain that the practitioner will discuss it, and support the patient. Guessing at causes or recommending supplements steps outside scope, while a flat refusal to engage is unprofessional.
  12. The correct answer is B (Report the corrected WBC, because nRBC nuclei are falsely counted as white cells): Nuclei of nucleated red cells survive lysis and are counted by the analyzer as leukocytes, so the raw count overstates the true white cell number. The report should give the corrected WBC and state the nRBC finding per 100 WBC. Releasing the raw value could mislead the clinician about infection or leukemia risk.
Case CHB4

A 38-year-old man had an emergency splenectomy after a motor vehicle collision. On day 12 his CBC shows hemoglobin 142 g/L, WBC 12.8 x10^9/L, platelets 820 x10^9/L and MCV 90 fL. The film shows many platelets and several red cells with a single round, dark purple inclusion. He has no fever and no bleeding.

  1. The correct answer is A (Reactive thrombocytosis, because the spleen no longer stores platelets and surgical stress raises production): After splenectomy the organ that stores platelets is gone, and surgical stress stimulates production, so a reactive rise is expected within days to weeks. Essential thrombocythemia is not the most reasonable explanation when a clear surgical cause exists. Fragments from trauma would be visible on the film, and no immune disease is described.
  2. The correct answer is A (Howell-Jolly body, a DNA remnant that the spleen normally removes but that stays after splenectomy): The single round dark purple dots are Howell-Jolly bodies, which are DNA fragments left in red cells after the nucleus is expelled. A working spleen pits them out, so they persist when the spleen is absent. Pappenheimer bodies are irregular iron granules, stippling is many fine dots, and Cabot rings are thin loops.
  3. The correct answer is C (Report Howell-Jolly bodies and the platelet count as found, since both are expected after splenectomy): Howell-Jolly bodies and a raised platelet count are expected after splenectomy, so both are reported as found. Repeating the film, leaving out a visible inclusion or diluting a count that lies within the measuring range would only delay or degrade a valid result. The chart history explains both findings.
  4. The correct answer is B (About 8 um, because 200 um divided by 25 cells gives 8 um per cell): The field diameter of 0.20 mm equals 200 um, and dividing by 25 cells gives 8 um per cell, which matches a normal red cell. Using the radius halves the answer, a decimal slip gives 80 um, and equating the whole field to one cell ignores the 25 cells counted.
  5. The correct answer is A (Primary polycythemia, because erythropoietin is low while white cells and platelets are also raised): Hemoglobin 205 g/L, hematocrit 0.61 L/L and RBC 6.9 x10^12/L show a true rise in red cell mass, while the low erythropoietin points to a primary marrow process rather than a hypoxic response. The raised white cell and platelet counts fit a marrow disorder affecting several cell lines. Dehydration would not lift the white cells and platelets, and a clot would not give such a consistent pattern across all three red cell values.
  6. The correct answer is D (Mild dilutional fall in hemoglobin and mild neutrophilia fit expected changes of pregnancy): Pregnancy expands plasma volume more than red cell mass, so hemoglobin, hematocrit and RBC fall together while the MCV stays normal, which argues against iron deficiency. A mild rise in the white count with neutrophilia is also normal in later pregnancy. A diluted sample would lower the white cells and platelets as well, and leukemia would need further findings.
  7. The correct answer is A (Run the unclog cycle, then repeat a control to confirm performance before reporting further patient results): Flagged obstruction and poor duplicate agreement point to a partly blocked aperture, which the cleaning cycle should clear. A control must be repeated afterward because earlier controls do not cover current performance. Recalibration will not fix a physical blockage, and manual dilution simply hides the problem.
  8. The correct answer is C (10.8 mL, because six multiplied by 1.8 mL equals 10.8 mL as required): Six multiplied by 1.8 mL gives 10.8 mL, which is the volume the stated policy requires. Using five times, two times or one times the dead space would leave residual flush solution or heparin that can distort clotting results.
  9. The correct answer is C (Prepare and review a stained blood film, then report the differential after manual examination): A blast flag is a morphology alert that an analyzer cannot confirm, so a stained film must be examined by a technologist before the differential is released. Repeating the run only hides the alert without resolving it. Omitting the differential would withhold information the laboratory is able to provide.
  10. The correct answer is A (Request a fresh specimen, because cell viability and antigen expression decline in aged samples): Flow cytometry needs viable cells, and antigen expression and viability fall as a sample ages. Results from a 52 hour old sample with heavy debris are unreliable, so a fresh specimen should be requested. Freezing whole blood damages cells, and extra antibody does not restore lost antigens.
  11. The correct answer is D (A 55 percent fall that meets the criterion, because 154 of 280 platelets were lost): The fall is 280 minus 126, which is 154, and 154 divided by 280 equals 0.55, or 55 percent, which is more than 50 percent. Dividing the day 6 count by the baseline gives the fraction remaining, not the fall. Day 6 lies inside the usual five to ten day window, so timing does not exclude HIT.
  12. The correct answer is C (Random error such as pipetting or mixing variation, so check sample handling and repeat controls): The two levels fell on opposite sides of the mean by more than four SD in total, which is the R-4s pattern for random error. A return to the mean on the next run supports an isolated handling problem rather than a change in the system. Reagent replacement or recalibration targets systematic causes that this pattern does not show.
  13. The correct answer is C (Post-exposure prophylaxis works best when started within hours, so the risk assessment is done urgently): Post-exposure prophylaxis is most effective when started within a few hours of exposure, so the assessment of the source and the exposure type is urgent. Antibodies take weeks to appear, so a quick test cannot confirm or exclude infection. Wound closing and claim forms matter far less than starting protection in time.
  14. The correct answer is A (Report blast-like cells on the differential and refer the film to the pathologist for review): Large cells with fine chromatin and a high nuclear to cytoplasm ratio are blast-like, so they are listed on the differential and the film goes for senior review as policy directs. A flag alone is not enough to release a differential, and naming a leukemia type is beyond the entry-level role. Repeating the CBC does not answer what the film already shows.
  15. The correct answer is B (A falsely low red cell count and hematocrit with normal hemoglobin, giving a spuriously high MCHC): Lysed cells are not counted, so the red cell count and the calculated hematocrit fall. The analyzer lyses all cells to measure hemoglobin, so total hemoglobin stays accurate, and dividing it by a low hematocrit inflates the MCHC. Free hemoglobin is still measured, so hemoglobin is not falsely low.
  16. The correct answer is C (Control material monitors performance only, while calibrators carry assigned values traceable to reference methods): Calibrators are produced with values assigned by comparison with reference methods, so the traceability chain reaches the result. Control material is meant to monitor stability against ranges, and its targets can be method-specific rather than traceable. Using it to set instrument factors would transfer any error in the control target into every patient result.
  17. The correct answer is A (Quantify the bleed by Kleihauer-Betke or flow cytometry, because dose depends on bleed volume): The rosette test is qualitative and shows only that fetal D-positive cells are present. A Kleihauer-Betke film or flow cytometry then estimates the volume of the bleed, which sets the number of immune globulin vials. The rosette result alone cannot give a volume.
  18. The correct answer is A (Place a balancing assembly with equal fluid opposite, because unbalanced rotors vibrate and fail): A cytocentrifuge rotor spins at high speed, so opposing positions must carry equal mass. A balancing assembly with the same fluid volume keeps the load even. Empty holders or reduced speed do not remove the imbalance risk.
  19. The correct answer is D (Reject the clotted tube and recollect, because clots trap platelets and invalidate the count): Fibrin strands mean the sample partly clotted, which traps platelets and gives a falsely low count that cannot be fixed in the laboratory. Straining or adding anticoagulant does not restore the original platelets. A fresh, well-mixed EDTA sample is needed, and the earlier value of 245 x10^9/L fits a spurious result.
  20. The correct answer is C (Biohazardous infectious material that can cause disease in people, so specimens need secure packaging): The pictogram of three crescents in a circle is the Canadian WHMIS symbol for biohazardous infectious material, which covers organisms and toxins that cause disease. Blood specimens fall in this class and need leakproof triple packaging under the Transportation of Dangerous Goods rules. The skull, exclamation mark and health hazard symbols describe chemical hazards.
  21. The correct answer is D (Decline to report it and contact the on-call pathologist, because the task is outside her competency): A registered technologist practises only within tasks she is competent and authorized to perform, and escalates the rest. A physician's request does not transfer her accountability for a report that leaves her laboratory. A second signature from someone equally untrained adds no real competence.
  22. The correct answer is B (67 cells per microlitre, because the mean of 60 is divided by 0.9 microlitre counted): Each large square holds 0.1 microlitre, so nine squares hold 0.9 microlitre. The mean of the two sides is 60 cells, and 60 divided by 0.9 gives 66.7, or 67 cells per microlitre. Adding the two sides double counts, and using one square volume overstates the result tenfold.
  23. The correct answer is A (Dilute the sample and repeat, because the huge white count clouds the hemoglobin measurement): A very high white cell count scatters light in the hemoglobin measurement, which falsely raises hemoglobin and MCHC. The patient looks pale and the film shows few red cells, so the hematocrit and film are the better clues. Diluting the sample and repeating removes the turbidity, and the indices are then recalculated. Editing results to fit each other or recollecting a sample with the same white count does not fix the interference.
  24. The correct answer is A (Call the backup hematologist from the escalation chain, because critical morphology needs prompt physician review): A critical finding needs a physician reviewer without a long delay, and the written escalation chain exists for exactly this case. Two unanswered calls do not discharge the duty, and a nurse message or a second technologist cannot replace physician review. The chain should be followed and every attempt documented.
  25. The correct answer is B (3:1, because 300 myeloid cells divided by 100 erythroid cells, excluding the other cell types): The M:E ratio compares myeloid cells with erythroid precursors only, so 300 divided by 100 gives 3:1, which sits inside the usual reference range of about 2:1 to 4:1. Including lymphocytes, plasma cells or the total count changes the numerator wrongly, and writing erythroid first inverts the ratio.
  26. The correct answer is B (Reject the tube and request recollection, because the identifiers do not match): The label and the requisition disagree on a core identifier, so the tube cannot be linked to this patient with confidence. Correcting or annotating a mismatched label is not acceptable because the error may mean the blood came from someone else. Recollection with proper identification protects the patient from a wrong result.
  27. The correct answer is D (Marrow suppression by alcohol and folate deficiency, together with platelet pooling in the enlarged spleen): Alcohol suppresses marrow production and is often accompanied by folate deficiency, which fits the macrocytosis. Portal hypertension enlarges the spleen, which pools a larger share of circulating platelets. Autoimmune destruction, consumption and clumping are not supported, because the film shows no clumps and there is no sign of widespread clotting.
  28. The correct answer is B (3000 rpm, because that speed gives a relative centrifugal force of about 1500 x g): At 3000 rpm and a 15 cm radius, RCF is 1.118 x10^-5 x 15 x 9,000,000, which is about 1509 x g. RCF depends on the square of the speed, so rpm cannot be set equal to the force. Using 1500 or 2000 rpm gives far too little force, and 4500 rpm gives more than double the force needed.
  29. The correct answer is C (Discard the expired vial, run a fresh one, and hold the result until controls pass): The deviation is large enough to be a 1-3s failure, but the vial is also past its open-vial limit, so the failure may come from degraded material. The expired vial is discarded, a fresh Level 3 vial is run, and the high patient result is held until the control passes. Acceptable low and normal levels do not verify performance at the high end.
  30. The correct answer is B (A negative test does not exclude disease, because donor red cells dilute hemoglobin S below detection): A solubility test needs roughly 10 to 20 percent hemoglobin S to turn turbid, and donor red cells after a recent transfusion can push the patient's S fraction below that. The negative result therefore cannot exclude sickle cell disease, and hemoglobin electrophoresis or HPLC on a sample from before transfusion is needed. Reagent failure is not the cause because donor cells do not consume dithionite.
  31. The correct answer is D (Large immature cells with scant cytoplasm and a high nuclear ratio, so blasts are suspected): The field is dominated by large cells with open chromatin, scant blue cytoplasm and a high nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio, which is the appearance of blasts and warrants urgent pathologist review. Small mature lymphocytes would have dense chromatin, and orderly granulopoiesis would show a range of maturing cells with granules. Rouleaux is a red cell feature and would not dominate a marrow field.
  32. The correct answer is D (No, because normal PT and aPTT can occur with relevant rivaroxaban levels, depending on reagent): Rivaroxaban inhibits factor Xa, and its effect on the PT and aPTT depends strongly on the reagent, so normal results can coexist with a relevant drug level. Neither test is a reliable rule-out. The thrombin time is not the right screen for an anti-Xa agent.
  33. The correct answer is B (Excess EDTA for the small blood volume shrinks red cells, so use a microtube): EDTA tubes are sized for a nearly full draw, and with only 0.5 mL the EDTA concentration rises well above the intended level, which pulls water out of red cells and lowers MCV and hematocrit. Too little EDTA would cause clots and low platelet counts rather than shrinkage. Hemolysis does not make the remaining cells small.
  34. The correct answer is C (Von Willebrand disease, because reduced VWF function also lowers factor VIII, which VWF protects in plasma): Low VWF antigen, low ristocetin cofactor activity and prolonged closure times with a normal platelet count fit von Willebrand disease. Factor VIII is also low because VWF carries and protects it, which prolongs the aPTT. Hemophilia A would not lengthen closure times, and aspirin would not lower VWF levels.
  35. The correct answer is D (Flag it as unreliable, because apixaban falsely raises the activity and can hide a deficiency): Apixaban inhibits factor Xa, the enzyme added in this assay, so the residual Xa is lowered and the antithrombin activity reads falsely high. A deficiency could be masked, so the result is not reliable on drug. The assay should be repeated off the drug or by a thrombin-based method.
  36. The correct answer is B (They fully activate contact factors before clotting starts, so timing reflects the intrinsic pathway): Silica, kaolin or ellagic acid activate the contact factors, and the incubation lets that activation finish. When calcium is then added the clock measures the intrinsic and common pathways from an even starting point. The test does not activate tissue factor and does not neutralize heparin.
  37. The correct answer is B (Myeloid, because CD13 and CD33 are myeloid associated markers and lymphoid markers are absent): CD13 and CD33 are myeloid associated antigens, and CD34 marks immature progenitors. The absence of CD3, CD19 and CD79a argues against T and B lineages. The pattern therefore supports a myeloid lineage assignment.
  38. The correct answer is D (Hemodilution of the aspirate with peripheral blood, so the smear underestimates the true marrow cellularity): A later pull from the same site draws in peripheral blood, which dilutes the marrow elements, removes spicules and gives mature cells in a low-cellularity smear. The biopsy shows the true cellularity, so the smear should not be used to judge it. Aplasia would show a hypocellular biopsy, and staining or clotting would not make the biopsy hypercellular.
  39. The correct answer is B (Bring it to room temperature and mix well by inversion, because cold blood sediments abnormally): Blood stored at 4 C sediments abnormally until it returns to room temperature, so the tube must be equilibrated and remixed before the test is set up. Warming to 37 C would speed the rate, and adding saline would dilute the sample and lower the result. The sample is still within the 24 hour limit, so it does not need to be rejected.
  40. The correct answer is D (90 x10^9/L, because 3.0 percent of the 3.00 x10^12/L red cell count is 90): Absolute reticulocytes equal the percentage times the red cell count: 0.030 x 3.00 x10^12/L = 90 x10^9/L, which is within the usual 25 to 100 x10^9/L interval. Dividing the red cell count by the percentage gives a meaningless larger number. The other values are off by factors of ten.
  41. The correct answer is C (Increased fragility, because the patient's cells lyse in saline concentrations the control cells tolerate): Cells that lyse at a higher salt concentration than the control have little spare surface area and burst early, so fragility is increased, as in spherocytosis. Decreased fragility would show lysis only in more dilute saline, as seen with target cells. The two curves are meant to be compared, so a difference in starting point is the result, not a fault.
  42. The correct answer is C (Report that the prolonged aPTT is not corrected by mixing, which suggests an inhibitor): When a prolonged aPTT is not corrected into the normal range after mixing with normal plasma, and stays long after incubation, an inhibitor is suggested rather than a simple factor deficiency, which would correct. The normal PT and fibrinogen and the absence of heparin make contamination unlikely. The report should state the mixing result so the case can go forward for further testing.
  43. The correct answer is B (Acetic acid diluent, because it makes hyaluronate form a stringy mucin clot that traps cells): Acetic acid precipitates hyaluronic acid into a stringy mucin clot that clumps cells and makes counting unreliable. Saline and hyaluronidase in saline are suitable because they do not lyse leukocytes. A light stain does not precipitate the fluid in this way.
  44. The correct answer is B (Millimetres per hour (mm/h), because it measures red cell fall in one hour): The Westergren method measures how far the red cell column falls in one hour, so the result is reported in mm/h. A per-minute unit does not match the fixed one-hour reading time. Percent and g/L describe volume fractions and concentrations, which are not what a sedimentation distance represents.
  45. The correct answer is A (Strike through once so the original stays legible, write the value, then initial and date): Good documentation practice keeps the original entry legible beneath a single line, adds the correct value, and shows who changed it and when. Correction fluid, scribbling, or destroying the page hides what was originally written and breaks the audit trail.
  46. The correct answer is B (The buffer pH was too high, because alkaline conditions favour blue dye binding and weaken eosin): Alkaline buffer pushes dye binding toward the basic dye, so red cells turn blue-gray and eosinophil granules lose their orange-red colour. A low pH does the reverse and gives pinkish cells. Short staining gives pale cells in general, and an acidic rinse would make the film pinker, not bluer.
  47. The correct answer is B (Verify intervals on the local population by age and sex, since populations and analytical methods differ): Pediatric values change with age and differ by method and population, so intervals need verification or local establishment, partitioned by age and sex as accreditation expects. Package insert limits and borrowed tables may not suit the local patients or analyzer. Adult limits misflag many healthy children, especially in the early years of life.
  48. The correct answer is D (Blood is drawn through a collagen-coated aperture until platelets plug it, and the time is reported): The PFA-100 pulls citrated whole blood under high shear through a membrane aperture coated with collagen and epinephrine or ADP. Platelet adhesion and aggregation block the aperture, and the closure time is the time until flow stops. Light transmission with stirring describes aggregometry instead.
  49. The correct answer is B (Investigate the cause, correct it, and show assessors the corrective action evidence): Accreditation bodies expect a documented investigation, a correction and evidence that the problem was fixed within the deadline. Removing a test or waiting for a later visit does not close the finding, and the vendor cannot fix a laboratory's own process.
  50. The correct answer is C (X-linked recessive, so a carrier mother passes the affected gene to about half of her sons): Hemophilia A is an X-linked recessive disorder because the factor VIII gene lies on the X chromosome, so affected males inherit the gene from carrier mothers, who pass it to about half of their sons. Autosomal patterns would affect both sexes equally, and X-linked dominant inheritance does not match the typical male-only presentation.
  51. The correct answer is D (24.2 x10^9/L, flagged high, with a comment about the smudge cells on the film): The absolute lymphocyte count is 31.0 x 78 / 100, which is 24.2 x10^9/L, far above the upper limit of 4.0, so it is flagged high with a comment about smudge cells. Dividing instead of multiplying, or dropping a factor of ten, gives values that cannot come from the formula. The film findings go for review as policy directs.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (3.6 percent, calculated as the observed 6.0 times 0.27 and then divided by 0.45): The corrected count is 6.0 x (0.27 / 0.45) = 3.6 percent. The correction lowers the percentage because the red cell mass is reduced and the same number of reticulocytes looks proportionally larger. Inverting the ratio gives 10.0, and omitting the division gives 1.6.
  2. The correct answer is C (Phone the clinician and record who received the result and when, since delay risks patient harm): A verified critical value must be communicated directly to a responsible clinician, with the recipient and the time documented, so treatment can start quickly. Waiting for staff to see the chart, averaging repeat runs or holding the result for a day all postpone care. Repeat testing is only used when a result looks doubtful, and nothing here suggests that.
  3. The correct answer is A (The 1 3s rule is broken, so the run is rejected and the cause is investigated): A single control result beyond three standard deviations from the mean violates the 1 3s rule, which is a rejection rule, so the run is rejected and patient results are held. The cause is investigated, such as control deterioration, reagent or calibration, and the control is repeated after correction. The warning rule cannot override a rejection rule, and acceptance cannot rest on one control level.
  4. The correct answer is C (Seal it at the fill line and replace it, since pressing the contents risks needle punctures): Sharps containers are sealed at the fill line, usually about three quarters full, because overfilling causes punctures when contents are pushed or fall out. Reaching in by hand or with tongs, and transferring loose sharps, exposes staff. Taping over protruding sharps does not make them safe to handle.
  5. The correct answer is C (60 x10^6/L, because 54 divided by 0.9 mm3 gives 60 cells per uL): The counted volume is 9 mm2 x 0.1 mm = 0.9 mm3 (0.9 uL), so 54 / 0.9 = 60 cells per uL, which equals 60 x10^6/L. Dividing by squares alone ignores the depth. Dividing by depth alone ignores the area counted.
  6. The correct answer is B (At least twenty results gathered over about twenty separate days to capture day to day variation): A reliable laboratory mean and standard deviation need at least twenty results collected across separate days, so that routine sources of variation such as reagent handling and operator differences are included. Very few results or results from a single shift underestimate the true spread. A large number run in one session still misses day to day variation.
  7. The correct answer is B (Wear gloves, contain and disinfect the leak, report it, and request a recollection): A leaked specimen is compromised and a spill risk, so staff wear gloves, contain and disinfect the leak, document the incident and ask for a recollection. Testing a leaked or transferred sample risks wrong results and exposure, and sending the box away leaves the collection site unaware of the packaging failure. Reporting the incident does not depend on results.
  8. The correct answer is C (Original value, corrected value, and who changed it when, because history must stay traceable): An audit trail must let an assessor reconstruct what was reported, what replaced it, and who made the change and at what time. Overwriting the earlier value removes the evidence that a clinician may already have acted on it. Recording only approvals or only the new value leaves the history incomplete.
  9. The correct answer is B (Spherocytes, because membrane loss leaves hemoglobin crowded into a much smaller cell volume): Spherocytes have lost surface membrane but kept their hemoglobin, so hemoglobin concentration per unit volume rises and the MCHC climbs above 360 g/L. Macrocytes and target cells do not raise concentration, and hypochromic cells lower it. A persistently raised MCHC is a useful clue to spherocytosis.
  10. The correct answer is A (Discard both tubes, report the event, and recollect both patients with correct bedside labelling): Once labelling is separated from the act of drawing, identity cannot be proven, whatever the hemoglobin values show. Delta checks only suggest a problem and cannot confirm that a tube belongs to a patient. Both patients must be recollected so no result is attached to the wrong person, and the event must be reported for review.
  11. The correct answer is D (Run it on the analyzer using mechanical clot detection, because turbidity and colour do not interfere): Mechanical clot detection senses viscosity change rather than light, so lipemia and icterus affect it far less than a photo optical method. Saline dilution changes clotting factor concentrations and gives a false result. Added citrate does not clear lipids and alters the blood to anticoagulant ratio.
  12. The correct answer is A (Red cells swelled in storage, raising MCV and hematocrit and lowering calculated MCHC, with stable hemoglobin): Red cells swell as EDTA samples age at room temperature, so MCV rises and the analyzer calculates a higher hematocrit, which pulls the calculated MCHC down while hemoglobin stays stable. The same indices would not come from real macrocytosis, since hemoglobin and the red cell count remain typical. Shrinkage would move the indices in the opposite direction, and platelet clumping does not change red cell indices.
Case CHB5

On a day shift in a regional hospital, the CBC analyzer passed its controls at 06:00. At 10:30 the repeat control set showed Level 1 hemoglobin at plus 2.3 SD and Level 2 hemoglobin at plus 2.4 SD within the same run. The previous seven control runs for both levels were within plus or minus 1 SD. Twelve patient CBCs were reported between 06:00 and 10:30.

  1. The correct answer is C (The 2-2s rule across levels, a rejection rule that most often indicates systematic error): Two control results in the same run beyond the same two SD limit on the same side violate the 2-2s rule, which is a rejection rule. It points to systematic error such as calibration drift or a reagent problem. The 1-3s rule needs a single result beyond three SD, and R-4s needs a spread above four SD.
  2. The correct answer is A (Stop reporting, find and correct the cause of the systematic error, then rerun the controls): A 2-2s violation is a rejection, so patient reporting stops until the cause is found and corrected. Controls must then be rerun and pass before results are released. Waiting until tomorrow, releasing after one level passes, or recalibrating without verifying with controls would all leave unverified results in circulation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Rerun samples still available, compare with first results, and correct reports where differences exceed limits): When a rejection rule fires, results released since the last acceptable controls may be affected. The laboratory retests retained samples, compares against the original values using its allowable difference limits, and issues corrected reports where needed. Leaving results alone ignores the possible error, and reanalyzing the whole day is unnecessary.
  4. The correct answer is A (Defective spectrin links weaken the membrane skeleton, so cells stay elongated after circulation): In hereditary elliptocytosis the horizontal protein links of the membrane skeleton, usually spectrin self-association or protein 4.1, are defective, so cells cannot recover their shape after being deformed in the circulation. Many carriers have normal hemoglobin and no hemolysis, as here. Iron deficiency causes pencil cells too, but with low hemoglobin and hypochromia.
  5. The correct answer is B (Factor XII, because contact factor deficiency prolongs the aPTT in vitro without impairing hemostasis): Factor XII is a contact factor needed for aPTT activation in the cuvette but is not required for hemostasis in the body, so deficiency gives a markedly long aPTT with no bleeding. Deficiencies of factor VIII, IX or XI usually cause bleeding, and factor X deficiency would prolong the PT as well.
  6. The correct answer is C (The INR is about 5.9, above the 5.0 limit, so it is reported as critical): The ratio 62 divided by 12.4 is 5.0, and 5.0 raised to the power 1.1 is about 5.9, which is above the critical limit of 5.0. Skipping the ISI gives 5.0 and makes the result look borderline. The INR is the standard way to report warfarin monitoring, so a call to the clinician follows.
  7. The correct answer is C (Reject and recollect, because additive potency and vacuum are guaranteed only until the expiry date): The manufacturer guarantees the additive amount and the vacuum only up to the expiry date, so a tube beyond it can give unreliable counts even when it fills. A full tube does not prove the EDTA is still effective. Because the patient is stable and recollection is easy, the specimen should be rejected.
  8. The correct answer is A (Ask for a regulated clinician to take the result, then document who received it and when): Critical results should go to a responsible licensed caregiver who can act, and the technologist must record who took the call. A receptionist note can be delayed or lost, and a fax does not prove that a clinician has seen the value. Holding the result leaves a patient at risk of bleeding.
  9. The correct answer is A (Release the tourniquet, withdraw the needle, apply firm pressure, and use the other arm for recollection): A rapidly forming swelling means blood is leaking from the vein into the tissue, a hematoma. The tourniquet must be released and the needle withdrawn at once, with firm pressure applied to stop further leakage. Recollection should use another site, and the tourniquet must never stay on while the needle is out.
  10. The correct answer is D (Cells turn spherical on standing, which disrupts rouleaux and falsely lowers the sedimentation rate): On long standing red cells lose their disc shape and become spherical, so they stack poorly into rouleaux and sediment more slowly. This lowers the ESR falsely. Plasma proteins do not break down within hours, and cells do not clump or shrink in the way the other statements describe.
  11. The correct answer is A (Raised MCHC with normal MCV, because spherocytes hold hemoglobin in a reduced surface area): Spherocytes lose membrane but keep their hemoglobin, so the hemoglobin concentration inside each cell rises and MCHC is often above 360 g/L while MCV stays near normal. Low MCHC with low MCV describes hypochromic microcytic anemias such as thalassemia or iron deficiency. A raised RDW occurs here too but is not specific.
  12. The correct answer is D (Test heparinized patient plasmas on the old and new lots and compare with anti-Xa results): A heparin range is tied to the response of a given reagent, so each new lot is checked against heparinized patient samples with a reference such as anti-Xa. Normal plasmas cannot show how the reagent responds to heparin, and an insert range was not validated on the laboratory's own analyzer and patients.
  13. The correct answer is C (11 hours, because 30 minus the 19 hours already logged leaves 11): The total across the cycle is what counts, so the hours logged add up to 8 plus 11, which is 19. Subtracting from 30 leaves 11 hours. An even yearly split or counting only one year does not match a cycle total.
  14. The correct answer is C (Liquid EDTA or sodium heparin, because neither forms crystals that mimic pathologic ones): Powdered anticoagulants and oxalate can form crystals that resemble calcium pyrophosphate or other pathologic crystals under polarized light. Liquid EDTA or sodium heparin avoid that artefact while preventing clots. Inflamed synovial fluid can clot in a plain tube and trap the cells.
  15. The correct answer is A (Withdraw and discard the volume your policy requires, then collect the light blue tube): Withdrawing and discarding the volume required by policy clears heparinized fluid from the catheter before the coagulation tube is filled. Reflushing with heparin adds the very contaminant that must be avoided. Waiting thirty minutes does not remove heparin from the hub, and a one millilitre discard is too small.
  16. The correct answer is A (Hepatic synthetic failure with splenic platelet pooling, because cirrhosis lowers factor and fibrinogen production): Cirrhosis reduces hepatic synthesis of fibrinogen and factors II, VII, IX and X, and portal hypertension pools platelets in an enlarged spleen. The known chronic disease argues against acute DIC. Warfarin and hemophilia A would not produce this combined picture.
  17. The correct answer is D (It must be platelet-poor plasma, with a platelet count below 10 x10^9/L, before freezing): Plasma that will be frozen must be platelet-poor, usually by a second centrifugation, because platelets that survive freezing release phospholipid and factors that disturb results after thawing. Filtration and dilution are not the standard requirement, and chilling does not replace removing the platelets.
  18. The correct answer is C (Run the manufacturer cleaning agent through the pathway, because it dissolves protein deposits): Weekly cleaning with the manufacturer agent removes protein film and cell debris that build up on apertures and in lines. Water alone does not dissolve protein deposits, and soaking parts in household bleach can damage seals and probes. Wiping the exterior leaves the counting pathway untouched.
  19. The correct answer is A (Microcytic hypochromic red cells, because size is microcytic and low hemoglobin content is hypochromic): Microcytic describes cells smaller than the reference size, and hypochromic describes reduced staining density with enlarged central pallor, so the two terms together fit this film. Hyperchromic would imply a dense cell with little or no pallor, and polychromatic cells are bluish young red cells rather than pale ones. Standard terms keep reports consistent between technologists.
  20. The correct answer is C (Yes, because vitamin K antagonist effect has cleared and the acute clotting episode has resolved): Vitamin K antagonist effect clears within a few weeks of stopping therapy, and no acute clot, pregnancy or estrogen remains to lower the proteins. Testing during warfarin or soon after a clot would give false low values instead. Estrogen and pregnancy are not the only conditions that lower these assays, but neither is present here.
  21. The correct answer is A (Request a recollection from the opposite arm, because line heparin contaminates the sample): A result that rose abruptly from 72 to 118 seconds in a sample drawn from the infusion arm suggests contamination with infusion heparin, so a fresh sample from the opposite arm is needed before any report or dose change. Reporting the value could lead to an unnecessary rate reduction. Diluting or re-running the same plasma cannot remove the contaminating heparin.
  22. The correct answer is C (Reject it and request a recollection, because in vitro clotting generates products that raise D-dimer): A clotted specimen has activated coagulation and fibrinolysis in the tube, so fibrin is formed and broken down in vitro and the D-dimer is falsely raised. Removing the clot or adding a comment does not restore an accurate value. The correct step is rejection with a recollection request, and the physician should be told why.
  23. The correct answer is C (Antiphospholipid antibodies can interfere with the strip clotting reaction and falsely raise INR): Point-of-care INR strips use a different reagent system than laboratory methods, and antiphospholipid antibodies can interfere with the clotting reaction, giving falsely high values. A venous laboratory INR avoids that interference. Capillary blood and lupus anticoagulant do not alter warfarin or factor VII in the way the other statements claim.
  24. The correct answer is D (Scratches and film obscure the grid and cells, so clean or replace the chamber first): Scratches and dried residue hide the ruled boundaries and can trap or mimic cells, so a count on this chamber is unreliable. Chamber depth is set by the glass and cannot be fixed with a factor. Residue is not removed by the diluent, and platelets are affected as much as white cells.
  25. The correct answer is B (Depleted stores before anemia, because ferritin falls first and hemoglobin drops once stores are exhausted): Iron deficiency develops in stages: stores fall first (low ferritin), then transport iron falls, and only later do hemoglobin and MCV decline. This donor has exhausted her stores but is not yet anemic. Thalassemia trait would show microcytosis with a normal ferritin.
  26. The correct answer is A (A buttoned laboratory coat, disposable gloves, and eye protection if splashing is possible, per routine practices): Routine practices call for a fastened laboratory coat and gloves for open blood work, with eye protection when a splash is possible. Gloves alone leave skin and clothing exposed. Bloodborne viruses spread by blood contact rather than through the air, and a full gown with a face shield and double gloves exceeds what a routine film preparation needs.
  27. The correct answer is D (Intrinsic pathway factors VIII, IX or XI, since these lengthen the aPTT and spare the PT): The aPTT tests the intrinsic and common pathways while the PT tests the extrinsic and common pathways. A prolonged aPTT with a normal PT therefore points to the intrinsic factors, such as VIII, IX, XI or XII. Common pathway deficiencies would lengthen both tests, and factor VII affects only the PT.
  28. The correct answer is B (Secure the reports and notify the privacy officer, because this is a possible breach): Exposed personal health information is a possible privacy breach and must be contained and reported through the privacy officer, who decides on notification and next steps. Quiet destruction hides the incident and prevents any assessment. Notifying patients is a later step coordinated by the privacy officer, not the first action of a bench technologist.
  29. The correct answer is A (Phone the emergency department at once to reach a physician, and document the call): A febrile young child with sickle cells and a low hemoglobin needs urgent medical review, so the laboratory contacts the responsible physician directly and records the call. Routine release lets the child go home unreviewed. Laboratory staff do not contact parents with results, and email is not a reliable urgent channel.
  30. The correct answer is C (Process the fluid within one hour and add albumin to the chamber to protect cells): Cells in CSF are fragile and begin to deteriorate within about an hour because the fluid has little protein or nutrient. Processing promptly and adding albumin to the chamber protects them and improves spreading. Higher speed distorts cells further, cooling overnight accelerates degeneration, and longer staining cannot restore cells that have already degenerated.
  31.
