The correct answer is B (Add a comment that donor hemoglobin A from recent transfusion masks the true hemoglobin S proportion): Donor red cells contain hemoglobin A, which can make up most of the pattern for weeks after transfusion and mimic trait. The report must state that donor cells are present so the result is not read as a change in genotype. Donor cells last for about their natural life of weeks, and the specimen can still be tested and interpreted with a comment.
  32. The correct answer is C (Run the manufacturer's cleaning cycle and repeat the background check, because debris causes false counts): A background count above the limit means the system is contaminated by debris, a partial aperture blockage or dirty diluent, and the fault must be removed rather than numerically offset. Further dilution changes the method and does not cure the source. Controls analyzed on an unclean system give unreliable assurance, so the cleaning and a passing background come first.
  33. The correct answer is C (5.7 x10^9/L, absolute lymphocytosis, because the value exceeds the adult upper limit): Absolute count equals percent times total count divided by 100, so 48 x 11.8 / 100 is 5.66, which rounds to 5.7 x10^9/L and exceeds the upper limit of 4.0. A normal-looking relative percentage does not exclude an absolute increase. The calculations that give 0.48 or 4.8 skip the total count or use the wrong factor.
  34. The correct answer is D (Reject it and recollect, because the four hour limit passed and platelets may neutralize heparin): Platelets in an uncentrifuged tube release platelet factor 4, which neutralizes heparin and falsely shortens the aPTT. After six hours the damage is already done and spinning cannot reverse it. The twenty four hour stability applies to PT in a capped citrate tube, not to a heparin monitoring aPTT.
  35. The correct answer is A (The dye precipitates residual RNA inside living unfixed cells into visible blue reticulum strands): Reticulocytes contain leftover ribosomal RNA, and a supravital stain such as new methylene blue clumps that RNA into a blue network inside the living cell. Fixing the film first would not produce this reticulum pattern. The dye does not target hemoglobin, and Romanowsky stains do enter young cells but show polychromasia rather than reticulum.
  36. The correct answer is C (Reject the tube and request a new sample, because the room temperature limit was exceeded): ESR depends on fresh red cells, and after about 4 hours at room temperature the cells swell and change shape, which lowers the sedimentation rate. This tube was held for 6.5 hours, past the policy limit, and refrigerating it now cannot reverse the change. Adding a comment or diluting with saline does not make the result valid.
  37. The correct answer is C (Collect by venipuncture instead, because cold, poorly perfused fingers give unreliable, hemoconcentrated capillary results): Cold, vasoconstricted fingers give sluggish flow and a sample that is concentrated, so capillary results from them can differ greatly from true values. A 41 g/L difference is far larger than the small usual capillary versus venous difference. A venous specimen is the reliable alternative, and no fixed bias can be used to correct the result.
  38. The correct answer is D (It senses ball motion rather than light absorbance, so sample colour has little effect): Mechanical analyzers detect the clot by the change in ball or probe movement as fibrin forms, so plasma colour does not alter the endpoint. Turbidimetric and chromogenic methods read light and can be affected by bilirubin, hemoglobin or lipid. Bilirubin is not removed during sampling and does not speed clotting.
  39. The correct answer is A (It sits flat on the raised rails and holds the chamber depth at exactly 0.1 mm): A hemocytometer count assumes a depth of 0.1 mm between the grid and the coverslip. The thick coverslip sits flat on the raised rails and holds that depth, so the counted volume is correct. A thin cover slip flexes or lifts and changes the volume, shifting every result.
  40. The correct answer is A (Trypanosoma, because a flagellum, undulating membrane and kinetoplast mark a trypanosome in the bloodstream): Trypanosomes are small extracellular flagellates with a kinetoplast and an undulating membrane, and East African disease is a travel-related fever that the film can show. Leishmania amastigotes live inside macrophages, malaria gametocytes sit inside red cells, and microfilariae are much larger sheathed worms.
  41. The correct answer is D (New methylene blue, which precipitates ribosomal RNA in living young red cells): New methylene blue is a supravital stain that precipitates the ribosomal RNA of reticulocytes into a blue network while the cells are still living. Wright-Giemsa stains dried films and shows polychromasia but not the reticulum. Prussian blue detects iron, not RNA.
  42. The correct answer is A (Implied consent, shown by the patient offering the arm after the procedure is explained): Routine venipuncture usually relies on implied consent, where a patient who understands the procedure and offers an arm indicates agreement. Written consent is reserved for situations the provincial rules or laboratory policy require, and a requisition or a family member cannot replace the capable patient's own agreement. The phlebotomist must still explain what will happen.
  43. The correct answer is B (Keep the tube at 37 C until testing, because warmth stops antibody binding): Cold agglutinins bind red cells at lower temperatures and the binding reverses on warming, so keeping the sample near body temperature prevents clumping. Chilling makes agglutination worse, and extra anticoagulant does nothing to antibody-coated cells. Room temperature can still be cool enough for antibodies with a high thermal range.
  44. The correct answer is A (Sezary cells, because cerebriform nuclei suggest a cutaneous T-cell lymphoma in the blood): Sezary cells have a convoluted, cerebriform nucleus and are the circulating cells of Sezary syndrome, a cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, consistent with erythroderma. Hairy cells have fine cytoplasmic projections and a smooth oval nucleus, and prolymphocytes have a single prominent nucleolus. Reactive lymphocytes vary in size and are not folded in this uniform way.
  45. The correct answer is D (Stomatocytes, seen in alcohol-related liver disease, because water entry swells the cell into a bowl): Stomatocytes have a slit-like central pallor and occur in alcohol use and liver disease, as well as in hereditary stomatocytosis. Spherocytes lack pallor, elliptocytes are oval, and teardrop cells have a single tail, so none fits a mouth-like slit.
  46. The correct answer is A (Replace plasma with saline and repeat hemoglobin, because lipemia falsely raises the measured value): The hemoglobin is out of proportion to the hematocrit, and a milky specimen points to lipemic turbidity adding apparent absorbance, which falsely raises hemoglobin and MCHC. Replacing the plasma with saline or running a plasma blank gives a reliable hemoglobin. The two measurements are related, the rule of three is only a check, and milky plasma is not clotting.
  47. The correct answer is B (Document the incidents and report them to a supervisor under the policy): A workplace policy gives a defined path. After asking him to stop, documenting each incident and reporting it to a supervisor under the policy is the next step. Confronting him publicly, tolerating the behaviour or responding in kind either escalates conflict or leaves the harassment unaddressed.
  48. The correct answer is D (Ask for the nurse or physician caring for the patient, and request a read-back at once): Critical results must reach a licensed professional who can act on them, so the call goes to the nurse or physician responsible for the patient. A read-back confirms that the value was heard correctly. Passing the value through a clerk, voicemail or a fax gives no assurance that anyone acted in time.
  49. The correct answer is B (Cabot rings, remnants of the mitotic spindle seen in megaloblastic and dyserythropoietic red cell production): Cabot rings are thin red-violet loops or figure-eights, thought to be mitotic spindle remnants, and appear in megaloblastic and dyserythropoietic anemias like this one. Stippling is granular, malaria rings contain a pale center and a nuclear dot, and Howell-Jolly bodies are small dense round dots.
  50. The correct answer is D (Report INR 2.6 with the target range, because the value lies within 2.0 to 3.0): The INR is reported as measured, together with the stated target, and 2.6 lies inside 2.0 to 3.0. The normal interval for people not on anticoagulants does not apply to warfarin therapy. Dose decisions belong to the clinician, and a result inside the target does not need a repeat.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Perform dRVVT lupus testing, because values that rise on dilution point to a phospholipid-dependent inhibitor): A mix that does not correct, together with several unrelated factors that look low and rise at high dilution, is typical of a lupus anticoagulant that dilutes out. The next step is dRVVT-based testing. True deficiencies of three factors in a patient without bleeding would be unusual, and a specific factor VIII inhibitor would not depress factors IX and XI.
Case CHB6

A 67-year-old woman who follows a strict vegan diet reports fatigue and numb feet. Hemoglobin 78 g/L, MCV 118 fL, RDW-CV 19.8 percent, WBC 3.1 x10^9/L, platelets 98 x10^9/L, reticulocytes 0.6 percent. LDH is markedly raised and indirect bilirubin mildly raised. The film shows macro-ovalocytes and neutrophils with six lobes. Serum vitamin B12 is 90 pmol/L (reference 150 to 700).

  1. The correct answer is A (Ineffective erythropoiesis, because fragile precursors die in the marrow and release LDH and bilirubin): In megaloblastic anemia, DNA synthesis is impaired and many precursors are destroyed inside the marrow. This intramedullary destruction releases LDH and raises indirect bilirubin without reticulocytosis. True hemolysis would show a high reticulocyte count.
  2. The correct answer is C (Folate can partly correct the anemia while neurologic damage from B12 deficiency continues to progress): Folic acid can bypass the block in DNA synthesis and improve the blood count, which hides the B12 deficiency, while the neurologic injury from B12 deficiency keeps advancing. The patient already has numb feet, which makes this a real risk. Folic acid does not alter the B12 immunoassay.
  3. The correct answer is A (More than 5 percent of neutrophils showing five or more nuclear lobes on the film): Hypersegmentation is commonly defined as more than 5 percent of neutrophils with five or more lobes, or any with six or more lobes. Neutrophils with three or four lobes are normal. Bilobed neutrophils suggest the Pelger-Huet anomaly, not megaloblastic change.
  4. The correct answer is A (Type 1 von Willebrand disease, because antigen and activity are both reduced to a similar degree): VWF antigen and activity are both reduced to a similar degree, giving a ratio near 1, which is the type 1 quantitative deficiency pattern, and factor VIII falls secondarily because VWF stabilizes it. Type 2A shows activity well below antigen, usually with a ratio below about 0.6. Hemophilia A has normal VWF levels.
  5. The correct answer is D (An INR of 2.3, because the 2.0 ratio is raised to the power 1.2): The ratio is 24.0 divided by 12.0, which is 2.0, and the INR is that ratio raised to the power of the ISI. Two raised to the power 1.2 is about 2.30, so the INR is 2.3. Dividing alone ignores the ISI, and multiplying the ratio or the patient time by the ISI is not the formula.
  6. The correct answer is A (Records may be viewed only for care or authorized purposes, so decline the request): Provincial health privacy law such as PHIPA allows access to personal health information only for care or another authorized purpose. Curiosity about a neighbour is not authorized, and secrecy does not make it lawful. Being an employee, not copying, or later permission does not repair an unauthorized view.
  7. The correct answer is A (Low speed and short time left cells suspended, so fluid soaked into the card): Cytocentrifuge deposition depends on enough force and time to drive cells onto the slide. Running at 300 rpm for 2 minutes instead of 1000 rpm for 5 minutes leaves cells suspended, so fluid soaks into the card and carries them away. A saturated card and a fluid ring argue against a clamp leak or a specimen problem.
  8. The correct answer is B (Biological Substance, Category B (UN3373), in a leakproof triple packaging system since pathogens are possible): Specimens that may contain pathogens, but not a high-consequence one, are shipped as Biological Substance, Category B, UN3373, using a leakproof primary container, secondary packaging with absorbent material and a rigid outer box. Category A is reserved for pathogens that cause permanent disability or life-threatening disease. An exemption applies only when the likelihood of pathogens is minimal, which suspected infection excludes.
  9. The correct answer is D (Precision is acceptable but accuracy is not, so investigate calibration before reporting more patient results): A CV of 1.2 percent shows results are consistent, but a bias of plus 4.5 percent exceeds the allowed 3 percent, so results are consistently too high. This is a systematic accuracy problem that points to calibration or reagent issues. Low imprecision does not make results accurate, and a bias above the limit cannot simply be documented.
  10. The correct answer is D (Systematic error, a sustained shift in the mean, often caused by new reagent lots or recalibration): A sustained move of the whole pattern to one side with unchanged scatter is a shift in the mean, which is systematic error. Triggers include a new reagent lot, a calibration change or maintenance. Random error would show wider scatter, not a displaced centre.
  11. The correct answer is C (2.5 percent, found by dividing the 50 infected cells by 2,000 red cells counted): Percent parasitemia is infected red cells divided by total red cells counted, times 100. Here 50 divided by 2,000 is 0.025, so 2.5 percent. The other values come from using the wrong denominator or inverting the fraction.
  12. The correct answer is C (Alert the ward to disregard the report, then correct records and report the incident): A wrong-patient result that has already reached the ward can harm care, so the first step is to alert clinicians to disregard it, then correct the records and open an incident review to find the cause. Delaying until an audit or editing silently leaves the ward acting on bad data and hides the error. Recollection is needed later for the requisition patient but does not come first.
  13. The correct answer is B (Citrate agar electrophoresis at acid pH, because S separates from D and G there): At alkaline pH, hemoglobins S, D and G run together, but on citrate agar at acid pH hemoglobin S moves with a different mobility from D and G, so the second method resolves them. A longer run at the same pH does not change relative mobility. Osmotic fragility and acid elution do not distinguish hemoglobin variants.
  14. The correct answer is B (Stop at once, release the tourniquet, and respect the refusal while documenting what was said): A capable patient may withdraw consent at any point, so the phlebotomist must stop, release the tourniquet and document the refusal. Continuing would be an assault, and neither a waiver nor a physician can override a capable patient's refusal at the moment. The ordering provider should be told about the uncollected specimen through the normal process.
  15. The correct answer is D (Withhold the count, recollect in a citrate tube, and report that value with a method comment): EDTA induced clumping traps platelets so the analyzer undercounts them, giving a falsely low value. Recollecting in citrate avoids the effect, and the result is reported with a comment about the method. Reporting the clumped count or applying a guessed multiplier gives an unreliable value.
  16. The correct answer is A (Patient name plus a second unique identifier, such as date of birth or health card number): Two unique patient identifiers, typically name plus date of birth or health card number, must agree between the tube and the requisition before a specimen is accepted. Cap colour, billing numbers, room numbers and route details do not identify the person. Matching only non-unique information could allow a specimen from a different patient to be accessioned.
  17. The correct answer is C (Flammable liquid, so the stain stays away from ignition sources because its vapours can ignite): The flame pictogram marks flammable products, and methanol-based stain gives off vapour that ignites near heat, sparks or hot plates. A flame drawn over a circle would mean an oxidizer, and a corrosion symbol shows liquid dripping onto a hand and a metal bar. Storage and handling therefore focus on keeping ignition sources away.
  18. The correct answer is A (4.1 x10^12/L, because 410 x 200 divided by the 0.02 uL counted gives 4.1 million/uL): Five small squares of 0.2 mm x 0.2 mm cover 0.2 mm2, and at 0.1 mm depth the counted volume is 0.02 uL. The count is 410 x 200 / 0.02, which is 4,100,000 per uL, or 4.1 x10^12/L. Using one square's area or the wrong dilution factor moves the answer by large factors.
  19. The correct answer is A (Exclude the single dense edge inclusions, because they are Heinz bodies and not precipitated RNA): New methylene blue also stains Heinz bodies, which appear as single, dense, round inclusions at the cell edge rather than a threadlike network. They must be excluded from the reticulocyte count or the result is falsely high. A Wright stain does not show a reticulum, and the specimen is acceptable.
  20. The correct answer is D (Hemoglobin A2 above 3.5 percent, with hemoglobin F normal or slightly raised): With less beta chain made, extra delta chains pair with alpha chains, so hemoglobin A2 rises above about 3.5 percent and hemoglobin F may rise slightly. A very high hemoglobin F is typical of beta thalassemia major, and hemoglobin H appears in alpha thalassemia, not beta trait.
  21. The correct answer is B (Turbidity from lipids falsely raises the photometric hemoglobin, which also raises the calculated MCHC): Hemoglobin is measured by light absorbance, and the turbid lipid particles scatter light so the reading is falsely high. Because MCHC is calculated from hemoglobin and hematocrit, it rises too, while the directly counted red cells and hematocrit stay normal. The laboratory can use a plasma replacement step to correct the hemoglobin.
  22. The correct answer is A (The four large corner squares, which together cover 4 mm2 of the 9 mm2 counting grid): In the routine white cell count, cells are counted in the four large corner squares, each 1 mm by 1 mm, so the area is 4 mm2. The central square is ruled finely for red cells and platelets. The nine-square area is used only when counts are low.
  23. The correct answer is C (Use a smaller drop and stop short of the slide end, because the film must taper): A feathered edge forms only when the blood runs out before the end of the slide, so a smaller drop and a push that stops short leave a tapered tail. Larger drops, extra pressure or warming the slide do not create tapering and often make the film thicker or distorted.
  24. The correct answer is C (Use a pre-warmed plain tube kept at 37 C until clotted, because cold precipitates the cryoprotein): Cryoglobulins are examined in serum, so blood must clot and retract at 37 C so that any cold-precipitating protein is not trapped in the clot. Anticoagulant tubes give plasma rather than serum and early chilling causes the precipitate to be lost. Fluoride and EDTA have no role in preserving cryoproteins.
  25. The correct answer is B (Phone the clinician now, because fibrinogen this low in a bleeding patient is a critical value): A fibrinogen of 0.9 g/L with a high D-dimer after thrombolysis in a bleeding patient signals a serious lytic state that the clinician must hear about immediately. Repeating the test first delays care, and a routine report ignores the bleeding risk. Protamine does not correct a thrombin time prolonged by a plasminogen activator.
  26. The correct answer is A (Phospholipid and a contact activator, with incubation allowing full contact factor activation before calcium is added): The aPTT reagent contains phospholipid substituting for platelet membranes and a contact activator such as silica or ellagic acid. The incubation lets factors XII, XI and prekallikrein activate fully, and the timer starts only when calcium is added. Tissue factor belongs to the PT reagent, and thrombin is not part of the aPTT reagent.
  27. The correct answer is D (Hold the contents, record the excursion, and inform the supervisor, because stability is now unverified): An out-of-range refrigerator means reagent stability can no longer be assured, so the contents are placed on hold until the supervisor decides their fate. The excursion must be documented with the reading and the time. Adjusting the thermostat or freezing the items does not restore verified storage conditions.
  28. The correct answer is C (Warm the tube at 37 C and repeat the count, because heat disperses the clumps): Cold agglutinins bind red cells below body temperature and the clumping reverses on warming. Incubating the sample at 37 C and repeating the count gives indices that reflect the true red cell population. Cooling would worsen the clumping, and reporting the clumped results would give misleading RBC, MCV and MCHC values.
  29. The correct answer is D (Remove the glove, let it bleed freely, then wash thoroughly with soap and running water): Immediate aid for a percutaneous exposure is to let the wound bleed freely and wash it thoroughly with soap and running water. Squeezing and scrubbing damage tissue and do not lower infection risk, and caustic agents such as bleach injure skin. Reporting and follow-up with occupational health happen right after the wound is washed.
  30. The correct answer is B (Many immature blasts with fine chromatin and a high nuclear to cytoplasm ratio): Acute leukemia is defined by abundant blasts: large cells with fine open chromatin, scant cytoplasm and a high nuclear to cytoplasm ratio. Mature small lymphocytes suggest a reactive or chronic lymphoid process, and rouleaux or hypersegmentation point to protein or megaloblastic disorders, not acute leukemia.
  31. The correct answer is A (It shows association, not agreement, so a steady offset can persist, because bias is separate): A correlation coefficient shows how tightly paired results rise together, but two methods can differ by a steady offset and still correlate at 0.99. Agreement is judged from the slope, intercept or mean difference between methods. Percentages can be correlated and the coefficient does not depend on cell morphology.
  32. The correct answer is C (Stop further INR reporting, notify the supervisor or on-call manager and preserve the log): Two failed control results mean patient results from that period are unreliable, so reporting should stop and the supervisor or on-call manager must be told, with the log preserved. Trusting the colleague, a partial recheck or deleting entries hides a quality failure.
  33. The correct answer is A (Examine a stained blood film for platelet clumps and estimate the platelet number): An unexplained large fall in platelets without bleeding often comes from EDTA-dependent clumping that the analyzer did not flag, so a film check is the best way to confirm the number. Reporting it as critical first could cause unnecessary treatment. Warming the tube or adding EDTA does not reverse clumps that have already formed.
  34. The correct answer is A (Systematic error, the 2 2s rule, so the run is rejected, because both points are high): Two consecutive control values beyond two standard deviations on the same side of the mean violate the 2 2s rule, which signals systematic error such as calibration shift. The run is rejected even though neither value passes the three-SD limit. Calling it a warning or no violation would let results from a biased system go out.
  35. The correct answer is B (Collect now, before the 0800 dose, because a trough is the lowest concentration in the interval): A trough sample is drawn shortly before the next scheduled dose, when the drug concentration is at its lowest. Drawing after the dose measures a peak or mid-interval value that the pharmacist cannot use for trough dosing decisions. The actual collection time should still be recorded so the result is interpreted correctly.
  36. The correct answer is A (The t(9;22) translocation producing the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene, an active tyrosine kinase): The Philadelphia chromosome, t(9;22)(q34;q11), creates the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene whose tyrosine kinase drives chronic myeloid leukemia. The t(15;17) change belongs to acute promyelocytic leukemia, t(8;14) to Burkitt lymphoma, and JAK2 V617F to polycythemia vera and related disorders.
  37. The correct answer is C (Lipemia falsely raises hemoglobin, so perform a plasma replacement blank and report the corrected value): Lipid particles scatter light in the hemoglobin reading, so hemoglobin and MCHC rise while the cell-derived hematocrit is unaffected. A plasma replacement procedure removes the lipid effect. Microclots tend to lower counts, and a true polycythemia would raise the hematocrit as well.
  38. The correct answer is A (Add hyaluronidase to break down hyaluronic acid, then mix gently and count the cells): Viscosity in synovial fluid comes from hyaluronic acid, and hyaluronidase breaks it down so the sample pipettes evenly. Acetic acid actually causes the mucin to clot, which makes counting harder. Heating and spinning do not restore an even cell suspension.
  39. The correct answer is D (Clean the probe with the recommended solution, run a wash cycle, then verify with a control): Dried reagent on the probe tip causes short sampling and must be removed with the manufacturer solution, followed by a wash cycle. A control run confirms that the cleaned probe aspirates correctly. Bending or scraping a probe damages it, and changing the volume setting hides the fault.
  40. The correct answer is A (Dimorphic population from transfusion, because donor cells of normal size coexist with small patient cells): Donor red cells of normal size circulate alongside the patient's own microcytic cells, giving a dimorphic curve and a wide RDW that fits the recent transfusion. Cold agglutinins mainly give a falsely high MCV with a low RBC rather than two clean peaks. Calibration drift would shift a single peak, not create two.
  41. The correct answer is D (Pronormoblast, basophilic, polychromatic, orthochromatic normoblast, then reticulocyte, because hemoglobin accumulates as the nucleus condenses): Erythroid cells shrink and the nucleus condenses as hemoglobin accumulates, so the cytoplasm shifts from deep blue (basophilic) through grey-blue (polychromatic) to pink-orange (orthochromatic) before the nucleus is extruded. Swapping the basophilic and polychromatic stages fails because ribosomal RNA, which makes the cytoplasm blue, is lost as hemoglobin builds.
  42. The correct answer is D (Many red cell fragments and microcytes, because their small volume places them inside the platelet window): Impedance counting cannot tell platelets from other particles of similar volume, so schistocytes and microcytes are counted as platelets and raise the result. Macrocytes are larger than the platelet window, and rouleaux or neutrophil lobes do not generate platelet-sized pulses. A film review confirms the interference and a manual or alternative count corrects the value.
  43. The correct answer is B (Telephone the clinician now and document the call, because bacteria inside neutrophils suggest bacteremia): Organisms inside neutrophils on a patient film, with fever and a left shift, are a critical finding that needs an immediate call and documentation, because treatment should not wait for culture growth. The clean batch rules out stain contamination. Toxic granulation and culture-first reporting do not replace the call.
  44. The correct answer is D (Acetic acid based diluent, because it precipitates hyaluronate and traps cells in a tight clot): Acetic acid reacts with hyaluronate in synovial fluid to form a mucin clot that traps cells and makes counting unreliable. Saline or hyaluronidase is used instead, since hyaluronidase breaks down the viscosity and normal saline does not clot the fluid. Hypotonic diluents would lyse cells rather than cause mucin clot formation.
  45. The correct answer is B (Intrinsic pathway factors, because the PT, thrombin time and fibrinogen are all normal): The PT of 12.4 seconds, thrombin time of 16 seconds and fibrinogen of 3.1 g/L are all within their intervals, so the extrinsic and common pathways and fibrinogen conversion are intact. The isolated aPTT prolongation to 74 seconds therefore localizes the defect to the intrinsic pathway factors VIII, IX, XI or XII. Heparin would also lengthen the thrombin time, which is normal here.
  46. The correct answer is A (18.0, above 13, which favours iron deficiency, consistent with the low ferritin): The index is 74 / 4.10 = 18.0, above 13, which favours iron deficiency and agrees with the very low ferritin. Values below 13 favour thalassemia trait. Inverting the ratio gives 0.06, which is not how the index is read.
Case CHB7

A 4-year-old child with fever attends the emergency department. The CBC shows a white count of 3.0 x10^9/L, with hemoglobin and platelets within interval. The automated differential reports neutrophils 20 percent, lymphocytes 70 percent, monocytes 8 percent and eosinophils 2 percent. The laboratory pediatric intervals at age 4 are neutrophils 1.5 to 8.5 x10^9/L and lymphocytes 1.5 to 7.0 x10^9/L. The critical limit for absolute neutrophils is below 0.5 x10^9/L.

  1. The correct answer is B (Neutrophils 0.6 x10^9/L and lymphocytes 2.1 x10^9/L, from white count times percentage over 100): Absolute count equals white count times percentage divided by 100, so neutrophils are 3.0 x 20 / 100, or 0.6, and lymphocytes are 3.0 x 70 / 100, or 2.1 x10^9/L. Taking a tenth of the percentage or dividing by ten misplaces the decimal. Both absolute values are needed to judge which cell line is truly abnormal.
  2. The correct answer is C (The absolute count is within the interval, so the high percentage reflects the low neutrophils): The absolute lymphocyte count of 2.1 x10^9/L sits inside the 1.5 to 7.0 interval, so there is no lymphocytosis. The percentage looks high only because neutrophils are low and make up a smaller share of a small total. Judging by absolute counts avoids this misleading impression.
  3. The correct answer is D (Report absolute counts with the neutrophils flagged low, and no critical call is required): Absolute neutrophils of 0.6 x10^9/L are below the interval, so they are flagged low, but they are above the critical limit of 0.5, so no critical call is required by this policy. Percentages alone hide the neutropenia, and not every value outside the interval is critical. Delaying the report for a repeat tube leaves the clinician without information during a febrile illness.
  4. The correct answer is C (Blasts that stain positive for myeloperoxidase and Sudan black B, reflecting primary granule enzymes): Myeloperoxidase and Sudan black B stain primary granule components found in myeloid blasts, and a positive result in at least 3 percent of blasts supports AML. Lymphoblasts are usually negative for both and may show PAS block positivity or TdT positivity. A panel with every stain negative does not point to myeloid lineage.
  5. The correct answer is B (Approve the change, verify performance, revise the SOP and train staff, then implement): Change control means the change is approved first, its performance is verified, the procedure is revised and staff are trained, and only then is it put into use. Doing verification or documentation afterward lets unverified methods affect patient results.
  6. The correct answer is B (A left shift, showing a single population of small red cells consistent with a low MCV): The patient's histogram has a single peak at a lower volume than the normal curve, which is a left shift and means the cells are small. A right shift would mean macrocytosis. The curve has one peak, so it is not a mixed population, and a normal RBC count says nothing about cell size.
  7. The correct answer is A (240 x10^9/L, which falls within the usual reference interval of 150 to 400 x10^9/L): Multiplying 12 platelets per field by 20 gives 240 x10^9/L, which lies inside 150 to 400. The values of 120 and 24 come from dividing or dropping a factor, and 2400 adds an extra zero. The estimate should then be compared with the analyzer count.
  8. The correct answer is B (Calibration verification, which confirms assigned values are recovered without changing the instrument settings): Calibration verification uses materials with assigned values spanning the reportable range and checks that the instrument recovers them within limits, without altering calibration factors. Calibration itself adjusts those factors. Daily controls monitor ongoing performance, and reference interval studies are separate from calibration.
  9. The correct answer is D (Obtain a read-back from a responsible nurse and document her name, the time and the value): Critical results need direct verbal communication to a caregiver who can act, confirmed by read-back and recorded with name, time and value. Electronic posting or a message with a clerk does not confirm that anyone who can act has received it. Skipping the nurse does not replace the required handoff.
  10. The correct answer is B (Report it within the age-adjusted interval without a high flag, since ESR rises with age): ESR normally rises with age, so the laboratory applies a higher upper limit for women over 70, and 24 mm/h falls inside it. The under-50 limit does not apply to this patient, and a value inside the interval is not critical or a sign of a faulty tube. Choosing the interval that matches age and sex keeps the flags meaningful.
  11. The correct answer is B (Monocytic, because fluoride inhibits nonspecific esterase in monocytic cells but not in other lineages): Nonspecific esterase is strongly positive in monocytic cells, and sodium fluoride suppresses that activity. Positivity that disappears with fluoride therefore supports monocytic lineage. Megakaryocytes and some T cells can show esterase, but it is not fluoride inhibited.
  12. The correct answer is A (Reject it and request recollection, because hemolysis can activate clotting and disturb optical detection): Hemolysis releases cell contents that can trigger coagulation and the pink colour disturbs optical clot readings, so the result cannot be trusted. Spinning again does not remove hemoglobin already dissolved in plasma, and dilution would change clotting factor concentrations. A fresh, carefully collected specimen is the only reliable fix.
  13. The correct answer is A (The median cubital, because it is well anchored and lies away from major nerves): The median cubital is usually large, close to the surface and held in place by connective tissue, and it is separated from the brachial artery and median nerve. The basilic vein lies next to the brachial artery and median nerve, so it carries a higher risk of injury and is used only when the others cannot be accessed. The cephalic vein is the next-best site, since it lies lateral and away from the artery.
  14. The correct answer is D (Cold activates platelets and impairs later aggregation responses, so storage stays at room temperature): Refrigeration causes platelet shape change and activation, which distorts the aggregation tracings and can blunt the response to agonists. Samples are held at room temperature and tested within about 4 hours. The other statements describe changes that cold does not cause.
  15. The correct answer is B (0.405 L/L, because MCV multiplied by RBC and divided by 1000 gives the volume fraction): Hematocrit (L/L) equals MCV (fL) x RBC (x10^12/L) divided by 1000, so 90 x 4.50 / 1000 = 0.405 L/L. Dividing MCV by RBC gives 20, not a fraction. The other values do not follow from the relationship between volume per cell and cell number.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Recollect in citrate, recount, and report the corrected value with a clump comment): Clumps trap platelets so the analyzer undercounts, and a citrate sample often prevents EDTA-induced clumping so a valid count can be obtained. The count is then reported with a comment explaining the original issue. Releasing 42 could delay the procedure unnecessarily, and warming does not reliably reverse platelet clumping.
  2. The correct answer is A (Risk rises below 1.0 x10^9/L and peaks below 0.5, especially if neutropenia persists for several days): The risk of serious bacterial infection rises as neutrophils fall below 1.0 x10^9/L and is highest below 0.5, with duration adding to the risk. Neutrophils are the key defence against bacteria, so the lymphocyte count and fever alone do not define the risk. Waiting until the count reaches 0.1 would delay protective measures.
  3. The correct answer is D (17.7 percent, which exceeds the 15 percent limit and suggests that an inhibitor is present): The index is (41 - 30) / 62 x 100, which is 17.7 percent, above the 15 percent cut-off, so an inhibitor is suggested. The other values come from using the wrong numerator or denominator. Only the formula with the patient time in the denominator matches the laboratory definition.
  4. The correct answer is B (Perform the add-on, record the original collection time, and note the request time on the order): At 20 hours refrigerated the specimen is inside the 24-hour limit, so the add-on is acceptable. Records must show the true collection time and when the add-on was requested so reviewers can judge stability later. Calling it a new collection would misstate the specimen's age, and skipping documentation breaks the audit trail.
  5. The correct answer is B (Document the clerical error, correct the entry process, and confirm patient reports were unaffected): The analyzer printout shows the true measured value agreed with the peer target, so the failure arose from a clerical step, not from analytical performance. The laboratory must still record the investigation, fix the entry process, such as a second-person check, and confirm that patient reporting was never affected. Recalibration or mass repeat testing is not justified when the instrument result was correct.
  6. The correct answer is A (Examine a carefully collected second specimen, because in vivo hemolysis recurs while artefact does not): Hemolysis in the circulation will show up again in a new, well collected sample, while a collection artefact will not repeat. Her diagnosis makes in vivo hemolysis likely but does not prove it for this tube. Respinning cannot remove dissolved hemoglobin, and refusing to interpret ignores a clinically useful finding.
  7. The correct answer is D (Verify the fax number against a trusted directory, because a misdirected fax is a privacy breach): Sending to an unverified destination risks disclosing results to the wrong party, and the damage cannot be undone by a later call. A cover sheet does not remove the laboratory's duty to confirm the recipient. Personal email is an unsecured channel that adds further risk.
  8. The correct answer is B (Platelet satellitism, an EDTA-dependent artifact that falsely lowers the count and needs a citrate recount): Platelets rimming neutrophils is satellitism, an in vitro reaction in EDTA that traps platelets around white cells so the analyzer undercounts them. Because the patient has no bleeding, the count is spurious and a citrate recollection gives a true value. Phagocytosis in sepsis is rare, and giant platelets or toxic granulation would look different on the film.
Case CHB8

A 9-year-old child awaiting tonsillectomy has a prothrombin time of 12.2 seconds, within its reference interval, and an aPTT of 64 seconds with a reference interval of 25 to 37 seconds. The technologist performs a mixing study with pooled normal plasma in a 1:1 ratio. The immediate mixed aPTT is 33 seconds, and after a two hour incubation at 37 degrees Celsius it is 35 seconds. The child has no bleeding history.

  1. The correct answer is A (Factor deficiency, because normal plasma supplied the missing factor and restored clotting): A true factor deficiency corrects when pooled normal plasma supplies at least 30 to 50 percent of the missing factor, and here the aPTT fell from 64 to 33 seconds and stayed corrected at 35 seconds after incubation. Inhibitors and lupus anticoagulant leave the mixed result prolonged, so they do not fit.
  2. The correct answer is B (Factors VIII, IX, XI and XII, because the aPTT depends on these intrinsic factors): A prolonged aPTT with a normal PT localizes the defect to the intrinsic pathway, so assays for factors VIII, IX, XI and XII are the logical next step. Factors II, V, VII and X are measured by the PT, and fibrinogen and factor XIII do not cause an isolated long aPTT with a normal PT.
  3. The correct answer is C (A positive Bethesda titer for factor VIII, because lupus anticoagulant does not neutralize that factor): A Bethesda titer measures neutralizing activity directed against a specific factor, here factor VIII, and a specific inhibitor typically strengthens with incubation. Lupus anticoagulant instead interferes with phospholipid-dependent reagents, and its effect is reduced when extra phospholipid is added. The dilute Russell viper venom time is a lupus anticoagulant test, not a test for factor-specific antibodies.
  4. The correct answer is B (210 x10^9/L, using the mean of both sides multiplied by 100 and divided by 0.1): The mean of the two sides is 210 platelets in 1 mm2 at 0.1 mm depth, a volume of 0.1 uL. Platelets per uL equal 210 x 100 / 0.1, which is 210,000 per uL or 210 x10^9/L. Adding the sides, or using the wrong volume, gives values off by factors of two or ten.
  5. The correct answer is B (The manual differential, because analyzers cannot reliably classify abnormal lymphoid cells and misplace them): Analyzers classify cells by size and scatter patterns, so cleaved atypical lymphoid cells are often counted as monocytes or other cells. A manual differential lets the technologist identify and report the abnormal cells accurately, so it replaces the automated differential when the two conflict. Averaging two unlike methods or relying on the known diagnosis would release an inaccurate report.
  6. The correct answer is C (Antibodies to platelet factor 4 and heparin complexes activate platelets and promote thrombin generation): Anti-PF4 and heparin antibodies bind platelet Fc receptors and activate platelets, which release procoagulant material and generate thrombin. This produces clotting even as platelets are consumed. Heparin does not consume antithrombin to cause this, large VWF multimers describe TTP, and protein C depletion belongs to warfarin-induced skin necrosis.
  7. The correct answer is B (Below the cut-off, because 220 ng/mL DDU converts to about 440 ng/mL FEU by doubling): FEU values are roughly double DDU values, so 220 ng/mL DDU is about 440 ng/mL FEU, which is under the 500 ng/mL FEU cut-off. Halving or tripling the number applies the wrong conversion, and treating the two units as identical ignores the fibrinogen equivalent scaling.
  8. The correct answer is A (Full name and date of birth, because the patient states both and staff match the requisition): Two person-specific identifiers such as full name plus date of birth or health card number must match between the patient and the requisition. Appointment time, physician name and site address are not unique to the patient. The patient should state the details, not just agree with them.
  9. The correct answer is B (Arrange a venipuncture by a skilled collector, because the line is a contamination source): The aPTT is more than doubled compared with venipuncture values on the same stable heparin rate, which suggests the line sample is contaminated with heparin. Drawing from a nutrition lumen is inappropriate because it interrupts feeding and raises infection risk. A small discard from the heparin lumen does not clear the contamination, and releasing the value would mislead dosing. A skilled venipuncture gives a trustworthy sample.
  10. The correct answer is C (Dilute the plasma and repeat, because excess antigen saturates antibodies and falsely lowers the signal): With antigen excess, too many D-dimer molecules saturate the antibody sites and the signal falls, so a very high value can read falsely low. Diluting the plasma brings the concentration back into the working range and exposes the true result. Recentrifuging or freezing does not change the antigen to antibody ratio.
  11. The correct answer is B (Adjust the chair and microscope height so the eyepieces meet her eyes, neck straight): Strain falls when the workstation is adjusted to the worker, with eyepieces at eye level, a neutral neck and supported forearms. Leaning toward the eyepieces or stretching the arms increases static load on the neck and shoulders. Long sessions without breaks add fatigue, and regular short breaks are advised.
  12. The correct answer is C (Reject it and request recollection, because a specimen of unverified identity cannot be reported safely): A specimen whose identity cannot be verified by two identifiers is rejected and recollected when it can be replaced, as a CBC can. Completing a label in the laboratory or adding a comment does not prove the tube came from that patient. Holding the tube for days risks cell deterioration and still leaves the identity unverified.
  13. The correct answer is A (Report the confirmed positive to the regional public health authority within the legislated timeframe): Reportable disease rules are set provincially, and laboratories that confirm a reportable organism notify the regional public health authority within the stated timeframe. Telling the physician does not replace that duty, and provinces pass information to federal bodies rather than laboratories reporting directly. Waiting for species typing would delay a time-limited notification.
  14. The correct answer is A (Telephone the low count urgently, because marrow output has stopped while destruction continues): A near-absent reticulocyte count in a child who normally compensates means the marrow has temporarily shut down, a dangerous crisis because red cells are still being destroyed. The result needs urgent communication, not routine reporting. Waiting for a transfusion delays care, and a low count does not mean that hemolysis has stopped.
  15. The correct answer is B (Possible sickle cell disease, reported as an abnormal screen needing prompt confirmatory testing and referral): In a newborn, hemoglobin F is expected, and a normal pattern is FA. FS with no A means the baby has inherited S without a normal beta chain gene, which is the pattern of sickle cell disease or sickle beta zero thalassemia. Trait would appear as FAS, and confirmatory testing with prompt referral is needed rather than waiting.
  16. The correct answer is A (Add one drop of oil on the film, because the 100x lens works only in oil): The 100x objective is designed to work with a drop of immersion oil between the film and the front lens, so oil is added when the nosepiece is partway round and the lens is then clicked into it. Lowering the lens onto the slide can crack it and the lens, and oil on the 40x lens does not give the same detail as a true immersion objective.
  17. The correct answer is C (Calibrate or verify as the manufacturer requires, because an optical component change can shift results): A lamp in the photometer path directly affects hemoglobin absorbance, so after replacement the manufacturer's procedure for calibration or calibration verification must be followed. Controls at limited levels may not reveal a proportional shift across the range. Counting channels use impedance or flow cytometry, so the lamp does not affect them.
  18. The correct answer is D (Stop fighting, close the door, evacuate, and tell responders where the fire is): Once the extinguisher is empty and the fire is growing, the fire is beyond the incipient stage, so the safe decision is to stop fighting, close the door to slow spread and evacuate while telling responders its location. Going back to attack risks smoke inhalation, and water on a solvent fire can spread burning liquid. Moving bottles exposes the worker to the fire.
  19. The correct answer is C (Report it with a comment that acute clotting and heparin can lower antithrombin, so retest later): Acute thrombosis consumes antithrombin and heparin therapy lowers its activity, so a value of 68 percent three days into treatment is unreliable for diagnosing inherited deficiency. A comment and retesting after the acute phase is the correct approach. Protamine is not a valid correction for this result, and a value below the reference interval is not normal.
  20. The correct answer is C (Gentle inversion three to four times right after collection, to mix citrate with the blood): Citrate only works if it is mixed into the blood right away, so three or four gentle inversions after collection prevent partial clotting. Vigorous shaking activates platelets and damages cells, while refrigeration can activate factor VII and platelets. Adding citrate later cannot undo clotting that has already begun.
  21. The correct answer is B (Smudge cells, fragile lymphocytes broken in spreading, reduced by mixing albumin into the blood): The ruptured nuclei among small mature lymphocytes are smudge cells, fragile lymphocytes that break when the film is spread. Mixing a small amount of albumin with the blood before making the film strengthens the cells and reduces smudging. Stain precipitate looks granular, aged neutrophils would not predominate, and reactive lymphocytes are intact large cells.
  22. The correct answer is C (Call the nurse with the corrected value, obtain read-back, issue a corrected report, document both): The nurse may already be acting on the wrong critical value, so the correction must be telephoned with read-back, followed by a corrected report. Both the original and the corrected calls are documented to keep the record honest. Deleting the first record or relying on the chart would hide the error and delay the correction.
  23. The correct answer is A (Postanalytical, prevented by interfacing the analyzer so results transfer directly, because keying invites errors): A wrong number typed after the analyzer produced a correct result is a postanalytical error, because it happens during result entry and reporting. Electronic transfer from the instrument removes manual keying and so removes this error source. The instrument result was right, so collection, handling and calibration were not the cause.
  24. The correct answer is D (Day 7, when the INR reached 3.4 and became the first point above the 3.0 limit): The INR values rise steadily as vitamin K dependent factors fall, and the plotted point of 3.4 on day 7 is the earliest above the 3.0 upper limit. Day 6 at 2.8 is still within the band. The peak of 3.9 on day 8 is higher but is not the initial crossing.
  25. The correct answer is B (0.42 L/L, because 42 percent equals 42 hundredths of a litre per litre): A percent is a part per hundred, so 42 percent is 0.42 as a fraction, written in L/L as the volume of red cells per litre of blood. The other values are off by factors of 10 or 100. Canadian reports show hematocrit in L/L rather than percent.
  26. The correct answer is D (Chilling activates platelets and factor VII, which can distort clotting times and factor results): Citrated whole blood for routine coagulation tests should travel at room temperature because cold exposure activates platelets and factor VII, changing clotting times. Cold does not stop citrate from binding calcium, does not freeze plasma in an ice bath, and does not lyse red cells in this way.
  27. The correct answer is C (Reactive cells vary in size and shape, while lymphoma cells tend to look alike): Reactive lymphocytes show a spectrum of sizes, nuclear shapes and cytoplasmic basophilia in the same field, which reflects a polyclonal response. Lymphoma cells are a clone, so they tend to be monotonous. Scalloping around neighbouring red cells is a feature of reactive cells rather than a rule for lymphoma, and age does not separate the two.
  28. The correct answer is C (12.5 x10^9/L, because 22.5 is multiplied by 100 and divided by 180): With 80 nRBC per 100 WBC the divisor is 180, so 22.5 x 100 / 180 equals 12.5 x10^9/L. Subtracting 80 percent or multiplying by 0.8 removes far too much or too little, and multiplying by 1.8 raises the count instead of lowering it. The correction lowers the reported WBC whenever nRBCs are present.
  29. The correct answer is B (INR is 1.6, because the 1.25 ratio is squared when the ISI is 2.0): The ratio is 15.0 divided by 12.0, which equals 1.25, and the INR is the ratio raised to the ISI. With an ISI of 2.0 this is 1.25 squared, or 1.5625, reported as 1.6. Using the ratio alone, multiplying by the ISI, or inverting the ratio each misses the exponent step.
  30. The correct answer is B (Discard the exposed reagent, load fresh vials, and repeat controls before testing any patient): Reagent held beyond its stated stability is not reliable, and the prolonged controls confirm the effect. It must be replaced and controls repeated before any patient results are reported. Changing the ISI or mixing old and new reagent would disguise a real fault.
  31. The correct answer is D (K2EDTA carryover raised potassium, so recollect in correct order rather than report the result): Potassium EDTA contributes potassium and chelates calcium, so carryover into a green tube falsely raises potassium. Normal renal function and no hemolysis make a true value unlikely given the documented sequence. Rerunning or diluting the same tube cannot remove the contaminant, so a fresh collection in the correct order is required.
  32. The correct answer is D (Repeat testing 12 or more weeks later, because acute illness and inflammation cause transient positives): A positive lupus anticoagulant result must be persistent to support antiphospholipid syndrome, so international guidance asks for a repeat at least 12 weeks apart. Acute illness and a high CRP can cause transient positives. A two-day recheck is too early, and the lupus anticoagulant result is not simply an artifact.
  33. The correct answer is A (It is hemoglobin D or G, because S would have separated from the A control): On acid citrate agar, hemoglobin S moves away from A, while D and G migrate with A. A patient band lying with the A control therefore rules out sickle hemoglobin and points to D or G. Hemoglobin C does not share the S position at alkaline pH.
  34. The correct answer is B (It follows analyzer stability with real patient results, so slow drift between runs becomes visible): A running average of patient MCV uses the stable population mean as a target, so a gradual shift in reagent or calibration appears even when control material between runs looks acceptable. It supplements control material and does not replace it. It cannot identify a single mislabelled specimen, and it does not set calibration limits.
  35. The correct answer is B (Decline the trip, disclose the offer to the manager, because it creates a conflict of interest): A personal benefit offered by a vendor during a purchasing decision is a conflict of interest, and the correct response is to decline and disclose. Abstaining or promising to judge fairly does not remove the appearance of influence. Keeping the offer secret makes the conflict worse.
  36. The correct answer is D (Mechanical shock from rapid acceleration and impact in the carrier, which ruptured fragile red cells): Loose tubes bump against the carrier and each other when the system accelerates and brakes, and this shock bursts fragile red cells. Pneumatic carriers are not refrigerated, EDTA does not leak from a capped tube, and light exposure of a few seconds does not turn hemoglobin pink.
  37. The correct answer is D (About 0.33 mL, because the smaller plasma fraction in the tube needs proportionally less citrate): With a hematocrit of 60 percent the term (100 minus 60) is 40, so the volume is 1.85 x 10^-3 x 4.5 x 40, which is 0.333 mL. The standard 0.5 mL suits a normal hematocrit and would leave too much citrate for the small plasma volume. Adding more citrate for extra cells reverses the logic of the adjustment.
  38. The correct answer is C (Hepatitis B vaccine, because the virus spreads by blood and vaccination gives lasting protection): Hepatitis B is the bloodborne virus for which an effective vaccine exists, and employers offer it to staff who may be exposed to blood. There is no licensed vaccine against hepatitis C or HIV, so safe handling and exposure response remain the main defence for those. Measles spreads by the respiratory route, not through prepared blood films.
  39. The correct answer is B (Registration requires ongoing demonstration of competence, because knowledge and technology change after the entry exam): Provincial colleges require members to keep their knowledge and skills current as part of maintaining registration, which protects the public as methods and instruments change. The program is not a punishment, not a source of revenue, and not waived by an employer's own training.
  40. The correct answer is B (Replace the stain and buffer and restain, because contaminated reagents deposit organisms on slides): Organisms spread across the whole slide, outside cells and in more than one patient's slides, point to contaminated stain, buffer or rinse water rather than infection. Replacing the reagents and restaining confirms this. Phagocytosed bacteria inside white cells, not background organisms, would justify an urgent call.
  41. The correct answer is D (20 mL of fetal whole blood, because 0.4 percent of 5000 mL gives that volume): Eight fetal cells in 2000 cells is 0.4 percent. Multiplying 0.4 percent by 5000 mL of maternal blood gives 20 mL of fetal whole blood. A misplaced decimal gives 2 mL or 200 mL, and the cell count itself is not a volume.
  42. The correct answer is A (Draw a discard tube first, because the tubing air would otherwise underfill the citrate): The tubing of a butterfly set holds air that takes up the first part of the vacuum draw, so a citrate tube filled straight away would be short drawn and the ratio would fail. A plain discard tube absorbs that air volume. Saline would dilute the sample, and an additive tube would contaminate the citrate.
  43. The correct answer is C (Plus 2.3 SD, which lies beyond the two SD warning limit but inside three SD): The difference from the mean is 147 minus 140, which is 7 g/L, and dividing by the standard deviation of 3 g/L gives plus 2.3 SD. That point is past the two SD warning limit but under three SD. Dividing by the variance or the mean does not give a value in standard deviation units.
  44. The correct answer is C (It falsely raises the ESR as cells slide along the wall, so the test is repeated): Tilting a sedimentation tube speeds the fall of red cells along the lower wall, which falsely increases the ESR. A correction factor is not validated, so the specimen must be set up again in a truly vertical rack. Reading at a different time breaks the standard one-hour method.
  45. The correct answer is D (Recollect into a tube with citrate volume reduced for the high hematocrit, then repeat): With a hematocrit above about 0.55 L/L there is less plasma in a fixed 9 to 1 blood to citrate mix, so excess citrate binds the calcium added by the reagent and falsely prolongs clotting times. The tube must be recollected with citrate volume reduced for the hematocrit. Adding citrate worsens the imbalance, and changing analyzers does not fix a preanalytical error.
  46. The correct answer is B (Adjust the block, let it stabilize at 37 C, and recheck before incubating any samples): The measured temperature is 1.4 C below target, outside the 1 C tolerance, so the block must be corrected and rechecked on the calibrated thermometer before use. Extending the time does not reliably compensate for a low temperature. Logging the dial setting instead of the measured value falsifies the record.
  47. The correct answer is D (Return it promptly to the refrigerator and discard it at the manufacturer's stated open-vial limit): Stabilized control cells deteriorate at room temperature and once exposed to air, so vials are returned to 2 to 8 C right after use and discarded at the stated open-vial limit. Freezing damages the cells and creates false variation. Leaving vials at room temperature shortens their useful life.
  48. The correct answer is A (Rerun at 1 in 5 and halve the result, since the curve uses 1 in 10): Results below the lowest calibrator are not reliable, so the sample is rerun with less dilution to bring the clotting time onto the curve. A 1 in 5 dilution contains twice as much plasma as a 1 in 10 dilution, so the reading is divided by 2. More dilution would push the clotting time further off the curve, and doubling the value reverses the correction.
  49. The correct answer is B (Coarse basophilic stippling present, suggest lead poisoning be considered, because stippling is precipitated ribosomal RNA): Coarse basophilic stippling is precipitated ribosomal RNA, and lead exposure disrupts the enzymes that clear RNA and heme synthesis, so lead should be raised for follow-up. Howell-Jolly bodies are single DNA fragments, Pappenheimer bodies are iron granules, and malaria rings have a pale center.
  50. The correct answer is A (Reject the tube, comment that it is underfilled, and request a recollected full-draw sample): Coagulation tubes depend on a fixed ratio of nine parts blood to one part citrate. A tube filled to less than half its volume holds excess citrate, which binds the calcium added by the reagent and falsely prolongs clotting times. Moving the blood cannot correct the ratio, so recollection is the only safe step.
  51. The correct answer is B (Decline until approval exists, because identifiable data cannot be used for unapproved research): Using identifiable health information for research requires approval from a research ethics board and proper authority first. A health card number is a direct identifier, so removing names alone does not de-identify the data. Personal email is not a secure channel for patient records.
  52. The correct answer is A (Confirm the balance is level, zero it, and handle the weights with forceps while comparing): A daily check begins with confirming the balance is level and zeroed, then certified weights are placed with forceps so skin oils do not change their mass. A tared pan does not correct for an uneven bench. A coin has no certified mass and cannot verify the balance.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Granulocytes across the full maturation range with a myelocyte peak, basophilia and few blasts): Chronic phase CML shows the whole spectrum of granulocyte maturation with a myelocyte and neutrophil peak, absolute basophilia and often a raised platelet count, while blasts are fewer than 10 percent. A maturation gap with abundant blasts points to acute leukemia, and smudge cells with small lymphocytes point to CLL.
  2. The correct answer is C (86 percent, below the criterion, so remedial training and reassessment follow): Agreement is 43 divided by 50, which is 0.86 or 86 percent, and that is below the 90 percent criterion. The technologist therefore needs remedial training and a repeat assessment. The 14 percent and 43 percent values come from using the disagreement count or the raw number instead of a percentage.
  3. The correct answer is A (Investigate the consistent positive bias, including calibration and calibrator traceability, before it exceeds the limit): Three surveys in a row with results close to the limit and in the same direction show a systematic positive bias, even though each single result passed. Controls may have target values that hide the bias, so calibration and calibrator traceability should be reviewed before a survey is failed. Applying a fixed correction to patient results or changing provider does not address the cause.
  4. The correct answer is A (Politely decline both, thank the resident warmly and report the offer to a supervisor): Accepting a costly gift or a social invitation from a patient she cares for blurs the professional relationship. Declining kindly and informing a supervisor protects both people. Accepting the dinner or the watch, openly or in secret, creates a boundary problem.
  5. The correct answer is C (About one result in twenty falls beyond two SD, so an occasional value is expected): In a Gaussian distribution about 95 percent of results fall within two standard deviations, so roughly one in twenty falls outside by chance. A single value at plus 2.4 is therefore a warning to look at other rules, not proof of failure. Failure rates of one in a thousand or one in three do not match the 95 percent coverage.
  6. The correct answer is C (Remove it before leaving the laboratory, because contaminated clothing must stay in the work area): Laboratory coats are removed and left in the work area so that contamination is not carried into eating areas or patient care units. A coat can hold infectious material that cannot be seen, so looking clean proves nothing. Folding or covering the coat does not change the fact that it has been worn at the open bench.
  7. The correct answer is B (156 x10^9/L, because multiplying 140 by 10/9 corrects for the one part of citrate): Citrate is a liquid, so the blood is diluted by one part in ten. The corrected count is the analyzer result multiplied by 10/9: 140 x 10/9 = 155.6, reported as 156 x10^9/L. Multiplying by 9/10 moves the answer the wrong way, and leaving the value unchanged ignores the dilution.
  8. The correct answer is D (Escalate to the supervisor or operator to reach a responsible caregiver and document attempts): A critical result must reach a responsible caregiver within the policy time, so failed calls trigger escalation through the supervisor or hospital operator, and every attempt is recorded. A routine report or voice message does not confirm that anyone acted. Waiting passively risks a missed deadline.
  9. The correct answer is A (Microcytic cells, because the patient curve peaks at a smaller volume than the normal curve): The horizontal axis shows cell volume in fL, and the patient curve peaks well to the left of the normal reference, so the cells are smaller than normal. The curve is a single smooth peak, which argues against two populations. A wide base without a shift would indicate anisocytosis rather than microcytosis.
  10. The correct answer is B (Request a recollection with a larger needle, because in vitro hemolysis distorts hematocrit and MCHC): The pink-red colour shows that red cells burst after collection, which here followed a forceful pull through a narrow needle. Lysed cells lower the measured red cell count and hematocrit while the free hemoglobin is still measured, so the MCHC of 393 g/L is impossibly high. The sample cannot be rescued by calculation or dilution and should be recollected with a larger needle and gentler technique.
  11. The correct answer is A (Blot with lens paper, then wipe with fresh paper moistened with approved lens cleaner): Oil is blotted first so it is not spread across the lens, and a lightly moistened lens paper then lifts the remaining film. Paper towels and gauze have coarse fibres that scratch lens coatings, and tap water leaves mineral deposits. Oil left on a dry objective can seep inside the barrel and attract dust.
  12. The correct answer is C (Flag it high (H), because 22 mm/h exceeds the upper limit of 20 mm/h): The report flag follows the laboratory's own interval for the patient's age and sex, and 22 is above 20 mm/h. Closeness to the limit does not remove the need for a flag. A critical call is reserved for values on the critical list, and ESR rises rather than falls with age.
  13. The correct answer is A (A raised MCHC with high reticulocytes and a normal MCV suggests spherocytic hemolysis): Spherocytes lack central pallor and have a high hemoglobin concentration, so the MCHC rises while the MCV stays near normal. A reticulocyte count of 12.5 percent shows the marrow is responding to red cell loss or destruction. The film review and DAT separate immune hemolysis from other causes, while iron deficiency or B12 deficiency would not give this combination.
  14. The correct answer is C (Splenic macrophages pit out a Heinz body together with a small piece of membrane): As red cells pass through the spleen, macrophages remove the attached Heinz bodies and take a bite of membrane with them, leaving a bite cell. The cell does not burst around the inclusion, and neutrophil ingestion occurs in immune settings. Nuclear pitting removes Howell-Jolly bodies, not denatured hemoglobin.
  15. The correct answer is B (Confirm one CBC is intended with the ward, then run it once, because duplicates waste blood): A duplicate request on a small child wastes limited blood, so the laboratory checks with the ordering unit and then performs a single test. Running it twice adds no value, and deleting or cancelling a request without telling anyone leaves a gap in the record.
  16. The correct answer is D (DIC consumes factors and fibrinogen, so normal tests favour a platelet process like TTP): DIC consumes platelets, factors and fibrinogen, so fully normal PT, aPTT and fibrinogen with schistocytes point toward a platelet-centred microangiopathy such as TTP. The film cannot be dismissed, because schistocytes also occur in DIC and other conditions. A count of 25 does not exclude DIC by itself, but the normal screening tests argue against it.
  17. The correct answer is B (Provide an adjustable chair with a footrest, or a height adjustable workstation, for her): Workstations are fitted to the worker, so a short technologist needs an adjustable chair with a footrest or an adjustable-height station so the eyepieces meet her eyes. Hunching or lifting the shoulders only moves strain to the neck and shoulders, and raising the microscope further worsens the mismatch. Cutting her viewing time ignores the fixable cause.
  18. The correct answer is B (Arrange orientation, supervised practice and competency sign-off on the new analyzer and policies before independent work): A long absence combined with new equipment and policy means competence must be re-established and documented before she works unsupervised. Self-assessment cannot replace observed sign-off, and delaying training until the annual review leaves her practising on an instrument she has not been trained on.
  19. The correct answer is C (Reject and recollect, because storage swells cells, raises MCV and degrades morphology and counts): After about a day at room temperature red cells swell, so the MCV rises, and white cells and platelets degrade. The changes match storage artefact, not a new patient condition, so the numbers and film cannot be trusted. Remixing cannot reverse cell damage, and calling macrocytosis would mislead the physician.
  20. The correct answer is A (Hereditary spherocytosis, because the high MCHC, spherocytes and reticulocytosis fit with a negative antiglobulin test): A negative DAT with small dense cells, a high MCHC and reticulocytosis fits hereditary spherocytosis, where membrane loss produces spherocytes without antibody. Warm autoimmune hemolysis would usually have a positive antiglobulin test. Iron deficiency has a low MCHC, and megaloblastic anemia gives macrocytes without spherocytes.
  21. The correct answer is D (Quality management and technical competence requirements that show a laboratory produces reliable results): ISO 15189 sets out quality management and technical requirements for medical laboratories, and provincial accreditation programs assess against it. It does not set pay, instrument manufacturing, or billing, which are handled by employers, manufacturers and health ministries.
  22. The correct answer is B (2.3, because the ratio of 2.0 raised to the power 1.2 is about 2.3): INR equals the patient PT divided by the mean normal PT, raised to the power of the ISI: 2.0 to the 1.2 power is about 2.3. Using the ratio alone ignores reagent sensitivity, multiplying by the ISI overstates the result and dividing by it understates the result.
  23. The correct answer is B (Anemia of inflammation, with high ferritin and low TIBC marking iron held in stores): A high ferritin, low serum iron, low TIBC and elevated CRP indicate that inflammation (through hepcidin) is trapping iron in macrophages. In iron deficiency the ferritin would be low and the TIBC raised. Transferrin saturation of 16 percent is low, which excludes iron overload.
  24. The correct answer is C (Spherocytes have less membrane for their volume, so they burst after taking in little water): A spherocyte has a low surface area to volume ratio, so only a small influx of water brings it to its bursting point. It therefore lyses at a higher saline concentration than a biconcave cell. Target cells have extra membrane and resist lysis, and heparin does not damage red cells at the usual concentration.
  25. The correct answer is D (Clots trap platelets and cells, so counts are falsely low and the specimen cannot be corrected): Clotting removes platelets, white cells and red cells from the liquid blood, so every count is falsely low and cannot be repaired. Removing the clot does not return the trapped cells. The whole specimen is unsuitable, not just the platelet count.
  26. The correct answer is D (1.30, screen ratio over confirm ratio, which exceeds 1.20 and supports a lupus anticoagulant): The screen ratio is 58 / 35 = 1.66 and the confirm ratio is 42 / 33 = 1.27, so the normalized ratio is 1.66 / 1.27 = 1.30. That exceeds 1.20 and supports a lupus anticoagulant. The screen ratio alone is not normalized, and inverting the ratios or mixing patient values gives meaningless numbers.
  27. The correct answer is B (Keep it at 4 C and inspect daily for seven days, because cold drives precipitation): Cryoproteins precipitate in the cold and redissolve on warming, so the serum is refrigerated near 4 C and checked each day for a gel or flocculent precipitate. Keeping it warm would hide a positive result, and freezing or a one hour stand is not the accepted observation method.
  28. The correct answer is C (ISI rates reagent sensitivity against an international reference, so values near 1.0 respond best): The International Sensitivity Index compares a thromboplastin with the international reference preparation, so a value near 1.0 means the reagent responds to vitamin K antagonist effect like the reference. It is used in the INR calculation and has nothing to do with shelf life, calcium or the normal time, which each laboratory determines for itself.
  29. The correct answer is A (Report with a comment that rivaroxaban can cause a falsely positive screening result): Rivaroxaban inhibits factor Xa and can make lupus anticoagulant screening tests falsely positive, so the report needs a comment about this interference. Citrated plasma is the correct specimen, and a prolonged time alone does not prove an antibody is present. Calling the result negative would ignore the known effect of the drug on the clotting time.
  30. The correct answer is C (Factor VII, because its short half-life of about 6 hours means levels fall first): Factor VII has a half-life of roughly 6 hours, the shortest of the vitamin K dependent factors, so its activity drops within a day and the PT lengthens early. Factor II has a half-life near 60 hours, which is why the full antithrombotic effect takes days. Factors IX and X have intermediate half-lives.
  31. The correct answer is C (Telephone the physician with the blast finding and document it, because acute leukemia is urgent): A new finding of blasts with Auer rods and severe cytopenias in a febrile patient is a critical result, so the physician must be told by telephone and the call documented under the laboratory critical value policy. Waiting for morning or sending the slide away delays urgent care. Repeating a sample is not needed when the film itself shows the blasts.
  32. The correct answer is D (Insert the STAT sample at priority now, since the analyzer allows it without interruption): A priority insertion lets the STAT sample be tested without disturbing the running batch, and testing it now protects the 30 minute result target. Waiting for a 12 minute batch would consume a large share of that target. Aborting the batch or switching to a manual method adds risk of error and rework without any need.
  33. The correct answer is B (Warm the specimen to 37 C, mix well, and repeat the CBC to disperse the clumps): Cold agglutinins clump red cells at room temperature, so the analyzer counts clumps as single cells. This lowers the RBC count and hematocrit and falsely raises MCV and MCHC. Warming the sample to 37 C disperses the clumps and allows a valid count, whereas chilling would worsen the agglutination.
  34. The correct answer is D (The coverslip is too thin or unseated, so the 0.1 mm depth cannot be trusted): Newton's rings show that an optically flat, thick coverslip is held firmly against the chamber shoulders, which fixes the depth at 0.1 mm. Without them the coverslip may be flexing or too thin, so volume and count become unreliable. Dilution and leakage are separate problems that the missing rings do not indicate.
  35. The correct answer is C (Hold the result, enter the new lot's ISI and mean normal time, and recalculate): The INR depends on the ISI and mean normal time of the lot actually in use, so wrong values give a wrong INR even when the reagent itself clots plasma correctly. The result must be held and recalculated with the correct constants before anything is reported. A fresh draw or a physician call would act on a number that is mathematically wrong.
  36. The correct answer is D (By provincial legislation and accreditation requirements, written into a laboratory retention policy): Retention times come from provincial law and the accreditation program, and the laboratory writes them into a policy so staff apply them consistently. Convenience, vendor advice, or reagent expiry dates do not set legal or accreditation record retention.
  37. The correct answer is B (Prolonged aPTT with normal PT and INR, repeat advised, because the comment only states findings): The aPTT is clearly above the stated interval while the PT and INR are normal, so the comment should state that pattern and suggest repeat testing. An isolated prolonged aPTT has several causes, including factor deficiency, inhibitors and heparin, so naming one is not supported. Calling the panel normal ignores the abnormal aPTT.
  38.
