The correct answer is D (108 fL, macrocytic, because the hematocrit multiplied by 1000 is divided by the RBC count): MCV in fL equals hematocrit in L/L times 1000 divided by the RBC count in x10^12/L, so 280 divided by 2.60 gives about 108 fL. A value above 100 fL is macrocytic. Dividing the RBC count by the hematocrit gives the wrong ratio, and dividing hemoglobin by the hematocrit gives the MCHC, not the MCV.
  39. The correct answer is A (Acid releases toxic hydrogen cyanide gas from the reagent, so cyanide waste must be kept separate): Cyanide salts react with acids to release hydrogen cyanide gas, which is highly toxic, so cyanide-containing waste must be collected separately and handled under the laboratory's WHMIS and waste procedures. Acid does not make the waste safe for the drain. Ferricyanide is not a fire hazard in this setting, and clogging is not the concern.
  40. The correct answer is D (Pink-salmon red cells, purple-blue nuclei, lilac granules and stained platelets, because dyes are balanced): A well-balanced Romanowsky stain gives pink-salmon red cells, purple-blue nuclei with visible chromatin, lilac neutrophil granules and purple platelet granules. Intense dark colour with precipitate, blue-green red cells or pink nuclei all point to a problem with pH, timing or reagent quality.
  41. The correct answer is B (Centrifuge the diluted lysate and read the clear supernatant, because white cell turbidity raises absorbance): A very high white cell count leaves nuclear debris and cell fragments that scatter light, adding apparent absorbance and raising both hemoglobin and MCHC. Spinning the lysate and reading the supernatant removes the turbidity. Reporting the value, halving the dilution or warming the sample does not remove the particulate interference.
  42. The correct answer is B (Hold the result and contact the collector to verify identity and recollect): A hemoglobin rise of 56 g/L with no transfusion and an MCV change from 96 to 79 fL is physiologically implausible, so the sample most likely belongs to another patient. The result must be held while identity is verified and a new specimen is collected. Releasing with a flag or repeating the same tube cannot fix a wrong-patient sample.
  43. The correct answer is A (Ferricyanide converts hemoglobin to methemoglobin, then cyanide forms stable cyanmethemoglobin read near 540 nm): Ferricyanide oxidizes the iron of hemoglobin from the ferrous to the ferric state, producing methemoglobin, and cyanide combines with it to form cyanmethemoglobin, which is stable and absorbs near 540 nm. Reduction, direct oxyhemoglobin measurement and heme splitting are not steps of the reference method. Almost all hemoglobin forms except sulfhemoglobin are converted.
  44. The correct answer is B (A CO2 or ABC extinguisher, which does not conduct, aimed at the base and swept across): Electrical equipment fires need a non-conductive agent, so carbon dioxide or ABC dry chemical is used, aimed at the base of the flames with a sweeping motion. Water and foam conduct electricity and risk shock. A class D extinguisher is made for combustible metals and is not suited to equipment fires.
  45. The correct answer is D (Refer the request to the privacy officer, because research release needs ethics approval and authority): Provincial privacy law allows disclosure for research only with approval from a research ethics board and under defined conditions, which the privacy officer checks. Health card numbers are identifiers, so removing names alone does not de-identify the data. A supervisor's instruction or the researcher's institution does not replace that lawful authority.
  46. The correct answer is A (Excess plasma proteins precipitate in the buffer and cloud the mixture, independent of any hemoglobin S): In whole-blood solubility reagents, high levels of plasma protein, lipid or very high cell counts can produce turbidity that is not caused by hemoglobin S, which is why a turbid result should be confirmed by electrophoresis or HPLC. Electrophoresis showing only hemoglobin A proves no S is present. Excess dithionite or reticulocytes do not cause this pattern.
  47. The correct answer is B (Run the cleaning cycle and repeat the background count before analyzing any patient sample): The WBC background exceeds the laboratory limit, which points to contamination or debris in the fluidics. The analyzer should be cleaned and the background repeated until all parameters pass. Applying a subtraction or ignoring one failed parameter would put inaccurate counts on patient reports.
  48. The correct answer is C (Switch off, let the rotor stop, and keep the lid closed about 30 minutes before opening): A broken tube releases aerosols of infectious material, so the unit is switched off and left closed so the aerosols settle before anyone opens it. Opening promptly or restarting would expose staff and spread contamination. Cleanup follows with gloves, eye protection and approved disinfectant.
  49. The correct answer is D (Hemoglobin 126 g/L and hematocrit 0.38 L/L, with the unit changes noted on the report): Hemoglobin in g/dL is multiplied by 10 to give g/L, so 12.6 g/dL is 126 g/L. Hematocrit as a percent is divided by 100 to give a fraction, so 38 percent is 0.38 L/L. The other entries misplace a decimal or leave one value unconverted.
  50. The correct answer is B (Hemolysis signals clotting activation and optical interference, so a recollected sample is needed): Traumatic collection with hemolysis raises concern that clotting was activated in the tube, and free hemoglobin also absorbs light on optical systems. A recollected sample gives a trustworthy result. Pink plasma does not come from anemia or from citrate, and a small shortening does not make the result safe to report.
  51. The correct answer is D (The buffer was too acidic, because low pH favours eosin binding over methylene blue): An acidic buffer shifts staining toward eosin, so red cells and eosinophil granules look intensely red while nuclei stay pale and blue-gray. An alkaline buffer causes the opposite picture, with blue red cells. Prolonged fixation or unfiltered stain does not produce this specific red-shifted colour balance.
  52. The correct answer is C (A normal thrombin time, because dabigatran directly inhibits thrombin and prolongs this test very sensitively): Dabigatran directly inhibits thrombin, and the thrombin time is extremely sensitive to it, so a normal thrombin time effectively excludes a clinically relevant level. The PT and aPTT respond variably and non-linearly. Fibrinogen synthesis is not suppressed by dabigatran.
  53. The correct answer is C (Warm the sample to 37 C, remix and repeat the CBC, then report that result): Cold agglutinins clump red cells in the cool tube, so the analyzer undercounts them, giving a low red cell count, a high MCV and a high MCHC. Warming the sample to 37 C reverses the clumping, and the repeat gives valid indices. Reporting the first run, or calling a critical anemia, would give the clinician a false picture.
  54. The correct answer is A (A red cell with two or more blue granules or filaments of reticulum): A reticulocyte shows at least two blue granules or a filamentous network of precipitated RNA within the cell. A single dense round body suggests a Howell-Jolly body, pale blue-green membrane bodies suggest Heinz bodies, and refractile dots on top of cells are stain precipitate. None of these are counted.
  55. The correct answer is B (Show it as 98 g/L, with the source laboratory and original units noted): One g/dL equals 10 g/L, so 9.8 g/dL becomes 98 g/L. Showing it in the same unit as the local result prevents a physician from reading a trend between 9.8 and 104. Noting the source and original unit keeps the record traceable.
  56. The correct answer is C (Explain the short wait for the female technologist and let the patient choose): Respecting a religious preference is part of patient centred care when it can be met safely. A twenty-minute wait still fits the thirty-minute result window, so the patient should hear the options and decide. Overriding the request, demanding paperwork or using a visitor skips the patient's own choice.
  57. The correct answer is C (Section 4, first aid measures, which describes immediate care steps for each route of exposure): The standard safety data sheet places first aid measures in Section 4, organized by eye, skin, inhalation and swallowing. Section 2 summarizes the hazards and Section 8 covers exposure controls and personal protection. Section 14 deals with transport, so none of those gives the immediate treatment steps.
  58. The correct answer is A (Re-examine the thin monolayer zone of the film, because crowding distorts apparent size variation): Cells in the thick area overlap and flatten, so they look more variable than they are, and the normal RDW-CV and MCV suggest the apparent anisocytosis is a reading artifact. The assessment must be made in the monolayer where cells barely touch. Reporting the crowded-area impression or dropping the finding without re-reading would both be unsupported.
  59. The correct answer is C (A near miss, which is still entered in the incident system so causes can be reviewed): A near miss is an error that is caught before it reaches or harms the patient, and it is recorded because it reveals weaknesses in the process. It is not an adverse event since no harm occurred, and it does not need disclosure to the patient. Leaving it unrecorded would remove the chance to prevent a later error that is not caught.
  60. The correct answer is A (Lupus anticoagulant, because phospholipid-dependent antibodies are neutralized by excess phospholipid in the reagent): Failure to correct on mixing indicates an inhibitor rather than a deficiency, and shortening with high phospholipid points to an antibody that depends on phospholipid, the lupus anticoagulant pattern. Lupus anticoagulant is associated with thrombosis rather than bleeding, matching the history. A factor VIII inhibitor would not shorten with extra phospholipid.
  61. The correct answer is D (Reject the short sample and request recollection, because excess citrate prolongs clotting times falsely): In an underfilled citrate tube the fixed volume of citrate is in excess for the blood present, which binds extra calcium during testing and falsely prolongs clotting times. Because a stable outpatient can be recollected, the short sample is rejected. Pooling tubes or adding saline alters the sample and does not restore the correct blood to anticoagulant ratio.
  62. The correct answer is C (The supravital stain precipitates residual RNA in young red cells as a blue network of strands): New methylene blue is a supravital stain: it precipitates the residual ribosomal RNA of young red cells into a visible reticulum, which is why they are called reticulocytes. Mature red cells contain no RNA, so they stay unstained. Hemoglobin content and nuclei are not what this stain shows, and fibrin does not form blue strands.
  63. The correct answer is D (Hemoglobin D or G, both comigrate with S but separate from it on acid citrate agar): On cellulose acetate at alkaline pH, hemoglobins D and G travel with S, so a band at that position is only presumptive for sickle hemoglobin. Citrate agar at acid pH separates S from D and G. Hemoglobin C migrates with A2, E and O at alkaline pH, and fetal hemoglobin runs between the A and S positions.
  64. The correct answer is C (The 90 percent standard is met, though the 37 minute late result needs review): Thirty-six of 40 is 90 percent, which equals the stated minimum, so the standard is met. The received time of 08:42 and released time of 09:19 give 37 minutes, which is outside the 30 minute limit. A late STAT result should still be reviewed to find the cause, even when the overall target is met.
  65. The correct answer is B (Notify the physician that EDTA carryover is possible and request urgent recollection in correct order): Carryover of EDTA into a citrate tube chelates calcium and can falsely prolong clotting tests, and the very high potassium supports contamination from the wrong order of draw. Because suspected APL makes timely and accurate coagulation results critical, the physician must be told and the specimens recollected in the CLSI order. Reporting as drawn, adding calcium or diluting the plasma are not acceptable ways to rescue a contaminated tube.
  66. The correct answer is C (Test the bleeding trauma patient first, because acuity ranks requests within the STAT group): Within a group of STAT requests, the patient in the most immediate danger is tested first, and active bleeding with low blood pressure outranks a stable outpatient. Arrival order is a tiebreaker only when acuity is equal. Both tubes run on the same analyzer one after the other, so the sequence must be chosen deliberately.
  67. The correct answer is D (Draw a plain discard tube first, because tubing air would underfill the light blue citrate tube): The air in the butterfly tubing takes up vacuum, so the first tube drawn can fill short. A plain discard tube is drawn first so the citrate tube fills to the correct 9 to 1 ratio. Holding the tube above the needle risks backflow, and EDTA before citrate would contaminate the sample.
  68. The correct answer is A (Time stamps linked to instrument, reagent lot, QC status and operator, to reconstruct the run): Traceability means linking each result to the instrument, reagent lot, QC status at the time, and the person who ran it, with time stamps. Invoices and contracts show purchasing, not the state of the run. A clinician's copy or recollection cannot reconstruct the analytical conditions.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Report 3+ target cells, because 14 per field falls within the laboratory's 11 to 20 band): The stated policy places 11 to 20 cells per field in the 3+ band, and 14 sits squarely inside it. Grades are assigned by the band a count falls in, not by which boundary it is nearer. Choosing a lower or higher grade would misstate how many cells the film actually contains.
  2. The correct answer is D (Warm to room temperature and mix by gentle inversion, since cold settled cells vary): Control cells settle and are viscous when cold, so the vial is brought to room temperature and mixed gently by inversion or rolling to resuspend them evenly. Vigorous mixing can damage the cells and add bubbles. Diluting or running cold gives values that differ from the assigned range.
  3. The correct answer is D (An oxidizer that can feed a fire, so store it apart from flammable solvents): The flame over a circle pictogram identifies oxidizers, which release oxygen and make fires burn more fiercely. They are stored away from flammable and combustible materials, not inside the flammable cabinet with them. The plain flame, corrosion and gas cylinder pictograms mean different hazards.
  4. The correct answer is B (Acute promyelocytic leukemia, because abnormal promyelocytes carry granules and Auer rod bundles): Faggot cells are abnormal promyelocytes with multiple Auer rods and dense granules and are the hallmark of acute promyelocytic leukemia. Lymphoblasts and CLL lymphocytes lack primary granules and Auer rods, and CML shows a spectrum of maturing granulocytes with basophilia rather than abnormal promyelocytes.
  5. The correct answer is C (2.0 percent, calculated as the standard deviation divided by the mean and multiplied by 100): The coefficient of variation is the standard deviation divided by the mean times 100, so 2.8 divided by 140 gives 0.02, which is 2.0 percent. Inverting the ratio or changing the multiplier gives values that do not describe relative imprecision. A lower CV means better precision.
  6. The correct answer is A (Cell morphology deteriorates in EDTA, so smears are best made within a few hours): In EDTA, neutrophils develop vacuoles, lobe changes and degeneration within hours, while monocytes and lymphocytes also change, so smears for differentials should be made promptly or from a fresh sample. The tube here sat for about ten hours before the film was made. EDTA is the right anticoagulant for morphology and is not toxic within an hour, and venous tubes are not protected from the change.
  7. The correct answer is B (Iron in mitochondria around the nucleus, which defines ring sideroblasts seen in myelodysplasia): Prussian blue stains iron, so the ring of blue granules shows iron-loaded mitochondria encircling the nucleus of an erythroid precursor, a ring sideroblast. Ring sideroblasts are associated with some myelodysplastic syndromes. Carbohydrate and lipid are not stained by Prussian blue, and the granules here lie within precursors rather than free outside cells.
  8. The correct answer is D (Depleted iron stores with raised binding capacity, because the liver makes more transferrin in deficiency): A low ferritin, low serum iron, high TIBC and low transferrin saturation form the typical iron deficiency pattern. TIBC rises because the liver increases transferrin when iron is scarce. Inflammation would show a normal or high ferritin and a low TIBC.
  9. The correct answer is A (Index of 11, which is below 13 and therefore favours thalassemia trait over iron deficiency): The Mentzer index is 62 / 5.6 = 11.1, and a value below 13 favours thalassemia trait because the RBC count is high for the degree of microcytosis. In iron deficiency the RBC count is low and the index is above 13. Multiplying MCV by RBC or dividing hemoglobin by RBC does not give the Mentzer index.
Case CHB9

A 71-year-old surgical inpatient has a CBC, ESR and PT/INR ordered for 0730. At 1215 the laboratory receives two lavender tubes and one light blue tube in one bag, all kept at room temperature. One lavender tube carries the patient's full name and hospital number. The other two tubes show only the surname and room number. Policy allows ESR for four hours at room temperature.

  1. The correct answer is A (Two unique identifiers, such as full name and number, because rooms hold different patients over time): Two unique identifiers, such as full name and a hospital number, are needed on the tube itself. A room number can belong to another patient the next day, so it does not identify anyone. Ward, test name and volume are not patient identifiers.
  2. The correct answer is C (Run the CBC and reject the ESR, because the tube passed the four hour ESR limit): The tube was collected at 0730 and is 4 hours 45 minutes old at 1215, so it exceeds the four hour room temperature limit for ESR. A CBC remains acceptable on EDTA blood for many hours. A policy limit is a rule, not a suggestion, and the ESR is the more time sensitive test.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reject both, notify the ward for recollection and document the reason, because identity is unverified): Unlabelled or partly labelled tubes cannot be proven to belong to this patient, and relabelling after the fact removes the safeguard. The ward must be told so that a new specimen is collected properly, and the rejection reason is documented. Citrate and EDTA tubes both need two identifiers.
  4. The correct answer is A (32 percent blasts, giving an absolute blast count of 14.4 x10^9/L, from 32 percent of 45): Blast percent is 64 divided by 200, or 32 percent, and the absolute blast count is 32 percent of 45 x10^9/L, which is 14.4 x10^9/L. Using the raw tally ignores the 200-cell denominator, and shifting the decimal or repeating the percentage gives the wrong absolute value. Over 20 percent blasts meets the usual threshold for acute leukemia.
  5. The correct answer is B (Request urgent recollection in a full tube, because excess citrate prolongs clotting times): Underfilling leaves too much citrate relative to blood, which binds more calcium than intended and artificially prolongs the PT and aPTT. Topping up from another tube or running only some tests does not restore the correct 9 to 1 ratio. Reporting with a comment would breach the fill policy and could lead to wrong treatment of a bleeding patient.
  6. The correct answer is C (Heparin explains the long thrombin time, because fibrinogen amount and fibrin formation look normal): Reptilase acts on fibrinogen without being inhibited by heparin, so a normal reptilase time shows fibrinogen quantity and fibrin formation are acceptable. The prolonged thrombin time is then best explained by heparin. A dysfibrinogenemia or very low fibrinogen would prolong the reptilase time as well, and fibrin degradation products would affect both.
  7. The correct answer is D (Finish the count, report blast-like cells, and refer the film, because blasts need urgent review): Large cells with nucleoli, fine chromatin and a high nuclear to cytoplasmic ratio in a cytopenic patient are suspicious for blasts, so the differential is completed and the cells are named as blast-like. The film is then referred to the pathologist or senior technologist under laboratory policy. Calling them reactive lymphocytes or rerunning the analyzer would delay a possibly urgent finding.
  8. The correct answer is B (4.3 x10^9/L, calculated as 86 x 20 divided by a counted volume of 0.4 uL): The four large squares cover 4 mm2, and at 0.1 mm depth they hold 0.4 uL. Cells per uL equal 86 x 20 / 0.4 = 4300, which is 4.3 x10^9/L. Using a wrong counted volume shifts the answer by a factor of four or ten.
  9. The correct answer is B (0.24 x10^9/L, which is severe neutropenia because it is below 0.5 x10^9/L): The absolute neutrophil count is the total WBC multiplied by the combined percentage of segmented neutrophils and bands: 2.4 x 10 percent equals 0.24 x10^9/L. Below 0.5 x10^9/L the neutropenia is severe, and the patient is at high risk of infection. Using only the segmented cells, or the lymphocyte percentage, gives wrong values.
  10. The correct answer is A (Verification with controls and retained patient samples, and documentation of the repair): After a repair that could alter sampling, the laboratory verifies performance with QC and compares retained patient results before reporting, and records the work. Powering on or running calibration alone does not prove accurate patient results. Administrative approval does not replace verification.
  11. The correct answer is C (Check for clots and recollect, because squeezing and slow flow can falsely lower platelet counts): Neonatal platelet counts normally fall within about 150 to 400 x10^9/L, so 62 x10^9/L is unexpected, yet every other result is in range. Slow flow, squeezing and a lack of mixing allow platelets to clump or clot, which produces a falsely low count. The right step is to look for clots and recollect, not to report a critical value or apply a correction.
  12. The correct answer is C (Platelet-rich microthrombi shear red cells, because ADAMTS13 deficiency leaves ultra-large von Willebrand factor multimers intact): In TTP, deficient ADAMTS13 leaves ultra-large von Willebrand factor multimers that bind platelets and form microthrombi in small vessels. Red cells are sheared as they pass through these platelet-rich strands, giving fragments. Fibrin strands belong to DIC, spherocytes belong to antibody-mediated hemolysis, and sickling needs abnormal hemoglobin.
  13. The correct answer is A (Describe what was learned, how it changes her practice, and her plan to apply it): Colleges expect reflective practice, meaning what was learned and how it will change work, not simply proof of attendance. Copying a handout or listing the presenter shows nothing about the technologist's own learning or application.
  14. The correct answer is A (Periodically, such as annually, and sooner after a long absence or a method or instrument change): Competency must be reassessed at defined intervals, commonly each year, and sooner when circumstances change, such as a long leave or a new instrument or method. Reassessing only at hire or after errors leaves skills unverified for long periods.
  15. The correct answer is D (Report left shift with toxic changes, and refer the film for review per policy): A count of 38.0 x10^9/L with toxic granulation, Dohle bodies and a graded left shift matches a leukemoid reaction to infection. Basophils and eosinophils are not raised, which argues against chronic myeloid leukemia, though a technologist cannot make that call alone. The film should be referred for review under laboratory policy, and no nucleated red cells are described that would justify a correction.
  16. The correct answer is B (Request a new citrate collection and recount, because EDTA clumping falsely lowers the count): Large clumps at the feathered edge, a clump flag and a well patient mean the low count is an artifact of the EDTA tube. A citrate or fresh recollection gives a count that can be reported. A comment does not replace a valid count, and releasing 22 as critical would alarm without cause.
  17. The correct answer is B (Reject it and request a recollection, because cold storage activates factor VII and alters results): Whole blood for coagulation testing should be kept at room temperature, because cold activates factor VII and platelets and distorts the PT and aPTT. Rewarming does not reverse that activation, and the two tests are not affected in a way that allows one to be safely reported. A fresh specimen avoids a misleading pre-surgical result.
  18. The correct answer is D (Compare patient samples on a second analyzer, because the new diluent may change red cell volume): A rising patient average with acceptable controls suggests a problem that affects patient samples differently from control material, such as a diluent that changes red cell size. Running retained patient samples on a second analyzer separates a true shift from a population change without altering anything. Recalibrating to the patient average, continuing to report, or discarding controls would not address the cause.
  19. The correct answer is C (RPI is 1.6, below 2, so the marrow response is inadequate for this anemia): First correct for anemia: 8.0 x 0.18 / 0.45 equals 3.2 percent. Then divide by the maturation time of 2.0 days, which gives an RPI of 1.6. An RPI below 2 means the marrow is not increasing output enough, despite a high raw reticulocyte percentage of 8.0, so the cause may include a hypoproliferative component.
  20. The correct answer is A (Both people sign the custody form with the date and time of transfer): Every transfer of a legal specimen needs signatures from the person giving and the person receiving it, plus the date and time. Without both entries a gap appears in the record that a court could use to question the evidence. An unattended tube, a changed label or an unsigned transfer all break the documented chain.
  21. The correct answer is C (Reagents differ in phospholipid and activator, so each reagent and analyzer needs its own interval): The activator type and phospholipid composition change how fast plasma clots, so each reagent and analyzer combination needs a locally verified interval. Ellagic acid reagents are not uniformly longer, and the interval is established by the laboratory rather than set by a regulator or copied from an insert.
  22. The correct answer is C (Decline to alter the value without evidence and refer the concern to the supervisor): A reported result must not be altered without evidence, and a suspected clump needs a documented investigation on a recollected specimen. Declining and informing the supervisor protects the integrity of the record. Changing the number, even with a comment, or deleting the result breaks that integrity.
  23. The correct answer is D (It is probably reactive to iron deficiency, because the low hemoglobin and MCV explain the rise): Iron deficiency is a well known cause of reactive thrombocytosis, and the low hemoglobin, MCV of 68 fL and pencil cells point to it. A myeloproliferative neoplasm is considered after reactive causes are excluded, so a platelet count above 450 x10^9/L alone does not establish a clone. Treating iron deficiency usually brings the platelet count back down.
  24. The correct answer is C (Declining RBC count with a clear supernatant fits a traumatic tap rather than subarachnoid hemorrhage): In a traumatic tap the blood comes from the needle track, so RBC counts fall from the earliest tube collected to the latest and the supernatant stays clear. In subarachnoid hemorrhage the counts stay similar across tubes and the supernatant is often xanthochromic after some hours. A clear supernatant does not indicate old blood, and the pattern gives no reason to suspect mislabelling.
  25. The correct answer is A (85.7 fL, from hematocrit times 1000 divided by the red cell count in x10^12/L): MCV (fL) = hematocrit (L/L) x 1000 / RBC (x10^12/L), so 0.30 x 1000 / 3.50 = 85.7 fL. Inverting the division gives 11.7, and the other values use the wrong scaling factor. A value of 85.7 fL is normocytic.
  26. The correct answer is C (Respin the transferred plasma in plastic without the brake, because platelets must be removed): A braked stop stirs the buffy coat and leaves too many platelets, so the plasma must be transferred carefully to a plastic tube and spun again with the brake off. A clear appearance is not a valid check, freezing does not remove platelets, and respinning the original tube disturbs the layer again.
  27. The correct answer is D (Precise but inaccurate, since results cluster tightly but sit consistently away from the true value): Precision describes how closely repeated results agree, and these ten results agree within 2 g/L. Accuracy describes closeness to the true value, and a mean near 152 is 12 g/L above the target of 140. The method is therefore precise but biased. Agreement between replicates alone does not prove accuracy.
  28. The correct answer is A (Discard the partial results, restart per procedure, pass QC, then rerun the interrupted tubes): A power loss during a run makes the interrupted results unreliable, so partial prints are discarded. After the restart, QC must pass before patient testing, and the interrupted tubes are rerun. Releasing printed values or skipping QC after an interruption risks reporting invalid results.
  29. The correct answer is C (Add 1.8 mL of buffer, because 0.2 mL in 2.0 mL total gives 1 in 10): A 1 in 10 dilution means one part plasma in ten parts total, so 0.2 mL of plasma needs a final volume of 2.0 mL. The buffer volume is therefore 2.0 minus 0.2, or 1.8 mL. Adding ten volumes of buffer would give a 1 in 11 dilution, and the other volumes do not reach ten parts.
  30. The correct answer is C (It falsely raises the ESR, because cells slide along the wall, so repeat the test vertically): A tube that is not vertical allows red cells to settle along the lower wall faster, which falsely increases the ESR, and even a slight tilt of about three degrees can raise results noticeably. The test must be restarted with a vertical tube because a correction factor cannot be applied. Reading the tilted tube or applying a subtraction would release an unreliable result.
  31. The correct answer is B (Debris was counted as platelets, because bright-field optics poorly separate it from true platelets): Under bright-field optics, platelets are small and pale, and debris or stain precipitate looks very similar, so non-platelet particles inflate the count. Phase contrast makes platelets refractile and easier to separate from dirt, and a filtered diluent reduces particles. The film agreeing with the automated count points to the manual result as the outlier.
  32. The correct answer is C (Notify the hematologist promptly, because hemoglobin, WBC and platelets all meet the stated criteria): Hemoglobin 74 g/L, WBC 1.8 x10^9/L and platelets 19 x10^9/L each fall below the defined limits at the same time, so the pancytopenia trigger is met. The policy does not depend on finding blasts, and printed flags do not replace notification. A clear policy trigger does not wait for a repeat unless the specimen is in doubt.
  33. The correct answer is A (8 percent, calculated as serum iron divided by TIBC times 100): Transferrin saturation is serum iron divided by TIBC: 6 / 72 x 100 = 8.3 percent, which is low. Inverting the ratio gives 12, and the unbound capacity gives 92 percent. A misplaced decimal gives 83 percent.
  34. The correct answer is B (Date, time, result given, name and role of the person notified, and read-back confirmation): Accreditation under ISO 15189 and provincial programs expects the record to show when the call happened, what was communicated, who received it and that a read-back was done. Omitting the recipient or the read-back leaves the call unable to be audited. The patient's diagnosis is not part of the critical call record.
  35. The correct answer is C (Water absorbed into the methanol, because moisture disturbs the cell membranes during fixation): Methanol readily absorbs moisture from humid air, and water in the fixative produces refractile, ring-like artifacts in fixed red cells. Replacing the fixative and keeping the jar tightly capped prevents this. Buffer pH and spreader speed affect colour and film shape, not the refractile rings that appear after fixation.
  36. The correct answer is D (Nucleated red cells or lysis-resistant red cells, because both are counted as small white cells): Nucleated red cells and red cells that resist lysis remain small after the lytic step, so they are counted just below the lymphocytes and falsely raise the WBC. Hypersegmented neutrophils and reactive lymphocytes are not smaller than normal lymphocytes, and eosinophils are counted with the granulocytes.
  37. The correct answer is B (0.65 x10^9/L, moderate neutropenia, because segmented neutrophils and bands are added together): Absolute neutrophil count includes segmented neutrophils and bands, so 25 percent of 2.6 gives 0.65 x10^9/L, which lies in the moderate grade of 0.5 to 1.0. Using segmented neutrophils alone gives 0.47 and wrongly places the patient in the severe grade. Using the total count ignores the differential entirely.
  38. The correct answer is B (Perform a saline replacement dilution and rerun, because saline disperses rouleaux that distort cell counting): Rouleaux can be counted as single large particles by the analyzer, giving a falsely high MCV and a falsely low RBC count. Diluting the sample in saline and rerunning disperses the stacks so the cells are counted individually. Warming is the remedy for cold agglutinins, not rouleaux, and neither reporting the artifact nor recalibrating without evidence of drift addresses the cause.
  39. The correct answer is C (Make films within a few hours, because prolonged EDTA contact causes crenation and vacuolated neutrophils): EDTA changes cell membranes over time, producing crenated red cells, vacuoles in neutrophils and other artefacts that can be mistaken for disease. Films should therefore be made soon after collection. Stored cells do not stain better, and venous EDTA blood is the normal specimen for films.
  40. The correct answer is A (Centrifuge twice, moving plasma by plastic pipette between spins, because one spin leaves platelets): A single spin typically leaves enough platelets to give residual counts well above 10 x10^9/L. A second spin of plasma transferred to a clean plastic tube removes most of the remainder. Filtering can activate factors and freezing releases platelet phospholipid that interferes with the assay.
  41. The correct answer is A (Limiting access and logging entries shows that no one could have altered or swapped the specimen): Locked and logged storage limits who can reach the tube and records each access. This lets the laboratory show later that nobody altered, swapped or contaminated the specimen. Storage temperature and anticoagulant stability are the same for a legal tube as for any routine one, so those reasons do not explain the cabinet.
  42. The correct answer is B (Stop releasing male reports and notify the supervisor so the table error is corrected and reported): A wrong reference interval on released reports is a systemic reporting error that can hide abnormal results, so reporting is paused and the supervisor is informed so the table can be fixed and affected reports corrected. Flagging a few results by hand does not address the other reports or the cause. Delaying correction leaves incorrect reports in patient charts.
  43. The correct answer is B (Telephone the ordering clinician now with a preliminary parasite finding, because malaria is urgent): Intracellular ring forms with double chromatin dots in a febrile traveller are a preliminary positive for malaria, which can progress rapidly, so the clinician must be told by phone. Species confirmation can follow but should not delay the first call. Overlying platelets lie on the cell surface rather than inside it.
  44. The correct answer is C (Reject it and recollect, because platelet factor 4 neutralizes heparin in standing blood): Platelets in uncentrifuged blood release platelet factor 4, which neutralizes heparin and falsely shortens the aPTT. The delay makes the result unreliable, so a new sample is needed. Acting on the short value could lead to a dangerous infusion increase, and a second spin or protamine cannot undo the neutralization.
  45. The correct answer is D (8 nRBC per 100 WBC with a corrected WBC of 11.1 x10^9/L, from 12.0 x 100/108): The corrected count removes the nucleated red cells that the analyzer counted as leukocytes, using WBC x 100 / (100 + nRBC). Here 12.0 x 100 / 108 gives 11.1 x10^9/L. Multiplying by 108/100 would raise the count in the wrong direction, and leaving the value unchanged ignores the laboratory rule.
  46. The correct answer is A (The outer box must allow gas to vent and show the dry ice mark): Dry ice sublimates into carbon dioxide gas, which can burst a sealed box, so the outer package must permit gas release and carry the dry ice marking and shipping information. Dry ice is regulated as a dangerous good in its own right, and it is kept outside the primary and secondary containers. Airtight sealing creates an explosion risk.
  47. The correct answer is C (Tubes in a sealed bag with absorbent, then padded inside the rigid outer box): Triple packaging uses a leakproof primary container, a sealed secondary container with enough absorbent material to take up all the liquid, and a rigid outer package, with the contents prevented from moving. Loose tubes in a box, a bag alone, or paper towel do not provide the leak containment and cushioning the system requires.
  48. The correct answer is B (Twenty individuals, accepting when two or fewer fall outside, because transference only needs checking): Transference of a published interval is checked with about twenty healthy people, and the interval is accepted when no more than two of them fall outside the limits. A full establishment study needs about 120 individuals per partition, which is far more work than verification requires. Matching only the mean does not show that the limits fit the local population.
  49. The correct answer is D (Decline to alter it, explain the film findings, and offer a repeat on a new sample): A result may be changed only when the data support the change, never on request or signature. The film excludes clumping, so the value stands, but a respectful explanation and an offer to repeat on a fresh sample address the physician's concern. Ending the conversation abandons the clinical team.
  50. The correct answer is A (Telephone the fibrinogen and the prolonged clotting times together as urgent, since bleeding is active): A fibrinogen of 0.9 g/L is below the critical limit, and with a prolonged PT and aPTT in a bleeding patient it points to a serious coagulation failure. The tube is acceptable, so no repeat is needed before the call. Delaying for repeat testing, assuming that bleeding is expected, or waiting for a pathologist all put the patient at risk.
  51. The correct answer is D (Telephone it as critical and refer the film for review, since blasts need prompt follow-up): A white count of 112 x10^9/L is far above the critical limit, and blast-like cells make it urgent. The result is telephoned and the film goes for review as policy directs. Dilution only addresses linearity of the count, and a very high count is not evidence of a contaminated tube.
  52. The correct answer is A (Prussian blue iron stain, because Pappenheimer bodies contain iron that stains blue with acid ferrocyanide): Pappenheimer bodies are iron-containing granules, often in siderocytes after splenectomy. They look like basophilic granules on a Wright film but turn blue with the Prussian blue reaction. RNA remnants and denatured hemoglobin are shown by supravital stains and are different inclusions.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Lipemia falsely raises hemoglobin, so replace the plasma with diluent and repeat the hemoglobin): Fat particles in a lipemic specimen scatter light in the hemoglobin channel, falsely raising hemoglobin, MCH and MCHC while the red cell count and hematocrit stay normal. Replacing the plasma with diluent and repeating the hemoglobin removes the interference. Cold agglutinins and hemolysis change the red cell count or hematocrit instead, and true polycythemia would not leave them normal.
  2. The correct answer is B (Helmet and triangular fragments, because fibrin strands in small vessels cut passing red cells apart): Fragmented helmet and triangular cells with low platelets and kidney injury indicate microangiopathic hemolysis, where fibrin strands in small vessels cut the red cells. Spherocytes and sickle cells come from membrane or hemoglobin defects and do not fit the platelet count and renal picture. Macro-ovalocytes with hypersegmented neutrophils belong to vitamin B12 or folate deficiency.
  3. The correct answer is C (Prolonged EDTA contact causes crenation and vacuoles, so late films show misleading red cell shapes): EDTA gradually changes cell membranes, producing crenated red cells, vacuolated neutrophils and distorted lymphocytes that can be mistaken for disease. A film made promptly shows true morphology. EDTA does not raise hemoglobin and platelets do not vanish from the tube.
  4. The correct answer is B (Ineffective hematopoiesis, because dysplastic precursors die in the marrow before they reach the blood): In myelodysplastic syndromes the marrow is often full of cells, but the dysplastic precursors are defective and many undergo apoptosis before release, so blood counts fall. Aplasia would give an empty, fatty marrow, which does not match the report. Antibody destruction and hemodilution do not produce a hypercellular, dysplastic marrow.
  5. The correct answer is D (220 x10^9/L with a comment on EDTA clumping, because the citrate result needs dilution correction): Sodium citrate dilutes blood by about one part in ten, so the citrate platelet count is multiplied by 1.1 to correct for it, giving 200 x 1.1 = 220 x10^9/L. The comment tells the clinician that EDTA caused pseudothrombocytopenia. Dividing by 1.1 corrects in the wrong direction, and the clumped EDTA count is falsely low.
  6. The correct answer is A (At 20:00 from the arm opposite the infusion, six hours after the change): The protocol sets the sample six hours after the change, which is 20:00. Drawing from the arm opposite the infusion avoids heparin contamination of the sample. Early draws at 15:00 or 18:00 are before steady state, and drawing through the catheter risks contamination even after a flush.
  7. The correct answer is B (Give a documented verbal handover covering the patient, pending tasks and recollection request, with read-back): A structured handover that names the patient, the pending work and the expected recollection prevents lost tasks, and read-back confirms the message was understood. Sticky notes and reliance on a system listing both omit context such as the recollection. A name alone leaves the next technologist to rediscover the problem.
  8. The correct answer is C (Use a loose tourniquet, a small butterfly needle, and light anchoring, with no slapping): Fragile veins rupture under high pressure and large needles, so a light tourniquet or low-pressure cuff, a small butterfly set and gentle anchoring reduce trauma. Slapping or tapping bruises the skin and can break fragile vessels. A bruised area should be avoided because puncturing it risks more bleeding and gives poor results.
  9. The correct answer is A (Polychromatophilic reticulocytes holding residual RNA, confirmed by a supravital new methylene blue stain): Polychromatophilic cells are young red cells that still contain residual ribosomal RNA, which gives the blue-grey tint, and they are the same cells counted as reticulocytes with new methylene blue. Perls stain detects iron, basophilic stippling is a granular inclusion, and rouleaux describe stacking rather than colour.
  10. The correct answer is D (Introduce yourself, confirm identity with two identifiers, explain the test, and obtain agreement before proceeding): A respectful approach starts with an introduction, identification using two patient identifiers, a plain explanation of what will be done, and the patient's agreement before the tourniquet goes on. Using a room number is unsafe, explaining after the fact removes informed choice, and the nurse cannot consent on behalf of a capable adult.
  11. The correct answer is C (Use the alias identifiers from the wristband, exactly matched on the requisition and each tube): Unidentified emergency patients are registered under an alias with a wristband, and the same alias number must be on the requisition and every tube so results and transfusion samples stay traceable. Waiting for a real name could delay lifesaving care, and a guessed name risks a later identity mix-up. A blank name label has no unique identifier.
  12. The correct answer is C (Report 5.2 percent flagged high, because it exceeds the upper limit of 3.5 percent): The flag follows the interval stated for the laboratory: 5.2 percent lies above 3.5 percent, so it is flagged high. A raised A2 with a low MCV and normal ferritin is the classic pattern prompting beta thalassemia consideration, and iron deficiency would tend to lower A2 rather than raise it. DNA testing is a later step and does not delay reporting the measured fraction.
  13. The correct answer is D (251, because the total volume of 5.02 mL is divided by the 0.02 mL blood volume): The dilution factor equals total volume divided by sample volume. The total is 5.0 + 0.02 = 5.02 mL, and 5.02 / 0.02 = 251. Leaving out the blood volume gives 250, which is a near miss, and the other values come from wrong volume conversions.
  14. The correct answer is A (Linearity, meaning results stay proportional to true concentration across the stated reportable range): A dilution series tests whether the measured value changes in direct proportion to the expected value across the range, which is linearity. Precision needs repeated measurement of one specimen, carryover needs a high sample followed by a low one, and accuracy compares the mean with a true value.
  15. The correct answer is A (A reactive neutrophilia, because toxic changes with infection and no blasts favour a reactive process): Toxic granulation and Dohle bodies with a clear infection and no blasts or basophilia indicate a reactive neutrophilia. Chronic myeloid leukemia shows immature granulocytes and basophilia, and acute leukemia shows blasts. A raised white count alone is not diagnostic of any of them.
  16. The correct answer is D (Replace the container with the new lot, record the lot and date, and run a control): Reagents are replaced as whole containers so that each lot and open date can be traced. Mixing lots can alter performance and makes problems impossible to trace. A control run after a reagent change confirms that results are unaffected.
  17. The correct answer is A (It links platelet glycoprotein Ib to exposed collagen and carries and protects factor VIII): Von Willebrand factor bridges platelet GPIb to exposed subendothelial collagen and carries and protects factor VIII in plasma. Low activity therefore causes mucocutaneous bleeding and a prolonged closure time. Binding GPIIb-IIIa to fibrin, activating factor X and inhibiting thrombin describe other proteins.
  18. The correct answer is C (In the labelled flammable liquid cabinet, because its steel walls slow fire spread and hold spills): Flammable liquids are stored in an approved flammable liquid cabinet because its double walled steel body and self-closing doors slow fire spread and contain spills. A standard refrigerator has sparking parts and can ignite trapped vapour. Sink storage and open shelves leave the solvent exposed to heat and ignition sources, and flushing solvent to drains is not permitted.
  19. The correct answer is D (Warm the sample to 37 C and repeat, because clumped cells falsely lower the count): A very high MCHC and MCH with a low RBC count and hematocrit, but a plausible hemoglobin, points to cold agglutinin clumping that makes the analyzer count aggregates as single large cells. Warming the specimen disperses the clumps so the repeat count is reliable. Reporting the values as measured would mislead the physician, and heparin is not suitable for a CBC.
  20. The correct answer is B (Keep the blower on, cover with disinfectant, allow contact time, then lift glass with forceps): The blower stays on so contaminated air continues to be drawn through the HEPA filter, while disinfectant is applied and given contact time before glass is lifted with forceps into a sharps container. Switching off the blower or raising the sash releases aerosols into the room. Alcohol and sweeping spread blood and do not provide a reliable kill.
  21. The correct answer is B (Verify the result herself and phone the critical value, because verification belongs to the registered role): Verification and critical value notification are professional duties of the registered technologist and cannot be delegated to someone whose role excludes them. Delay endangers a patient with severe thrombocytopenia. Splitting the tasks so that notification precedes verification would also release unchecked information.
  22. The correct answer is A (4.87 x10^9/L, because segmented and band neutrophils together make 58 percent of 8.4): Absolute count equals the percent of the cell type times the white cell count divided by 100, and neutrophils include both segmented and band forms: 58 percent of 8.4 is 4.87 x10^9/L. Using segmented cells alone gives 4.37, and bands alone gives 0.50. Reporting a percentage as a count ignores the white cell total.
  23. The correct answer is C (Provide update training and reassess competency on new module before independent work): A long absence and a significant equipment change both call for retraining and a fresh competency check before the person works unsupervised. Staffing pressure, verbal briefings, or self-declared memory do not replace documented competence.
  24. The correct answer is B (Hemolysis falsely raises serum folate, so recollect a fresh non-hemolyzed sample before reporting): Red cells contain much more folate than serum, so a hemolyzed specimen releases folate and gives a falsely high result that can hide a true deficiency. A fresh sample is needed. Reporting the value would wrongly exclude folate deficiency.
  25. The correct answer is A (1.26 x10^9/L, because the percent is multiplied by the total count and divided by 100): The absolute count equals the total WBC multiplied by the percentage and divided by 100, so 9.0 x 14 / 100 gives 1.26 x10^9/L. Dividing the total by the percent, or leaving out the division by 100, gives values that are off by large factors. Adding the percent to the total has no meaning.
  26. The correct answer is A (Notify the clinicians promptly and issue corrected reports for both patients): When wrong results are already in the record, patient safety comes first: the clinicians are told and corrected reports are issued so care does not rely on false values. Root cause analysis and retraining follow, and waiting for questions leaves both patients at risk.
  27. The correct answer is B (10.0 x10^9/L, because the count is multiplied by 100 and divided by 140): Nucleated red cells are counted by the analyzer as white cells, so the white count is corrected with WBC x 100 / (100 + nRBC). Here 14.0 x 100 / 140 equals 10.0 x10^9/L. Multiplying by 1.4 raises the count when it should fall, and subtracting 40 percent removes far too much.
  28. The correct answer is D (To confirm detector sensitivity and laser alignment are stable so results compare day to day): Calibration beads have a fixed, known fluorescence, so their peak position shows whether detector gain, laser alignment and fluidics are performing as on previous days. They are not a patient sample, a cleaning agent or a test of antibody quality. A drifting peak means the instrument needs attention before patients are run.
  29. The correct answer is D (Count undiluted fluid in all nine squares, because clear fluid has few cells per microlitre): Clear CSF holds very few cells, so it is counted undiluted in all nine large squares and the total is corrected for the volume counted. Dilution would reduce an already low count to a meaningless number. Hyaluronidase is used for viscous synovial fluid, not CSF, and staining is used for differentials rather than chamber counts.
  30. The correct answer is A (IU/mL, measuring inhibition of factor Xa, because the result reflects drug activity itself): The chromogenic anti-Xa assay measures how much factor Xa is inhibited by heparin and antithrombin, and it is reported as heparin activity in IU/mL. It is not a clotting time and it is not a factor assay reported as a percent. Platelet antibody testing is a different investigation.
  31. The correct answer is C (Venom activates factor X directly, so intrinsic factor deficiencies rarely change the dRVVT result): Russell viper venom activates factor X directly, bypassing factors XII, XI, IX and VIII, and the reagent has a limited phospholipid content. A phospholipid-dependent inhibitor therefore prolongs the time clearly, while single intrinsic factor deficiencies do not. The venom needs calcium and phospholipid, so the other statements are wrong.
  32. The correct answer is A (Dilution by infusion fluid is likely, so recollect from the opposite arm before releasing results): Hemoglobin, white cells and platelets all fell by the same proportion while the cell indices stayed the same, which is the signature of dilution rather than a different patient, whose indices would differ. Saline in the collection arm explains it, so the specimen is recollected from the opposite arm. Calibration drift would not affect only this specimen, and early blood loss alone does not lower all three counts equally.
  33. The correct answer is C (Vitamin B12 or folate deficiency, because impaired DNA synthesis causes ineffective marrow production): A high MCV with macro-ovalocytes and hypersegmented neutrophils is the classic picture of megaloblastic anemia, and the same DNA synthesis defect can reduce white cells and platelets. Aplastic anemia usually has a normal or mildly high MCV without hypersegmentation, and iron deficiency or acute blood loss would not give macrocytosis with hypersegmented neutrophils.
  34. The correct answer is D (Move the stain to the flammable cabinet at low level, then separate the acid bottle): The flammable stain is near a heat source and is stored overhead, so it should be moved carefully to the flammable cabinet at a low height, and the acid then stored on its own tray. Sealed bottles can still fall, leak or feed a fire. Decanting into small jars at the bench adds exposure, and waiting for an audit leaves the hazard in place.
  35. The correct answer is C (Test the rule with documented patient and simulated cases and obtain approval before release): Accreditation standards expect each autoverification rule to be validated in the laboratory with documented test cases, including results that should be held, and to be approved before use. Quality control does not test patient decision rules, and vendor testing cannot cover local limits. Night shift use gives less oversight, not more.
  36. The correct answer is B (To verify the new strip lot and meter give results within the expected control range): Liquid control checks that the meter and the new strip lot perform as expected before patient testing begins. The control does not calibrate to a venous INR, represent a patient, or change the strip expiry date.
  37. The correct answer is B (Factor deficiency, because normal plasma supplied the missing factor and normalized the clotting time): Normal plasma contains about 100 percent of each factor, so a 1:1 mix gives at least 50 percent, which is enough to normalize the aPTT when a factor is simply deficient. An inhibitor would keep the mix prolonged. Fibrinogen deficiency would also lengthen the PT, which is normal here.
  38. The correct answer is D (It is not affected by lupus anticoagulant or high factor VIII, which distort clotting-based methods): The chromogenic method activates protein C with a snake venom activator and measures cleavage of a color substrate, so it does not depend on clotting. Lupus anticoagulant and raised factor VIII can distort the clotting-based aPTT method. The chromogenic assay measures activity, not antigen, and does require an activator.
  39. The correct answer is B (Let the lamp cool, unplug the microscope, and handle the new bulb with lint free cloth): A halogen bulb is very hot after use, so it must cool and the unit must be unplugged before changing it. Skin oils on a new bulb create hot spots that shorten its life, so it is handled with a lint free cloth. Working with power on risks shock, and moisture on a bulb is not appropriate.
  40. The correct answer is B (25 percent, which exceeds the 15 percent cut-off and therefore supports an inhibitor): The Rosner index is (50 minus 30) divided by 80, times 100, which is 25 percent. Because 25 is above the laboratory's 15 percent limit, the pattern supports an inhibitor rather than a simple factor deficiency. The other values come from dividing by the wrong quantity or subtracting in the wrong direction.
  41. The correct answer is D (In the monolayer where red cells just touch, because platelets are spread evenly there): Platelets are estimated in the monolayer, the zone where red cells lie side by side with little overlap, because distribution and staining there are most representative. The feathered edge and sides carry uneven numbers and platelet clumps, while the thick area hides platelets under overlapping cells.
  42. The correct answer is A (Fetal cells, because hemoglobin F resists elution by the acid buffer and keeps its stain): Adult hemoglobin is eluted from maternal cells by the acid buffer, leaving pale ghost cells. Hemoglobin F resists elution, so fetal cells keep the eosin stain and appear pink-red. Reticulocyte RNA and nuclear chromatin play no part in this resistance.
  43. The correct answer is D (Prolonged tourniquet time forced fluid out of vessels, concentrating cells and raising the measured hemoglobin): A tourniquet left on beyond about one minute raises venous pressure and pushes water and small molecules into the tissues. The cells and large proteins that stay behind become more concentrated, so hemoglobin and hematocrit read falsely high. The spleen does not empty into the arm because of a tourniquet, and extra EDTA concentration does not change the hemoglobin.
  44. The correct answer is D (Report 71 percent lymphocytes, 21 neutrophils and 8 mesothelial, with a note about low cellularity): When fewer than 100 nucleated cells are present, every cell is counted and each type is expressed as a percentage of the cells actually counted: 27 of 38 is 71 percent, 8 of 38 is 21 percent and 3 of 38 is 8 percent. A comment on low cellularity tells the reader the percentages rest on a small count. Treating raw counts as percentages or refusing to report ignores the information on the slide.
  45. The correct answer is A (Recollect into sodium citrate and recount, correcting by 1.1, because EDTA caused the clumps): EDTA-dependent clumping traps platelets in aggregates that the analyzer does not count as single platelets, giving a falsely low result. A citrate recollection prevents that clumping, and the result is multiplied by 1.1 because 9 parts blood to 1 part citrate dilutes the sample. Mixing the same EDTA tube again does not reverse the clumps.
  46. The correct answer is A (Move to the monolayer near the feathered edge, because spread cells show nuclear chromatin detail): In a thick area, cells overlap and do not flatten, so nuclei look dense and chromatin cannot be assessed. The monolayer near the feathered edge shows cells spread evenly, so nuclear shape, chromatin and nucleoli can be judged, and a new film can be made if no good area exists. Calling cells malignant or normal from a thick area, or staining longer, does not fix the spreading problem.
  47. The correct answer is B (Hold release and investigate calibration, because precision is acceptable but the bias exceeds the limit): Precision describes random error and is good here, but the comparison shows a systematic difference of 9 g/L, about 7.5 percent at 120 g/L, which is well beyond the 3 percent allowance. A method must satisfy both precision and accuracy before patient testing, so calibration and calibrator values need investigation. A fixed subtraction is not an accepted way to hide a bias in a new method.
  48. The correct answer is C (A supravital preparation of fresh unfixed cells, because Romanowsky stains do not show Heinz bodies): Heinz bodies are precipitates of denatured hemoglobin that are not shown by Romanowsky stains. They are seen on fresh unfixed cells stained supravitally with crystal violet or new methylene blue. They contain protein rather than iron or glycogen.
  49. The correct answer is A (Toxic granulation, vacuolization and Dohle bodies in neutrophils, graded by the number of cells affected): Heavy dark granules, vacuoles and bluish edge inclusions in neutrophils are described as toxic granulation, vacuolization and Dohle bodies, typical of infection or inflammation. The cells are neutrophils, so lymphocyte or blast terms do not apply. Hypersegmentation refers to nuclear lobe count and is a different finding.
  50. The correct answer is C (Use a chromogenic anti-Xa assay, because the lupus anticoagulant makes the aPTT unreliable for monitoring): A lupus anticoagulant prolongs the baseline aPTT, so the aPTT cannot reflect heparin effect reliably. A chromogenic anti-Xa assay is not affected by phospholipid-dependent inhibitors. Raising the target, using the thrombin time or using the INR does not remove the interference.
  51. The correct answer is C (Report the value with draw time noted, because the peak range does not fit): A peak range applies only to a sample taken at the peak, so a 9 hour sample is closer to a trough and cannot be compared against it. The value is measured correctly and is reported with the draw time so the clinician can interpret it. Converting the value by a calculated factor invents a result that was never measured.
  52. The correct answer is A (Issue a preliminary report noting pending review and inform the clinic, because silent delay harms care): The numeric results are valid and the clinician needs them, so a preliminary report that clearly states the film is under review keeps care moving while the referral is honoured. Withholding everything or releasing without comment would either delay care or hide an unresolved finding. Technologists can communicate that a review is pending.
  53. The correct answer is C (Treat the iron first and repeat, because low iron lowers hemoglobin A2 and masks trait): A ferritin of 6 ug/L shows iron deficiency, which lowers hemoglobin A2 and can bring a carrier's level into the normal range. With a family history, the sensible step is to correct the iron deficiency and repeat the measurement. Declaring the carrier state excluded would risk missing beta trait.
  54. The correct answer is D (Falsely prolonged clotting times, because carried-over EDTA binds the calcium the assay needs): EDTA chelates calcium, and the PT/INR depends on calcium being restored, so even a small carryover from the lavender tube lengthens the clotting time falsely. Citrate does not shorten clotting times. Closed-system tubes still carry additive on the needle, so the sequence matters. Hemolysis is not the expected effect of this carryover.
  55. The correct answer is A (Reject and request a full recollection, because excess citrate relative to plasma falsely prolongs clotting): The tube is designed for nine parts blood to one part citrate. When it is underfilled, there is too much citrate for the plasma, which binds the calcium added in the test and falsely lengthens PT and INR. Adding saline does not restore the ratio, and no analyzer can correct this error.
  56. The correct answer is C (Listen calmly and refer her to the supervisor, because complaints deserve a fair review): A calm listening response with a referral shows respect and a fair process. Defending the technologist, deferring the complaint or demanding a written submission discourages feedback. The supervisor can then review the concern properly.
  57. The correct answer is A (A 60 percent decrease, which exceeds the limit, so clumps, clots and identity are checked): The change is 240 minus 96, divided by 240, which is 60 percent, and that is greater than the 50 percent limit. Using today's value as the base gives 150 percent, which is a common miscalculation, and 40 percent is simply the share that remains. A flagged platelet fall prompts checks for clumps, clots and mislabelling before release.
  58. The correct answer is D (Hold the result and request recollection from the opposite arm, citing possible contamination): A sample drawn from the infusion arm is a classic source of heparin contamination, and a jump from 38 s to 112 s is larger than the rate change would explain. The result is probably wrong, so it is held and a new sample is requested from the opposite arm. Repeating on another analyzer or diluting does not remove heparin from the sample.
  59. The correct answer is B (At minus 70 C in sealed plastic tubes, because factor activity stays stable for months): Factor VIII is labile, so plasma intended for storage beyond a short time is frozen at minus 70 C or colder in aliquots. Frost-free freezers warm repeatedly during defrost cycles, and refrigeration or room temperature allows loss of activity within hours to days.
  60. The correct answer is B (EDTA-dependent clumping in the tube, which traps platelets and gives a falsely low automated count): In EDTA-dependent pseudothrombocytopenia, antibodies cause platelets to clump in the lavender tube, so the analyzer counts fewer single platelets. The clumps seen on the film make a true low count an unlikely explanation. Giant platelets would be seen as large single cells, and heparin does not cause clumping of this kind.
  61. The correct answer is A (Disseminated intravascular coagulation with severe hemorrhage, because promyelocyte granules release procoagulant material): Abnormal promyelocytes release tissue factor like procoagulant material and fibrinolytic activators, so acute promyelocytic leukemia often presents with or rapidly develops DIC and life-threatening bleeding. The low platelet count and bruising fit this picture. Tumour lysis and leukostasis occur in other leukemias but do not explain the particular urgency attached to this film.
  62. The correct answer is C (Gently roll the core across slides and air-dry them, because rolling avoids crush artifact): Touch imprints are made by gently rolling or touching the fresh core on glass and allowing the slide to air-dry before staining. Pressure crushes the cells and creates artifact, and a wedge film is meant for liquid aspirate rather than a solid core. Fixing the core in methanol before imprinting would ruin the cell detail the imprint is meant to show.
  63. The correct answer is C (It lyses red cells so they are not counted, leaving white cells to generate the pulses): Red cells outnumber white cells roughly a thousand to one, so they must be destroyed or the white cell pulses would be lost among them. The lysing reagent ruptures red cell membranes while white cells remain countable by size. The diluent, not the lyse, supplies electrolyte, and the reagent is not a stain.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The absolute count removes the effect of a low red cell count on the percentage): A percentage is relative to the red cells present, so a low red cell count raises it even when output is unchanged. The absolute count multiplies the percentage by the red cell count and shows true production. Maturity is described by the immature reticulocyte fraction, not by the absolute count.
  2. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin S is present, but the test alone cannot separate trait from disease or quantify it): Reduced hemoglobin S is insoluble in the high-phosphate buffer and makes the solution cloudy, so a cloudy tube is a positive screen. The test cannot tell trait from disease and does not measure the amount, so electrophoresis or HPLC must follow. A clear solution would be a negative result.
  3. The correct answer is A (Toxic granulation, which supports bacterial infection or another strong inflammatory stimulus to the marrow): The neutrophil shows coarse dark blue-purple granules scattered through the cytoplasm, which is toxic granulation, a reactive change driven by bacterial infection, inflammation or growth factors. Auer rods are thin needle-like rods in blasts, hypersegmentation means more than five nuclear lobes, and Chediak-Higashi granules are very large and few. A white count of 19.4 x10^9/L with fever fits a reactive neutrophilia.
  4. The correct answer is D (The methanol in the stain fixes the cells to the glass, because it precipitates cell proteins): Wright stain is made up in methanol, so the undiluted stain fixes the film by precipitating proteins and fastening cells to the glass. Ionization and colour development begin only after buffer is added, which is why the sheen and the second timed step matter. The undiluted stain does not rehydrate or rinse the film.
  5. The correct answer is D (Report the pattern to the supervisor, because patient safety duties apply before any harm occurs): Registered members share responsibility for protecting patients and should report unsafe practice early rather than wait for harm. Discussion among peers or silent workarounds do not correct the colleague's behaviour or involve those with authority to act.
  6. The correct answer is B (Aspirate water and hold it vertically for 15 seconds, looking for a falling meniscus): With water drawn into a tip and the pipette held upright, a falling meniscus or forming droplet during the wait signals a leak, usually from a worn seal, loose tip or damaged piston. Bubbles on dispensing do not diagnose a leak, a single weighing cannot reveal one, and tapping does not test the seal.
  7. The correct answer is B (Suspect infusion contamination, withhold the result, and request a recollection from the opposite arm): Proportional falls in hemoglobin, RBC and platelets with unchanged indices after drawing near an infusion suggest dilution of the sample, so the result is withheld and recollected from the opposite arm. Reporting it, even with a comment, could trigger an unnecessary transfusion. Repeating the same tube only reproduces the same diluted result, so a new specimen is needed.
  8. The correct answer is A (The INR reads 1.8, not 2.0, so the lot's own mean normal PT must be entered): With an ISI of 1.0 the INR equals the prothrombin time ratio. Using the true mean normal PT of 10.8 seconds gives 21.6 divided by 10.8, or 2.0, while the old 12.0 second value gives 1.8. The INR is therefore underestimated and a warfarin patient could be wrongly dose-adjusted, so the new lot's own mean normal PT must be entered. The ISI does not remove the effect of the denominator.
  9. The correct answer is B (Heparin in the sample inhibits reagent thrombin, because the fibrinogen level is adequate): Heparin acts through antithrombin and neutralizes the thrombin reagent, which lengthens the thrombin time even when fibrinogen is normal. The Clauss fibrinogen is adequate, so a fibrinogen defect does not explain the prolongation. Factor XIII and vitamin K dependent factors do not influence the thrombin time because the reagent bypasses the earlier coagulation cascade.
  10. The correct answer is A (Smudge cells, because fragile leukemic lymphocytes burst under the pressure of spreading): Fragile CLL lymphocytes are easily crushed while the film is spread, leaving smeared nuclei with no cytoplasm. Stain precipitate appears as dark crystals lying over or beside cells rather than as ruptured cells. Neutrophil and erythroblast artifacts do not explain the large number of ruptured cells among otherwise small, uniform lymphocytes.
  11. The correct answer is C (0.3 mL, because one part of saline is added to every four parts of blood sample): A ratio of four parts blood to one part saline means the saline volume is one quarter of the blood volume, so 1.2 mL of blood needs 0.3 mL of saline. Using one third or one half would over-dilute the sample and lower the sedimentation rate. Reversing the ratio would give 4.8 mL, which drowns the sample.
  12. The correct answer is A (Decline release, because sharing is limited to people in the patient's circle of care): Personal health information may be shared without consent only with providers who need it to care for that patient. Being a hospital employee or a neighbour gives no right to look. A single laboratory value tied to a named patient is still personal health information, and a manager cannot widen who may receive it.
  13. The correct answer is D (Lipid particles scatter light and raise baseline turbidity, so the fibrin signal change is masked): Photo-optical analyzers detect the rise in turbidity or fall in light transmission as fibrin forms. Lipemic plasma already scatters light, so the baseline is high and the clot signal is lost. Triglyceride does not alter pH or reagent calcium, and optical systems do not rely on a moving probe or bead.
  14. The correct answer is D (Immune hemolysis is more likely, because IgG-coated cells lose membrane in the spleen and form spherocytes): IgG on the red cells is removed in part by splenic macrophages, which leaves spherocytes and a high MCHC, so the film and indices alone cannot separate immune from inherited spherocytosis. The positive direct antiglobulin test tips the balance toward immune hemolysis. Hereditary spherocytosis would have a negative test, and the positive result is real, not nonspecific.
  15. The correct answer is B (Label the frosted end in pencil when smearing, because pencil resists methanol and stain): Pencil graphite is not dissolved by methanol, buffer or water, so it stays readable on the frosted end through fixation, staining and rinsing. Ballpoint and many marker inks dissolve in alcohol, paper labels peel in the stain bath, and relying on rack order invites specimen misidentification.
  16. The correct answer is D (Inaccuracy is minus 1.8 percent, outside the limit, so remove the pipette from use for service): The error is (98.2 minus 100) divided by 100, or minus 1.8 percent, which is beyond the 1.0 percent limit. The pipette must be taken out of service and repaired or recalibrated. A misplaced decimal hides the problem, and changing the dial setting is not an acceptable correction.
  17. The correct answer is B (4.8 x10^9/L, because 60 percent of 8.0 is the segmented plus band total): Absolute count equals percent times total white count divided by 100. Segmented plus band neutrophils is 60 percent, so 60 x 8.0 / 100 equals 4.8 x10^9/L. Using segmented cells alone, bands alone or the lymphocyte percentage answers a different question.
  18. The correct answer is A (Consumption is subsiding and the coagulopathy is resolving, because fibrinogen, platelets and PT are all recovering): Rising fibrinogen and platelets together with a falling PT and D-dimer show that consumption is slowing and synthesis is catching up. Transfusion support alone would not steadily normalize the PT and D-dimer. Liver failure would worsen the PT rather than improve it, and values this abnormal do recover once the underlying cause is treated.
  19. The correct answer is A (They are Auer rods, crystallized granule material that indicates myeloid lineage and supports acute myeloid leukemia): Auer rods are needle shaped, pink-red crystals of fused primary granules and are seen only in myeloid blasts, so they confirm myeloid lineage. They are not sepsis organisms, toxic granules or red cell inclusions. Their absence does not exclude AML, but their presence settles the lineage question.
  20. The correct answer is B (The cytoplasm fragments into platelets after the nucleus becomes polyploid through repeated endomitosis): Megakaryocytes undergo endomitosis, replicating DNA without dividing, which produces a large polyploid cell; the cytoplasm is then shed as proplatelet fragments that become platelets. Platelets are anucleate, so extruded nuclei and nucleated buds cannot be platelets. True mitotic division would yield nucleated cells, not platelets.
  21. The correct answer is B (The zone where red cells just touch, because cell morphology is clear and well spread): The differential belongs in the monolayer zone where red cells just touch and still show central pallor, because cells are spread without distortion and white cells are fairly evenly distributed. The thick body hides detail, the feathered edge flattens and distorts cells, and the side edges hold a disproportionate number of large white cells that would bias the count.
  22. The correct answer is A (Advise repeat testing after iron repletion, since iron deficiency can lower HbA2 falsely): Iron deficiency lowers HbA2, so a borderline value of 3.3 percent can hide beta thalassemia trait. Her microcytosis, a high red cell count for the MCV, and a carrier partner raise the suspicion. Repeating the same specimen only repeats the same biology, whereas retesting after iron repletion, or DNA studies, can settle the question.
  23. The correct answer is B (It links the tube, order and result so each specimen can be tracked): An accession number is a unique laboratory identifier that ties the physical specimen, its order and its result together, which prevents mix-ups when patients share names. It does not replace the patient's identity on the report or act as a billing code. The analyzer may add its own sample position, but that is separate from the accession number.
  24. The correct answer is B (Label each tube at the bedside right after the draw, checking it against the wristband): Tubes must be labelled at the point of collection with the patient present so the label can be checked against the wristband. Labels applied later, from memory or in a batch, allow tubes from different patients to be swapped. Every tube needs its own label, not just one of them.
  25.
