The correct answer is D (Sodium heparin, because it keeps viable dividing cells alive for culture without chelating calcium): Chromosome analysis needs living cells that can divide in culture, and sodium heparin is the standard anticoagulant that supports this. EDTA and citrate chelate calcium and can inhibit cell growth, while a clotted sample cannot be cultured.
  26. The correct answer is D (Release the tourniquet once blood flows and continue filling tubes, to limit hemoconcentration): The tourniquet is needed only to locate and enter the vein, and it should be released as soon as blood flows well. Releasing it lets venous pressure fall so the remaining tubes are not affected by stasis. Restarting is unnecessary once blood is flowing, and tightening the band would increase hemoconcentration.
  27. The correct answer is A (Wrong blood in tube or similar error, because MCV rarely shifts this much within one day): MCV is a stable measure because red cells live about 120 days, so a jump of over 20 fL in a day with no transfusion points to a preanalytical or identification error. The technologist should check labelling and request a new sample. Instrument drift would have affected the quality control results too.
  28. The correct answer is B (Phone the clinician with read-back, because integrity is acceptable and 6.8 exceeds the 5.0 limit): With acceptable specimen integrity and a value above the 5.0 critical limit, the policy calls for a telephone report with read-back within the time limit. A repeat is not required by the stated policy and would delay a bleeding-risk warning. The laboratory reports results but does not adjust drug doses.
  29. The correct answer is B (240 x10^9/L, because 12 platelets multiplied by 20 gives the estimate): The estimate equals the average platelets per oil field times the laboratory factor, so 12 x 20 gives 240 x10^9/L. That value lies inside the 150 to 400 x10^9/L reference interval and can be compared with the analyzer result. The other values come from using the wrong multiplier.
  30. The correct answer is C (A leukoerythroblastic picture suggests marrow infiltration, so flag the film for pathologist review): Nucleated red cells, teardrop cells and immature granulocytes form a leukoerythroblastic picture, which means the marrow space is being crowded or scarred, as in infiltration or fibrosis. In aplastic anemia the marrow is empty and such immature cells are not released. The film should be referred for review since a marrow examination will be needed.
  31. The correct answer is D (Close the cap, set the bottle aside, and report it so the supervisor can identify it): An unlabelled container must be treated as hazardous until it is identified, so the safe step is to close it, set it aside and tell the supervisor. Smelling an unknown liquid exposes the worker to vapour, and pouring it down a drain may break disposal rules or create a reaction. Topping up a stain jar would risk contaminating stain that is in use.
  32. The correct answer is D (The 2-2s rule, as two consecutive controls exceed one two SD limit, signalling systematic error): On day 9 and the following day two successive results lie beyond the same two SD limit, which is the 2-2s rejection rule. This pattern usually reflects systematic error such as a calibration or reagent change. R-4s detects random error and needs points on opposite sides, while 10-x needs ten points in a row.
  33. The correct answer is A (Left shift, because immature bands and metamyelocytes are released early as the marrow responds to demand): Left shift means more immature neutrophils, here bands and metamyelocytes, released early into the blood when the marrow responds to a demand such as appendicitis. Right shift refers to hypersegmented forms seen in megaloblastic states. A leukemoid reaction requires a much higher count and a larger proportion of immature cells, and Pelger-Huet anomaly is an inherited nuclear defect with a normal count.
  34. The correct answer is A (Perform factor V Leiden genetic testing, because a low ratio is only a functional screen): A low aPTT-based activated protein C resistance ratio is a functional screen, and the factor V Leiden mutation is confirmed by molecular testing. Protein S deficiency, antithrombin deficiency and a factor VIII inhibitor are not the usual cause of this pattern.
  35. The correct answer is B (Refer the film to the pathologist, because teardrop cells with immature forms suggest marrow fibrosis): Teardrop cells whose tails point in many directions are true dacrocytes, and together with nucleated red cells and myelocytes they form a leukoerythroblastic picture. This pattern needs medical review for marrow fibrosis or infiltration. Spreading artifact gives tails pointing in one direction only.
  36. The correct answer is A (Reject it and recollect, because excess citrate relative to plasma falsely prolongs clotting times): Coagulation tubes need the 9 to 1 blood to citrate ratio, and an underfilled tube holds too much citrate for the plasma, which prolongs clotting times and inflates the INR. The ratio cannot be repaired by topping up or by dilution. The tube is rejected and a properly filled one is collected.
  37. The correct answer is B (Ten will remain, so she should tell the charge technologist early and request help): At 18 specimens per 90 minutes the rate is 0.2 per minute, so 60 minutes adds 12 more for a total of 30. That leaves 10 of the 40 unfinished. Telling the charge technologist early lets the team redistribute work, while skipping quality control or staying silent puts patient results at risk.
  38. The correct answer is A (Collect with the patient reclined and explain each step, because fainting risk falls): Collecting with the patient reclined lowers the risk of injury if fainting occurs, and explaining each step reduces anxiety. An upright chair raises fall risk for a patient with prior fainting, and restraint by a parent is inappropriate. Withholding explanation tends to increase fear.
  39. The correct answer is D (Remove the gloves, clean the hands, and put on fresh gloves before touching shared surfaces): A glove that has touched blood must be replaced and hands cleaned before anything shared is touched, such as a microscope or keyboard. Alcohol on a glove does not reliably remove blood or make it safe to reuse. Using fewer fingers or layering gloves still spreads the contamination onto shared equipment.
  40. The correct answer is C (Minus 1.4 percent, meaning the method reads slightly lower than the assigned target value): Percent bias is the difference between the observed mean and the target, divided by the target and multiplied by 100, so 138 minus 140 is minus 2, divided by 140 is minus 1.43 percent. The sign is negative because the method reads low. Quoting the raw difference or reversing the sign misstates the bias.
  41. The correct answer is D (Examine the thin feathered edge, because true stacking persists there while an artifact disappears): Stacked red cells in the thick body can be a drying or thickness artifact, so the thin feathered edge, where cells lie singly, is the place to judge. If stacking persists there it is real, as with raised plasma proteins. Changing buffer, drop size or looking only at thick areas does not answer the question.
  42. The correct answer is C (Telephone the clinician with the corrected result, then document the call and report the event): The ordering clinician holds the patient relationship and decides how disclosure happens, so the laboratory's first duty is to give that clinician the corrected platelet count of 18 x10^9/L at once. The call is documented and the event entered in the incident system for review. Waiting for a meeting delays care, and a corrected report alone may not be seen in time.
  43. The correct answer is B (Request a peripheral recollection, because heparin from the line can contaminate the sample): A sample drawn through a line used for heparin infusion can carry heparin into the tube, which prolongs the aPTT and the thrombin time together. The result then does not reflect the patient's true coagulation status, so a clean peripheral recollection is needed. Dilution or a lupus anticoagulant comment would misstate the cause, and a critical call could prompt an unwarranted dose change.
  44. The correct answer is A (Run the blower to purge, wipe the surfaces, then load only needed supplies): The blower is run for several minutes so airflow stabilizes and particles are purged, the work surface is disinfected, and only the supplies needed are placed inside before work starts. Loading before airflow is established or starting work immediately skips this equilibration. A UV lamp with the sash open exposes the eyes and skin and is not a substitute for cleaning.
  45. The correct answer is A (Stating details tests real knowledge, whereas agreeing with read details can be automatic): Asking patients to state their own details forces recall, so a drowsy or confused patient who simply says yes is less likely to be misidentified. The wristband remains a required check and is compared as well. Privacy is not breached by a quiet request in a private area.
  46. The correct answer is C (Chronic exposure to low oxygen raises erythropoietin and red cell production at altitude): Living at high altitude lowers the oxygen supply, which raises erythropoietin and red cell mass, so hemoglobin and hematocrit sit slightly above sea-level intervals in healthy people. Dehydration could also raise values but is not suggested here. Polycythemia vera needs further evidence, and an analyzer does not read high because of the collection altitude.
  47. The correct answer is C (Positive with anti-C3d and negative with anti-IgG, because IgM fixes complement then leaves the cells): In cold agglutinin disease, IgM binds red cells in the cold, fixes complement and then dissociates as blood warms, leaving C3d on the cells. The DAT is therefore typically anti-C3d positive and anti-IgG negative. Warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia more often shows IgG, with or without complement.
  48. The correct answer is D (Stop the release, retrain and reassess the technologist, and review earlier results for impact): Releasing results without checking QC is an observed performance gap, so patient results must be held, the technologist retrained and reassessed, and recent results reviewed for impact. An old sign-off does not prove current practice, and a single lapse is handled through a competency process, not automatic dismissal.
  49. The correct answer is D (How far each control result lies from the laboratory's own mean, counted in standard deviations): The mean and standard deviation lines are calculated from the laboratory's own control data, so they describe the expected spread of that method. A result's distance from the mean, counted in standard deviations, lets the technologist judge acceptability the same way every day. The insert range is only a starting guide and does not set patient release limits.
  50. The correct answer is A (Lymphocytes are normal at 1.9 x10^9/L, and the real finding is neutropenia at 0.9 x10^9/L): Absolute count equals percent times total count: 62 percent of 3.0 is 1.86, about 1.9 x10^9/L, which is inside 1.0 to 4.0. Neutrophils at 30 percent give 0.9 x10^9/L, well below 2.0, so the true abnormality is neutropenia. A raised percentage of lymphocytes here only reflects the shortage of neutrophils.
  51. The correct answer is A (A phone call to a clinician with read-back documented, because receipt must be confirmed): New blast cells in a child are an urgent finding, so direct verbal notification to a clinician is required, with read-back and documentation to prove the message was received. Passive methods such as a courier, a chart flag or a handover note do not guarantee that anyone acts promptly.
  52. The correct answer is D (Issue corrected reports for both patients, notify both clinicians, and keep originals visible as amended): A misattributed result that has been viewed must be corrected openly: corrected reports go out, the clinicians are told directly, and the originals remain in the record marked as amended. Deleting or quietly overwriting destroys the audit trail. Waiting for a query leaves one patient with a wrong result in the chart.
  53. The correct answer is B (20, because 400 uL of total volume divided by 20 uL of blood gives it): The dilution factor is the total volume divided by the volume of blood. Twenty microlitres of blood in 380 uL of diluent makes 400 uL, so the factor is 400 / 20 equals 20, a 1 in 20 dilution. Dividing the diluent alone by the blood gives 19, which forgets that the blood is part of the final volume.
  54. The correct answer is A (Dilution weakens inhibitors such as heparin, and excess thrombin makes clot time fibrinogen dependent): Diluting the plasma reduces the effect of inhibitors such as heparin and fibrin degradation products. The high thrombin level removes other factors from the reaction, so the clotting time depends mainly on the fibrinogen concentration and can be read from a curve. Thrombin acts on fibrinogen directly, not on the intrinsic pathway.
  55. The correct answer is A (1.6 percent, because 2.4 percent is multiplied by 0.30 over 0.45): The raw count is 24 per 1000, or 2.4 percent. The correction multiplies by the patient hematocrit over the normal hematocrit: 2.4 x 0.30 / 0.45 = 1.6 percent. Inverting the ratio gives 3.6 percent, and skipping the division gives 0.7 percent.
  56. The correct answer is D (It is presumptive HbS, because HbD and HbG co-migrate there, so confirmation is needed): At alkaline pH, HbS, HbD and HbG migrate to the same position, so the band is only presumptive. A second method such as acid citrate agar electrophoresis or a solubility test separates HbS from HbD and HbG. HbC does not run with HbS at this pH, since it migrates with HbA2.
  57. The correct answer is C (Replace the spreader with a smooth-edged slide, because a chipped edge drags cells into streaks): A chipped spreader edge drags cells unevenly and leaves ragged tails and streaks, so replacing it with a smooth slide is the fix. A steeper angle gives a shorter, thicker film, and diluting the blood alters the cell distribution without curing the cause.
  58. The correct answer is B (Vigorous shaking of the tube, because mechanical force bursts fragile red cells in vitro): Hard shaking subjects red cells to shear forces that burst membranes, releasing hemoglobin into the plasma. Gentle inversion mixes the additive without this damage. A 21-gauge needle is a standard size for adult collection, and a fully filled tube or a short tourniquet time does not by itself lyse cells.
  59. The correct answer is A (1 3s rule, so results are held, because one value beyond 3 SD rejects the run): A single control value beyond three standard deviations violates the 1 3s rule, which is a rejection rule most sensitive to random error. The run is rejected and patient results are held until the cause is found and acceptable controls are obtained. The 1 2s rule is only a warning, and the 2 2s and R 4s rules need different patterns than one extreme point.
  60. The correct answer is C (1.02 x10^9/L, which is above the interval, so the eosinophils are flagged high): The absolute count is 8.5 x 12 / 100, which is 1.02 x10^9/L, above the upper limit of 0.5, so it is flagged high. Writing the percent as a decimal or dividing by it gives values that do not follow from the formula. Reporting the absolute value lets the clinician see an eosinophilia that 12 percent alone does not show clearly.
  61. The correct answer is D (Heparin is unsuitable for routine CBC, so request a lavender tube recollection before reporting platelets): Heparin can clump platelets, leaves a blue background on Romanowsky stained films and distorts white cell morphology, so EDTA is the standard CBC anticoagulant. A clump flag on a heparinized tube cannot be trusted as a patient finding. Estimating from the film does not replace a valid sample.

Practice Test 13 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (7.2 x10^9/L, because 60 percent is applied to the corrected WBC of 12.0 x10^9/L): The corrected WBC is 18.0 x 100 / 150 = 12.0 x10^9/L, and 60 percent of that is 7.2 x10^9/L. Using the raw count inflates every absolute value because the nRBC nuclei were counted as white cells. The corrected WBC is the total, not the neutrophil count.
  2. The correct answer is B (The last tube, because blood from a traumatic puncture clears as flow continues): A traumatic tap adds blood to the first fluid collected, which would falsely raise the red and white cell counts. The final tube has the least blood contamination, so it is the best pick for the count. The tubes do not hold identical cell concentrations.
  3. The correct answer is D (Report parasites consistent with Babesia and call the physician, because Plasmodium does not form tetrads): Tetrad forms with small pleomorphic rings are the hallmark of Babesia, which is tick borne in parts of North America and can be severe, so the physician must be called. Plasmodium does not produce tetrads, and a patient without travel makes imported malaria less likely. Platelets and trypanosomes do not sit inside red cells.
  4. The correct answer is D (Phone the physician at once as a critical result, documenting the name, time and read back): A neutrophil predominant CSF with intracellular organisms suggests bacterial meningitis, which is a life threatening critical result. The physician must be called without delay, and the call must be documented with name, time and read back. Finishing QC, repeating slides first or couriering the slide all delay treatment.
  5. The correct answer is C (The medial or lateral plantar heel surface, because bone lies close beneath the posterior curve): Heel punctures are made on the medial or lateral plantar surface to avoid the calcaneus, which lies near the skin at the posterior curve and can be injured or develop osteomyelitis. The central arch is also avoided because of nerves and tendons. The heel tissue is not equally deep everywhere, so the site must be chosen carefully.
  6. The correct answer is D (Provincial and accreditation requirements, because the policy must meet or exceed those minimums): Retention periods come from provincial regulation and the standards of the accreditation body, and the laboratory writes its own policy to meet or exceed them. Storage space is a practical limit that must be solved, not a reason to shorten retention. Clinician preference and test speed have no bearing on how long a record must be kept.
  7. The correct answer is A (Validated manual methods with quality control and a flag on results produced in downtime): Downtime procedures use validated manual methods with QC and identify results produced during the outage so they can be traced. Releasing results unreviewed or estimating from old values removes quality assurance. Technologists can assess quality when QC and acceptance criteria are in place.
  8. The correct answer is C (A fall of 25 percent exceeds the limit, so the result is held for investigation): Percent change is the difference divided by the previous value: 142 minus 106 is 36 g/L, and 36 divided by 142 is about 25 percent. This exceeds the 20 percent limit, so the result is held and identity, collection and clinical change are reviewed. Dividing by the new value gives a wrong percentage, and g/L cannot be compared directly with a percent limit.
  9. The correct answer is C (Raise the condenser near the stage and adjust its diaphragm, because focused light restores contrast): The condenser sits beneath the stage and must be raised near the slide so it focuses light onto the film. Its aperture diaphragm is then adjusted to balance contrast and resolution. Brightness alone does not restore contrast, and removing the condenser or closing the diaphragm entirely degrades image quality.
  10. The correct answer is D (The 10x rule, a systematic shift, so check calibration, because bias has moved the mean): Ten consecutive control values on one side of the mean violate the 10x rule, which signals a small systematic shift even when every point is inside two standard deviations. Causes include calibration drift, reagent deterioration or changed control material. A 1 3s violation needs an extreme single point, and staying within two SD does not mean the run is in control.
  11. The correct answer is B (Myelocyte, because metamyelocytes and later stages no longer synthesize DNA or divide): Myelocytes are the final granulocyte stage that divides; at the metamyelocyte stage the cell stops synthesizing DNA and only matures. The promyelocyte divides too, but it is earlier, so it is not the final dividing stage. Bands and segmented forms belong to the non-dividing maturation pool.
  12. The correct answer is C (Handle slides by the edges and wipe with clean lint-free tissue, because grease repels blood): Round holes are classic signs of grease or fingerprint contamination, because oil on glass repels the drop and leaves bare patches. Handling slides by their edges and wiping them clean removes the source. Drop size, stain pH and fixation time cannot restore blood to areas that the glass repelled.
  13. The correct answer is A (Recollect in citrate and multiply the repeat platelet count by 1.1, because citrate dilutes the blood): Clumps in EDTA indicate EDTA-dependent platelet aggregation, which falsely lowers the automated count. Collecting into sodium citrate prevents this in most patients, and the result is corrected for the 9 to 1 dilution. Estimating from clumps or reporting the low value would mislead clinicians.
  14. The correct answer is D (Reject the aPTT and recollect in the correct tube order, documenting the heparin carryover error): A green heparin tube filled before the light blue tube can carry heparin into the citrate sample, so the aPTT is falsely long while the PT is only mildly affected. A normal PT does not prove the sample is clean because many reagents neutralize heparin. The sample must be rejected and recollected in the proper order, and the error documented for quality review, rather than reported or repeated.
  15. The correct answer is A (Repeat on a fresh sample with a vibration-free rack, because delay and vibration alter results): ESR must be set up within about four hours of collection at room temperature, and the tube must stand vertical on a level, vibration-free surface. Both faults can change the sedimentation rate, and the normal CRP is a hint that the result may be an artifact. Reporting or averaging a compromised reading carries the fault forward.
  16. The correct answer is C (Remake the buffer and check the water, because pH 7.4 shifts colours toward blue): The rinse buffer sets the final pH during staining, so a reading of 7.4 against a target of 6.8 would push colours toward blue. The reasonable step is to remake the buffer and look for a cause such as poor water or a wrong tablet. Using it as is, only for rinsing, or blending it with other buffer ignores the pH specification.
  17. The correct answer is B (It lyses red cells while preserving platelets, so platelets stand out as refractile bodies): Ammonium oxalate at 1 percent lyses red cells but leaves platelets intact, and under phase contrast the platelets appear as small, round or oval, shiny refractile bodies. It does not stain platelets, it does not lyse them, and it does not fix them to the chamber. The chamber is left in a moist dish for about 15 minutes so platelets settle before counting.
  18. The correct answer is A (Hemoglobin S is still below the detection level because hemoglobin F predominates in early infancy): Newborns and young infants carry mostly hemoglobin F, which does not sickle, so hemoglobin S can be below the level the test detects until about six months of age. Since the controls worked and the hemoglobin concentration is adequate, reagent failure and dilution are not the explanation. The solubility test also gives a positive result in sickle trait, so it cannot distinguish trait from disease.
  19. The correct answer is A (96 x10^9/L, from multiplying 120 by 100 and then dividing by 125 (100 plus 25)): The corrected count is the reported WBC multiplied by 100 and divided by 100 plus the nucleated red cells per 100 WBC: 120 x 100 / 125 equals 96 x10^9/L. Subtracting the nRBC number directly or inverting the factor gives incorrect values, and the stated analyzer limitation means no correction was applied. The corrected value is the one that should be reported and used for the differential.
  20. The correct answer is B (A large cohort of polychromatic reticulocytes, because a brisk marrow response raises average volume): Reticulocytes are larger than mature red cells, so a strong reticulocytosis of about 4 percent corrected raises the MCV and gives polychromasia on the film. The marrow is responding, which makes B12 deficiency and marrow suppression unlikely, since both lower the reticulocyte count.
  21. The correct answer is B (Report a corrected WBC of 3.6 x10^9/L as leukopenic, with the nRBC count per 100 WBC): The corrected count is 5.0 x 100 / 140, which is 3.6 x10^9/L and falls below the reference interval, so a true leukopenia would be hidden if the raw count were reported. Reporting the nRBC count per 100 WBC lets the clinician see why the number changed. Adding nRBCs or rejecting the sample both misuse the correction.
  22. The correct answer is C (Results go to the care team or to people the patient has authorized): Health information may be released only to the patient's care providers or to people the patient has authorized, as provincial privacy law such as PHIPA requires. An employer paying for or interested in the result has no automatic right to it. A nurse ordering the test can use it for care but cannot pass it to a third party without consent.
  23. The correct answer is C (Its ability to bind platelet GPIb and agglutinate platelets when ristocetin is added): Ristocetin causes VWF to bind platelet glycoprotein Ib, so the cofactor assay measures how well the patient's VWF agglutinates platelets, which is a functional test. VWF antigen measures protein amount by antibody binding, and carrying factor VIII is a separate function reflected by the factor VIII assay.
  24. The correct answer is D (The 4 1s rule signals a systematic shift, so targets are re-established for the new lot): Four consecutive results more than one standard deviation on the same side of the mean break the 4 1s rule, which points to a systematic shift. Because the chart still used the old lot's mean, the likely cause is that the new lot has a different mean control value, so a new target range must be established from repeated runs. The pattern is not random error, and no evidence suggests expiry.
  25. The correct answer is C (2.4 x10^9/L, because 28 percent of the 8.4 x10^9/L total count is about 2.35 x10^9/L): Absolute count equals percent times total white count divided by 100, so 28 times 8.4 divided by 100 gives about 2.35 x10^9/L, which rounds to 2.4. Dividing the percent by 100 alone leaves out the total count, and the 62 percent figure belongs to the neutrophils. The result lies inside the adult lymphocyte interval of 1.0 to 4.0 x10^9/L.
  26. The correct answer is B (It exceeds the age interval at 19.6 x10^9/L and suggests infection such as pertussis): The absolute count is 70 percent of 28, or 19.6 x10^9/L, well above the age limit of 13.5 even though lymphocytes normally predominate in infants. A prolonged cough with marked lymphocytosis is a classic pattern in pertussis. Chronic lymphocytic leukemia is not diagnosed from a count alone and is not seen in infants of this age.
  27. The correct answer is C (Natural antibodies bind platelet glycoprotein sites exposed after EDTA chelates calcium, causing clumping in vitro): Some people carry natural antibodies that react with platelet glycoprotein IIb-IIIa after EDTA chelates calcium and changes the receptor shape. The platelets then clump in vitro and the analyzer reads a falsely low count. Heparin and citrate do not expose the site, which is why the heparin tube looks normal.
  28. The correct answer is A (Two distinct red cell populations are present, so a blood film should be reviewed to confirm): A bimodal curve means small and large red cells coexist, and the reported mean of 90 fL falls between the peaks and describes neither group, so the MCV is misleading and the RDW is high. Platelets would appear at the far left of the scale, and an aperture fault does not create a clean, symmetrical second population. A film review confirms the dimorphism.
  29. The correct answer is B (2.4 percent, because 4.0 multiplied by 0.27 and divided by 0.45 corrects for anemia): The corrected reticulocyte count is the reticulocyte percentage times the patient hematocrit divided by the normal hematocrit, so 4.0 x 0.27 / 0.45 equals 2.4 percent. The raw 4.0 percent overstates marrow output because fewer red cells are present in the denominator. Inverting the ratio gives 6.7 percent, which makes the error worse.
  30. The correct answer is A (Recollect with reduced citrate volume, because too much citrate per plasma volume prolongs clotting): When the hematocrit is above about 0.55 L/L, a standard blood to citrate ratio leaves too little plasma for the fixed amount of citrate. The excess citrate binds the calcium that is added in the test, so clotting times become falsely prolonged. The sample should be recollected into a tube with the citrate volume adjusted for the hematocrit, not reported or topped up.
  31. The correct answer is C (Phone the physician that day and refer the film for review, since this is urgent): Ring forms with double chromatin dots suggest malaria parasites, which can be life threatening, so the finding is communicated by telephone the same day and the film is sent for confirmation. A lack of travel history on the requisition does not rule it out. Waiting for a repeat or relying on routine delivery delays care.
  32. The correct answer is C (The thick film concentrates parasites for detection, and the thin film preserves morphology for species identification): A thick film lyses red cells and layers many fields of blood into a small area, which makes low parasitemia easier to detect. A thin film is fixed in methanol and keeps red cell shape, which is needed to recognize species by the size of infected cells and parasite forms. The two films serve opposite purposes, so neither is simply a spare.
  33. The correct answer is B (Reject the sample and request recollection, because platelet factor 4 release can neutralize heparin): For patients on unfractionated heparin, platelets in an uncentrifuged tube release platelet factor 4, which binds heparin and shortens the aPTT, so the limit is shorter than for PT. The sample is past the laboratory limit, so a fresh tube is required. Spinning now cannot undo the neutralization, a 24 hour limit applies to PT rather than heparin aPTT, and cold storage activates factors and worsens results.
  34. The correct answer is B (Recollect with less citrate matched to the hematocrit, because plasma volume is reduced): When the hematocrit is above about 0.55 L/L the plasma volume is reduced, so the fixed citrate volume is relatively excessive and binds the calcium added at testing, falsely prolonging clotting times. Recollecting with citrate reduced to match the hematocrit corrects this. EDTA cannot be used for coagulation testing, and extra citrate would worsen the problem.
  35. The correct answer is C (In the flammable-liquids cabinet, because methanol vapour ignites easily and the cabinet limits fire spread): Methanol-based stain is a flammable liquid and belongs in a rated flammable-liquids cabinet that protects the bottles from heat and limits the spread of a fire. A general shelf offers no fire protection, and an acid cabinet mixes incompatible classes of chemical. An ordinary refrigerator contains sparking parts that can ignite vapour.
  36. The correct answer is D (Use 4.62 x10^12/L, the laboratory's own measured mean, since lots differ for the same product): Each control lot has its own target on the laboratory's analyzer, so the chart mean for a new lot is taken from the laboratory's own accumulated data. The measured average is 4.62. Carrying over the old mean or the insert value would show a false shift or hide a real one.
  37. The correct answer is D (PT reagents neutralize heparin, so the PT stays normal at therapeutic heparin levels): Commercial PT thromboplastin reagents contain a heparin neutralizer, usually effective up to about 1 unit/mL, so the PT stays normal at therapeutic heparin concentrations. The aPTT reagent has no such neutralizer and lengthens in proportion to the heparin effect. Heparin acts through antithrombin on thrombin and factor Xa, not on the contact factors alone.
  38. The correct answer is C (Color comes from residual factor Xa, so lower color signal means a higher heparin level): Heparin enhances antithrombin inhibition of the added factor Xa. The remaining active factor Xa cleaves the chromogenic substrate and releases the colored product, so color is inversely proportional to heparin concentration. Heparin does not cleave the substrate and the method does not measure fibrin formation.
  39. The correct answer is A (Filter or replace the stain, restain affected smears and document it, because debris mimics platelets): Stain precipitate can look like platelets, bacteria or inclusions and may lead to false reports. The source reagent must be filtered or replaced, affected smears restained, and the corrective action recorded. Rinsing dried slides does not reliably remove debris, and a comment does not correct a faulty film.
  40. The correct answer is B (Leave the lid shut for 30 minutes so aerosols settle, tell the supervisor, then disinfect): A tube that breaks during a spin can create an aerosol, so the lid stays closed for about 30 minutes while it settles and the supervisor is told. Cleaning then proceeds with disinfectant and full protection. Opening the unit at once releases the aerosol, restarting the run spreads the damage, and wiping only the seam leaves the contaminated interior untouched.
  41. The correct answer is D (Read to the top of the red cells, because the buffy coat holds white cells): The packed cell volume refers to red cells alone. The thin layer above them is the buffy coat, made of white cells and platelets, so it is excluded and the reading stops at the top of the red cells. Including part or all of the buffy coat or reading at the plasma boundary overstates the hematocrit.
  42. The correct answer is B (It is the geometric mean normal PT, the denominator of the ratio raised to ISI): The mean normal prothrombin time, calculated as a geometric mean from at least 20 healthy donors tested with the new lot, is the denominator in the PT ratio before it is raised to the ISI. Without a lot-specific value the INR can be biased. The median control and the upper normal limit serve other purposes.
  43. The correct answer is B (As a marked corrected report showing the original value, the new value, and the correction time): A corrected report keeps the audit trail by showing what was originally reported, what the true value is, and when and by whom it was changed. Overwriting or deleting hides that history, and a phone call without a corrected report leaves a wrong value in the chart. The treating clinician should also be told promptly.
  44. The correct answer is A (Investigate clerical, handling and analytical causes of the deviation and document the review): An unacceptable survey result is treated like a patient-impacting problem: check transcription, sample handling, and analytical performance before changing anything. Adjusting calibration without evidence, discarding the result, or repeating until it fits the mean all hide the true cause.
  45. The correct answer is B (Storage artifact from prolonged contact with EDTA, so films should be made promptly after collection): EDTA damages cells on standing, producing crenation, vacuolated monocytes and nuclear changes in neutrophils, so films should be made soon after collection. These changes reflect the sample age and not a patient disorder. Spreader speed and too little anticoagulant would cause other defects such as ridges or clots.
  46. The correct answer is C (Unbuffered water shifts the pH, so films stain too blue and eosinophil granules fade): Romanowsky dyes bind according to pH, and the buffer holds it near 6.8. Alkaline tap water pushes the stain toward blue and weakens the eosin, which dulls eosinophil granules. Fixation and dye strength are not changed by rinse water.
  47. The correct answer is B (As a band neutrophil, because its nucleus has uniform width and no thin filament between lobes): A band has a nucleus of uniform width with no thin filament separating lobes, so it is counted as a band neutrophil. A segmented neutrophil has lobes joined by a thread-like filament. A metamyelocyte has a kidney-shaped nucleus with indentation but no band form, and thickness of the nucleus alone does not identify toxic change.
  48. The correct answer is C (Lower it to the marked height, because that restores face velocity and shields her): Fume hoods capture vapour at a set face velocity, which is reached only when the sash is at the marked working height, and the sash also acts as a barrier against splashes. A fully open sash lowers face velocity and lets vapour escape toward the worker. Removing the sash or sealing it completely defeats either the airflow or the ability to work.
  49. The correct answer is D (Repeated small draws can cause iatrogenic anemia, so volumes and test frequency should be minimized): A premature infant has a very small total blood volume and limited marrow reserve, so many small draws add up to a real loss of red cells. This iatrogenic anemia is a recognized risk, which is why laboratories use microsamples and combine tests. Frequent small samples are not harmless because the losses accumulate.
  50. The correct answer is D (Low haptoglobin with raised LD and indirect bilirubin, because hemoglobin escapes destroyed cells): Free hemoglobin from destroyed red cells binds haptoglobin and lowers it, while LD from the cells and unconjugated bilirubin from heme breakdown both rise. A raised haptoglobin is an acute phase response and points away from hemolysis. A raised direct bilirubin suggests an obstruction or liver problem rather than red cell destruction.
  51. The correct answer is A (It dilutes interfering heparin and fibrin degradation products, so clotting time depends mainly on fibrinogen): The patient plasma is diluted so that interfering substances such as heparin and fibrin degradation products are diluted out, making the clotting time depend mainly on fibrinogen when thrombin is in excess. Calcium and factor XIII play no role in this thrombin-driven endpoint. The dilution is not intended to separate fibrinogen from other plasma proteins.
  52. The correct answer is B (A breach of confidentiality, because information from work was used for a personal purpose): Using information seen on a requisition to contact an acquaintance about a diagnosis uses work access for a personal purpose, which is a breach of confidentiality. Friendship does not authorize the contact. A private message still reveals knowledge gained through the job.
  53. The correct answer is A (Smoking raises carboxyhemoglobin and can mildly raise both hemoglobin and white cell counts in adults): Smoking raises carboxyhemoglobin, which reduces oxygen delivery and stimulates a mild rise in red cell mass, and it also causes a chronic low-grade leukocytosis. These effects explain the mildly high hemoglobin and white count in a healthy smoker. Smoking does not shrink red cells directly or suppress the marrow.
  54. The correct answer is C (3.0 percent, because 6.0 multiplied by 0.225 and then divided by 0.45 equals 3.0): Corrected reticulocyte percent = retic percent x (patient hematocrit / 0.45) = 6.0 x 0.5 = 3.0 percent. Inverting the ratio overstates the result, and skipping the division leaves an unscaled value. The correction adjusts for the low red cell mass that exaggerates the percentage.
  55. The correct answer is B (Stop the draw, explain the purpose calmly, and document the refusal for the practitioner): A competent adult may refuse collection at any moment, and consent given earlier or implied by a requisition can be withdrawn. The technologist explains the purpose of the test and records the refusal so the practitioner knows the test was not done. Restraining or pressuring a patient is an assault, not collection.
  56. The correct answer is D (Keep the STAT platelet run as priority and give both callers honest time estimates): The STAT platelet count on an intensive care patient carries the highest clinical risk and stays the priority. Giving both callers honest estimates lets each physician plan around the delay. Ignoring the phone removes communication, and releasing by arrival order ignores urgency.
  57. The correct answer is C (Stain precipitate artifact, so restain with filtered stain, because parasites lie inside red cells): Identical granules on the glass between cells cannot be parasites or inclusions, because those lie inside red cells. Unfiltered stain leaves precipitate that settles on cells and glass, so restaining with filtered stain and re-reading is the right step. Calling an urgent result or reporting inclusions would send false findings.
  58. The correct answer is A (Investigate the failed delta check for a mislabelled or wrong-patient tube before triggering reflex tests): A fall of 83 g/L in a week with a changed MCV fails any reasonable delta check and points toward a wrong-patient or mislabelled tube. Reflex tests on a misidentified sample would waste resources and produce misleading results. The identification check comes first, and the critical call follows only once the specimen is confirmed to belong to the patient.
Case CHB10

A 58-year-old patient on warfarin is seen in a rural clinic. A phlebotomist draws a 2.7 mL light blue tube, but the tube is only half filled because the vein collapsed. The same-day CBC gave a hematocrit of 0.62 L/L. The therapeutic INR target is 2.0 to 3.0, and the analyzer reported an INR of 4.1 from this tube.

  1. The correct answer is A (Excess citrate relative to plasma binds more calcium, which falsely lengthens the clotting time): A half-filled tube leaves the usual 0.3 mL of citrate mixed with too little blood, so excess citrate chelates the calcium that is added back in the test and lengthens the clotting time. The error runs towards prolongation, not shortening. Platelets are removed before the PT is measured, so a platelet shortage is not the mechanism.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reduce the citrate volume, because the blood contains less plasma to receive the anticoagulant): Above a hematocrit of about 0.55 L/L the plasma fraction is smaller, so the standard citrate amount is excessive and prolongs clotting times. Laboratories reduce the citrate volume using a formula. Adding citrate worsens the imbalance, and EDTA cannot be used for clotting assays.
  3. The correct answer is A (Withhold the result and recollect a properly filled, hematocrit adjusted citrate tube for repeat testing): Both problems push the INR upward, so the value cannot be trusted and the flawed tube cannot be corrected by calculation. Warfarin dosing based on a falsely high INR could cause harm. A properly filled tube with citrate volume adjusted for the hematocrit gives a valid result, and repeating a flawed tube only repeats the flaw.
  4. The correct answer is D (Refer her to the ordering clinician or records office, because phone identity is unverified): Patients have a right of access to their own records, but access goes through a process that confirms identity, such as the clinician or the records office. A name and birth date given by phone are easy for another person to supply. Refusing all release ignores the right of access, and a fax to an unverified number risks a breach.
  5. The correct answer is B (About 10.8, which is below 13 and agrees with beta thalassemia trait): The Mentzer index is MCV divided by RBC: 66 / 6.1 is about 10.8. A value below 13 favours thalassemia trait, and a high red cell count with a low MCV is typical of it. The raised HbA2 of 5.4 percent supports this, whereas iron deficiency would usually show a lower red cell count.
  6. The correct answer is D (Demargination from vessel walls into the circulating pool, driven by exercise and epinephrine release): Exercise and epinephrine release shift neutrophils from the marginating pool along vessel walls into the circulating pool, giving a rapid and transient neutrophilia of mature cells. A bacterial infection would usually show toxic changes and a left shift, which are absent here. Leukemia would show a persistent change with immature or basophilic forms, and the spleen does not explain a short-lived rise.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (About 0.36 L/L, so the large mismatch needs investigation and a repeat before release): Dividing the hemoglobin of 120 g/L by 10 and multiplying by 3 predicts a hematocrit near 36 percent, or 0.36 L/L. A reported 0.48 L/L is far outside that estimate and gives an MCHC of 250 g/L, so a sampling, identification or analyzer problem must be sought before release. A repeat on a fresh, well-mixed sample resolves it.
  2. The correct answer is C (600 x10^9/L, because the measured value at 800 falls outside the 5 percent limit): Values at 20 through 600 stay within 5 percent of expected (597 against 600 is a difference of 0.5 percent), but the measured value at 800 is 655, about 18 percent low, which fails. The upper linear limit is therefore 600 x10^9/L, and higher specimens must be diluted and rerun. A plateau shows the detector or counting system saturating.
  3. The correct answer is D (Internal complexity such as granules and lobulation, while forward scatter mainly tracks cell size): Forward scatter is proportional to cell size, whereas side scatter increases with internal structure such as granules and nuclear segmentation, which is why granulocytes sit high on the side scatter axis. Hemoglobin concentration, surface antigens, viability and fluorescence are measured by other detectors or reagents. Combining both angles separates lymphocytes, monocytes and granulocytes.
  4. The correct answer is D (Numerous schistocytes with a platelet count of 12 x10^9/L, because thrombotic microangiopathy needs rapid treatment): Many schistocytes with severe thrombocytopenia and new neurologic symptoms suggest a thrombotic microangiopathy, a condition where delay in treatment is dangerous, so it is a critical morphology call. Occasional target cells, mild toxic change and slight polychromasia are common, expected findings that go on the routine report.
  5. The correct answer is B (Platelets release platelet factor 4, which binds heparin and falsely lowers the measured anti-Xa activity): Activated platelets release platelet factor 4, which neutralizes heparin and so gives a falsely low anti-Xa result. Prompt double centrifugation removes the platelets and prevents this. The other statements describe effects that do not occur in a chromogenic anti-Xa assay.
  6. The correct answer is C (Stop using the cabinet, tag it out of service, and notify the supervisor to arrange recertification): A cabinet past its certification date with a faulty airflow alarm cannot be trusted to contain aerosols, so it is removed from use, tagged and reported while an alternative plan such as a certified cabinet elsewhere is arranged. A fume hood does not filter infectious aerosols, and a surgical mask offers little protection against them. Urgency does not justify using failed containment.
  7. The correct answer is D (The peak near day 8 followed by a fall shows an appropriate marrow response): The reticulocyte count rises above the 2.5 percent upper limit by day 4, peaks at 9.1 percent around day 8 and returns toward normal by day 14. This is the expected response to effective iron therapy, with the peak at roughly one week and a fall as the new cells mature. A flat curve would suggest non-adherence, malabsorption or another cause.
  8. The correct answer is D (About 0.2 percent, which is within the limit, so carryover is acceptable): Carryover is the first low result minus the third low result, divided by the third high result minus the third low result, times 100. That is (0.5 minus 0.3) divided by (100.0 minus 0.3), or 0.2 percent. Leaving out the baseline low value or misplacing the decimal gives wrong figures, and the real value is below the 1.0 percent limit.
  9. The correct answer is C (Telephone the result to the responsible clinician with read-back, and document who was notified): A telephoned result with read-back and documentation is the required response to a value on the critical list, whether or not the patient has symptoms. Releasing the result only to the chart leaves the clinician unaware, and delaying for a repeat or for film review postpones notification of a patient at high risk of infection.
  10. The correct answer is D (The ratio is 6 to 1, so reject it because excess citrate affects clotting): 1.8 mL divided by 0.3 mL gives 6, so the tube holds far more citrate per volume of blood than intended. Too much citrate binds the calcium added in the test and lengthens clotting times, so the sample must be rejected. Reporting with a comment or removing blood does not restore the correct ratio.
  11. The correct answer is B (Mix gently during collection and finish quickly, so the EDTA stops platelets clumping and clotting): Capillary blood begins to clot as soon as it leaves the finger, so the tube must be filled promptly and mixed by gentle inversion while collecting. This lets the EDTA reach the blood before platelets aggregate. Vigorous shaking can cause hemolysis and heparin does not preserve platelets better than EDTA.
  12. The correct answer is D (Hold both results, verify identity against the requisitions, and report the discrepancy to the supervisor): A possible identification error invalidates both results until identity is confirmed against the requisitions and card labels, and it must be reported through the incident process. Repeating the analysis cannot fix a labelling problem because the same card would be tested again. Guessing from family history risks giving a baby a wrong diagnosis or a missed disease.
  13. The correct answer is B (Repeat with a fresh vial, and if it still fails, recalibrate and then verify again): The 168 g/L result is 12 g/L low, 6.7 percent below the assigned value and outside the 3 percent limit, so the verification failed at the high level. Because the material was opened weeks ago, deterioration or contamination must be excluded first with a fresh vial. If it still fails, recalibrate using the calibrator and verify again. Changing the acceptance limit or ignoring the failure would hide a possible reporting error.
  14. The correct answer is B (A delta check rule, because the fall from yesterday's result exceeds the laboratory's allowed change limit): A delta check compares a new result with the patient's previous one and holds it when the change exceeds a set limit, which fits a fall from 396 to 48 in a day. The count is inside the linear range, so a linearity rule did not apply. Critical and reference rules would also hold many other results that the laboratory autoverifies routinely.
  15. The correct answer is C (Withhold the result, notify the supervisor and request recollection, because specimen identity is in doubt): A suspected labelling mix-up makes the identity of the specimen unreliable, so no result from either tube should be released. Repeat analysis only proves the analyzer is consistent, not that the blood belongs to the named patient. Relabelling by comparing results is an unacceptable way to establish identity.
  16. The correct answer is A (Reduced erythropoietin production by the damaged kidneys, so the marrow is not stimulated to respond): Normocytic anemia with a low reticulocyte count indicates a hypoproliferative marrow. In kidney disease, erythropoietin production falls so red cell production is under-stimulated. The other causes are hemolytic and should show reticulocytosis.
  17. The correct answer is D (Supporting instrument data and QC were not kept for the required period, so verification fails): The reported value alone does not prove the result was valid; the raw data and QC from that run must be retained for the required period and survive an instrument replacement. Keeping only the final report leaves the laboratory unable to verify it. Replacing an analyzer is acceptable, but discarding its records without archiving them is not.
  18. The correct answer is D (Report each case formally and ask the committee for a workstation hazard assessment): Several staff with similar symptoms point to a workplace hazard, so each case is formally reported and the joint health and safety committee is asked for an ergonomic hazard assessment, which can propose changes that fit the budget. Private management leaves the cause unaddressed, and abandoning duties skips the internal process. Waiting a year delays prevention.
  19. The correct answer is B (125 x10^9/L, because 25 x 100 divided by 0.02 mm3 counted volume gives 125,000 per uL): Platelets per microlitre equal the count times the dilution divided by the volume counted. The counted volume is 0.2 mm2 x 0.1 mm = 0.02 mm3, so 25 x 100 / 0.02 = 125,000 per uL, which is 125 x10^9/L. The other values come from using the wrong dilution factor or the wrong counted volume.
  20. The correct answer is D (CBC and differential results, collection time and any clinical history, because context guides interpretation): Morphology is interpreted together with counts, indices, timing and the clinical picture, so the reviewer needs the current CBC, differential, collection time and any history supplied. Sending the slide alone or withholding current values makes review less accurate. The bench technologist describes findings but does not supply a diagnosis or treatment advice.
Case CHB11

A 28-year-old woman presents to an emergency department with confusion, petechiae and dark urine. CBC: hemoglobin 71 g/L, platelets 12 x10^9/L, WBC 10.2 x10^9/L, reticulocytes 9.8 percent. The film shows frequent schistocytes and polychromasia, with scarce platelets. PT 12.1 s (11 to 14), aPTT 29 s (25 to 35), fibrinogen 3.4 g/L (2.0 to 4.0). LDH is markedly raised and the DAT is negative.

  1. The correct answer is C (Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia with severe thrombocytopenia and normal coagulation, a pattern typical of TTP): Schistocytes, polychromasia and a raised LDH show red cells are being fragmented in the small vessels, and the negative DAT excludes immune hemolysis. Normal PT, aPTT and fibrinogen separate this from DIC, where clotting factors are consumed. Severe thrombocytopenia with fragmentation and normal coagulation points to TTP.
  2. The correct answer is D (Telephone the result to the responsible physician right away, following the critical value policy): TTP is a medical emergency that needs urgent plasma exchange, so a platelet count this low with schistocytes meets critical-result notification under the laboratory's policy. Documenting the name of the person notified and the time is part of the call. Waiting for routine release, a pathologist signature or a repeat tomorrow would delay treatment.
  3. The correct answer is D (Check the film's feathered edge for platelet clumps and estimate the platelet count): EDTA can cause platelet clumping, which makes the analyzer undercount and gives a false thrombocytopenia. The feathered edge is where clumps collect, and the film also shows whether platelets are truly scarce, as expected here. Warming helps for cold agglutinins, not clumping, and dilution corrections do not detect a spurious count.
  4. The correct answer is B (Examine a film for platelet clumps, because the raised tail points to a false count): A platelet curve that fails to return to baseline suggests clumps, giant platelets or fragments counted in the wrong size range, so the automated count may be spurious. A stained film shows clumps or large platelets directly. Releasing the value unchecked could trigger an unnecessary transfusion or work-up, and dilution does not break up clumps.
  5. The correct answer is B (The 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles, because 2.5 percent of values lie beyond each limit): A central 95 percent interval leaves 2.5 percent of healthy values below the lower limit and 2.5 percent above the upper limit. The 5th and 95th percentiles would bound only the central 90 percent. The observed extremes depend on a few unusual donors, and the interquartile range covers only half of the population.
  6. The correct answer is C (12.0 x10^9/L, because 15.0 x 100 divided by 125 removes the nucleated red cells): The corrected count is the reported count multiplied by 100 and divided by 100 plus the nucleated red cells per 100 white cells, so 15.0 x 100 / 125 equals 12.0 x10^9/L. Subtracting 25 percent gives 11.25, which overcorrects because the nucleated cells number 25 per 100 white cells, not 25 percent of the total. Adding them instead would raise the count further, the opposite of what the correction requires.
  7. The correct answer is D (0.7 x10^9/L, because nine squares at 0.1 mm depth hold a volume of 0.9 uL): The counted volume equals the area counted times the 0.1 mm depth. Nine large squares cover 9 mm2, so the volume is 0.9 uL, and 63 x 10 / 0.9 equals 700 per uL, or 0.7 x10^9/L. The 1.6 value used 4 mm2, which applies only when the four corner squares were counted.
  8. The correct answer is B (Platelet satellitism around neutrophils, because adhering platelets are not counted as separate particles): In platelet satellitism, platelets adhere in a ring around neutrophils in EDTA blood, so the analyzer does not count them as free platelets and the result is falsely low. Fragments raise rather than lower a platelet count, and rouleaux or reactive lymphocytes do not trap platelets in this way.
  9. The correct answer is C (B lymphoid lineage, because strong CD19, CD10 and CD22 outweigh a weak aberrant CD13): Lineage is assigned by the pattern of lineage-defining markers, and CD19, CD10 and CD22 with negative myeloperoxidase point firmly to B lymphoid cells. Weak CD13 is a known aberrant expression in B lymphoblasts and is not enough for a myeloid or mixed assignment. CD3 is negative, which excludes T lineage.
  10. The correct answer is D (Platelet phospholipid released on freezing neutralized the inhibitor, so a false negative result is possible): Lupus anticoagulant testing needs platelet-poor plasma, usually below 10 x10^9/L, obtained by double centrifugation. Residual platelets release phospholipid when frozen, which can neutralize a lupus inhibitor and produce a false negative. The venom is added by the laboratory and is not in the plasma.
  11. The correct answer is D (Any specimen may carry infectious agents, because infection status is often unknown or unrecorded on arrival): Routine practices treat all blood and body fluids as potentially infectious because many carriers have no symptoms and no diagnosis on the requisition. Relying on a high-risk label misses unrecognized infections. Fewer patients does not remove the risk from one specimen, and blood is a main route for hepatitis B, hepatitis C and HIV.
  12. The correct answer is A (Heparin contamination is likely, so recollect from the opposite arm and annotate the first result): Drawing from the arm with the infusion can contaminate the sample with heparin, which massively prolongs the aPTT and also lengthens the PT and lowers the Clauss fibrinogen. Recollecting from the opposite arm resolves the problem. DIC, reagent failure and factor deficiency are less likely given the collection site and the pattern.
  13. The correct answer is A (Warm the tube at 37 C, remix it and rerun, because heat reverses agglutination): The high MCV and MCHC with a low RBC are classic for clumps counted as single large cells. Warming the same tube at 37 C usually disperses the agglutinates so a valid rerun is possible. Reporting the spurious indices misleads the clinician, and cold or room temperature handling keeps the antibody active.
  14. The correct answer is B (11.2 x10^9/L, because the count is multiplied by 100 and divided by 125): The corrected count equals the analyzer WBC times 100 divided by 100 plus the nRBC count per 100 WBC, so 14.0 x 100 / 125 gives 11.2 x10^9/L. Subtracting 25 percent of the total, or multiplying by 125 over 100, uses the wrong relationship. The stem states that the instrument count still includes the nucleated red cells.
  15. The correct answer is B (Run a fresh vial of control, because deteriorated or mishandled control is the simplest cause): Troubleshooting starts with the simplest, cheapest causes. Control material that has been open for days can evaporate or deteriorate, so a fresh vial quickly shows whether the instrument or the control is at fault. Recalibration, reagent replacement and a service call are later steps that waste time and may mask the true cause.
  16. The correct answer is A (Recollect in sodium citrate, recount, and multiply by 1.1 to correct for dilution): Clumps along the feathered edge mean the analyzer count of 38 x10^9/L is falsely low, so it must not be released. Blood drawn into sodium citrate usually prevents EDTA-dependent clumping, and the result is multiplied by 1.1 because the citrate adds one part to nine of blood. Vortexing cannot reverse antibody-driven aggregation, and the film does not agree with the instrument count.
  17. The correct answer is D (An appropriate marrow response, with a peak near day 6 and a later decline): After vitamin B12 is given, the marrow releases a wave of reticulocytes that rises within days, peaks around the end of the first week and then falls as hemoglobin recovers. The curve peaks at 10.5 percent on day 6 and returns toward the reference limit. A steady climb would suggest ongoing loss, and no rise would suggest failure.
  18. The correct answer is A (Stop, remove the tourniquet, respect the refusal, and document it and inform the ordering provider): A capable adult may withdraw consent at any moment, even after preparation has begun. The technologist must stop, remove the tourniquet, respect the decision and record it with notice to the ordering provider. Continuing, repeated persuasion or a reduced collection all override the refusal.
  19. The correct answer is D (Cold agglutinins clumping red cells, so the analyzer undercounts cells and overestimates the mean volume): Cold agglutinins cause red cells to clump at room temperature, so clumps are counted as single large particles. The RBC falls, the MCV rises, and because hematocrit is calculated from them, the MCHC becomes falsely high. Lipemia raises hemoglobin readings but does not produce clumps on the film.
  20. The correct answer is D (Notify the ward and supervisor, issue a corrected report and document it, because accuracy protects patients): A released error must be corrected promptly and communicated so no clinical decision rests on the wrong value. A silent edit leaves anyone who already saw the number uninformed and hides the event from quality review. Waiting for an audit or for someone to ask delays the correction without good reason.
  21. The correct answer is B (It lets red cells lyse so parasites concentrate and low-level infection is easily detected): A thick film is dehemoglobinized during staining, so many layers of blood are concentrated into a small area, which makes scanty parasites easier to find. The thin film is fixed to preserve intact red cells and parasite shape. Methanol would stop the lysis that makes the thick film sensitive.
  22. The correct answer is A (16.1 x10^9/L, because the total count is multiplied by 100 and divided by 112): Nucleated red cells are counted as white cells by many analyzers, so the count is corrected: corrected WBC = WBC x 100 / (100 + nRBC per 100 WBC). Here 18.0 x 100 / 112 gives 16.1 x10^9/L. Multiplying by 112 over 100 would raise the count instead of correcting it, and using the nRBC percent directly does not apply the formula.
  23. The correct answer is B (Drug-induced agranulocytosis from marrow suppression or immune destruction, which requires stopping the drug): The patient began an antithyroid drug three weeks before an abrupt neutropenia with fever, a classic timing for drug-induced agranulocytosis, and the red cells and platelets are normal. Vitamin B12 deficiency and aplastic anemia would involve other cell lines, and benign ethnic neutropenia does not drop to 0.2 x10^9/L. The drug should be stopped and the clinician informed.
  24. The correct answer is D (Sickle cell disease is likely, because HbS is present and normal HbA production is absent): In a newborn, HbF dominates and a little HbA is normally present. A pattern of HbF plus HbS with no HbA means no normal beta chains are made, which fits sickle cell disease (HbSS or HbS with beta zero thalassemia). Trait would show HbA larger than HbS, and thalassemia major would show no HbS. The result needs prompt confirmatory testing.
  25. The correct answer is A (Wipe away the first drop, because tissue fluid contamination distorts blood cell counts): The initial drop is contaminated with tissue fluid and often with alcohol and activated platelets, so it is wiped away with sterile gauze. Collecting subsequent free-flowing drops gives a more representative sample for the cell counts. Warming improves flow but does not remove tissue fluid contamination from the surface drop.
  26. The correct answer is A (Plus 3.0 SD, a clear systematic shift that requires investigation before reporting patient results): The shift is 146 minus 140, which is 6 g/L, and dividing by the standard deviation of 2 g/L gives plus 3.0 SD. Ten consecutive results on one side also violate the 10-x rule. The smaller values come from dividing by the wrong number, and 12 comes from multiplying instead of dividing.
  27. The correct answer is D (Upright posture moves plasma water into tissue, concentrating cells, whereas recumbency returns fluid to vessels): When a person stands or sits, hydrostatic pressure pushes water and small molecules out of the vessels, so cells and proteins become more concentrated. Lying down lets that fluid return and the hemoglobin falls by roughly 5 to 10 percent. The controls were in range, so analytical drift is not the cause, and red cells are neither destroyed nor released from marrow within minutes.
  28. The correct answer is C (Target cells, because excess membrane surface relative to volume produces a central hemoglobin bull's-eye): Target cells show a central dark spot, a clear ring and a dense rim, and they form when membrane surface area is excessive for the cell volume, as in liver disease. Stomatocytes have a slit, spherocytes lack pallor, and acanthocytes carry spicules, so none of these matches the bull's-eye pattern.
  29. The correct answer is B (Telephone the result to the responsible nurse, request read-back, and document the call): A value past the critical limit must be communicated directly to the person responsible for the patient, with read-back to confirm it was understood, and the call must be documented with who, when and what. A flag on the chart or an email does not ensure that anyone acts quickly. Repeating the test is not needed because the sample was properly collected and is not clotted.
  30. The correct answer is B (Light blue citrate tube, spun promptly to platelet-poor plasma, because platelets can neutralize heparin): Anti-Xa testing uses citrated plasma, which should be centrifuged promptly to platelet-poor plasma because platelets release platelet factor 4, which neutralizes heparin. EDTA and serum tubes are not valid for coagulation methods. A heparin tube would add heparin to the sample and distort the result.
  31. The correct answer is C (Light blue is filled before green heparin, so heparin cannot carry into it): The chart lists the light blue citrate tube second and the green heparin tube fourth, so citrate is filled before heparin and cannot pick up heparin from the needle or holder. Filling green first would carry heparin into the citrate tube, and EDTA before citrate would carry EDTA, which binds calcium and invalidates coagulation tests. Grey tubes are filled last in the chart.
  32. The correct answer is D (Draws before 0745 are past 6 hours and need recollection, but later ones are acceptable): Six hours before 1345 is 0745, so any specimen collected earlier has exceeded the room-temperature limit and must be recollected. Specimens drawn later are still within limits. The length of the downtime does not matter, only the time since collection.
  33. The correct answer is D (An inhibitor interferes, because dilution weakens it and the calculated activity rises with dilution): A true factor level gives activity that stays about the same at each dilution after correcting for the dilution factor. An inhibitor is diluted out, so the calculated activity climbs with dilution. This non-parallel pattern warns the technologist that the low result is unreliable and needs follow-up.
  34. The correct answer is B (A written record of every handler, with dates, times and signatures): Chain of custody is the written record showing who held a specimen, when and where it changed hands, so that the specimen can be shown to be untouched. A temperature log or a repeat verification addresses quality, not custody. Consent for release is a separate privacy matter.
  35. The correct answer is C (The date, time, error message, affected specimens and any action taken, written when it occurred): Equipment records capture what happened as it happened: date, time, error message, specimens affected and actions taken. This supports traceability and accreditation review. Cost, a guess at the cause or a shift-log-only note does not meet record requirements, and laboratory staff on duty document the failure themselves.
  36. The correct answer is A (30.1 x10^9/L, from 62 percent of the total WBC of 48.6 x10^9/L): The absolute count is the percentage multiplied by the total WBC and divided by 100, so 62 x 48.6 / 100 gives 30.1 x10^9/L. A percentage is not a count, and the total WBC includes other cell types. A misplaced decimal would understate the blast burden by a factor of ten.
  37. The correct answer is D (Decline the add-on and ask for a new specimen, because the tube exceeded the stability limit): The tube is one hour past the stated stability limit, so the D-dimer may no longer reflect the patient's level and the add-on is declined. Comments, cut-off changes and cooling do not restore a degraded analyte. A new specimen is requested promptly because the patient has chest pain and the result may guide care.
  38. The correct answer is D (The very low absolute reticulocyte count, because the marrow releases almost no new cells): A reticulocyte percentage of 0.2 percent with an absolute count of 4.7 x10^9/L (reference 25 to 100) shows the marrow is not replacing red cells despite severe anemia. In blood loss or hemolysis the count would be raised. Pancytopenia with a normal MCV and no blasts fits marrow failure, but the reticulocyte count is the direct measure of red cell production.
  39. The correct answer is A (Push forward in one smooth, steady motion, because a pause leaves ridges across the film): The spreader should glide in one continuous motion once the blood has run along its edge, since a pause or jerk produces ridges and uneven cell distribution. Rocking or pressing damages cells and ruins the feathered edge, and lifting the spreader lets the drop begin to clot.
  40. The correct answer is C (Inspect the plasma for hemolysis or lipemia and recollect, because this MCHC is not plausible): Hemoglobin 148 g/L divided by hematocrit 0.32 L/L gives an MCHC of 463 g/L, far above the ceiling near 360 g/L, and the MCH of 43.5 pg is equally implausible. This pattern signals an interference such as hemolysis or lipemia, so the sample must be checked and recollected. A hematocrit of 0.32 L/L is not a critical value, and diagnosing a disease from one report is outside scope.
  41. The correct answer is B (Carryover is 0.24 percent, which meets the 1.0 percent limit, because 0.6 is divided by 249.7): The numerator is the first low minus the third low: 0.9 minus 0.3 is 0.6. The denominator is the third high minus the third low: 250 minus 0.3 is 249.7. Dividing 0.6 by 249.7 and multiplying by 100 gives about 0.24 percent, below the 1.0 percent limit, so the analyzer passes. Leaving out the subtraction gives 0.36 percent, which is an incorrect value.

Practice Test 15 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (87.5 percent agree, so it fails, because 35 of 40 is below 90 percent): If 5 of 40 specimens exceeded the limit, then 35 agreed, and 35 divided by 40 is 87.5 percent. That is below the required 90 percent, so the comparison does not meet its criterion and the discrepancies must be investigated. The 12.5 percent figure is the proportion that disagreed, not the proportion that agreed.
  2. The correct answer is A (Rebook for morning, because serum iron peaks early and falls through the day): Serum iron has a marked diurnal rhythm, with the highest values in the morning and lower values later in the day. The earlier results were all morning samples, so an afternoon value could look like a change that is only timing. No fixed correction factor exists, and eating beforehand would add yet another variable.
  3. The correct answer is A (Let the chamber rest for about five minutes so that the cells settle onto the grid): Cells need a few minutes to settle onto the ruled surface so that they lie in one focal plane and the count reflects the true number in the chamber volume. Counting while they drift gives uneven, unreliable counts. Drying the chamber or adding stain distorts or alters the cells rather than helping them settle.
  4. The correct answer is C (Two red cell populations of different sizes are averaging out to give a normal MCV): The film shows both microcytes and macrocytes, so the mean volume falls in the normal range while the RDW is very wide, which is the classic picture of combined deficiency such as iron with B12 or folate. A normal MCV does not mean the cells are uniform. The RDW does not invalidate the MCV.
  5. The correct answer is C (A workplace label giving product name, safe handling information, and a safety data sheet reference): A container filled from a supplier container and used by several people needs a workplace label with the product identifier, safe handling information and a reference to the safety data sheet. The jar is not exempt because it is in the same room, and a pictogram or date alone does not identify the product. The label must stay readable so that any worker can identify the hazard.
  6. The correct answer is B (Enzyme activity has faded with smear age, so repeat the stains on a fresh smear): Neutrophils are the internal control, and their failure to stain shows the enzymes have decayed in the stored smear. The results cannot be used to assign lineage. Auer rods are myeloperoxidase positive and point to a myeloid process, so the stains must be repeated on a fresh smear.
  7. The correct answer is A (Pregnancy lowers free protein S, so inherited deficiency cannot be diagnosed now; retest postpartum): Free protein S falls physiologically during pregnancy, so a low value at 28 weeks cannot establish an inherited deficiency. Protein C is normal and the patient is receiving low molecular weight heparin rather than warfarin. Protein S assays remain valid in pregnancy, so rejection is not needed, and testing should be repeated after delivery.
  8. The correct answer is B (Give a structured written handover of pending work, because that prevents lost results): A structured written handover lists pending work, instrument status and reagent problems so the next team can act without guessing. Relying on logs or a bench note leaves gaps, and handing over only the STAT samples hides the reagent shortage that could delay later testing.
  9. The correct answer is B (Implied consent is acceptable for routine collection, provided the patient understood what was explained): Implied consent applies when a capable patient understands what is explained and cooperates, as by rolling up a sleeve. It is accepted for routine venipuncture. Written forms, a nurse witness or the fact of admission are not required to make this consent valid.
  10. The correct answer is C (The rise is about 195 percent, so the film and chart are reviewed before release): The rise is 28.0 minus 9.5, divided by 9.5, which is about 195 percent, well above the 100 percent limit, so the result is checked against the film and clinical history before release. A value of 66 percent comes from using today's count as the base, and 295 percent is the ratio rather than the change. Delta rules apply to white cell counts as well.
  11. The correct answer is A (Absolute monocytosis, expected in chronic granulomatous infection because monocytes are recruited to granulomas): An absolute monocyte count of 1.3 x10^9/L exceeds the reference interval, so true monocytosis is present. Chronic infections such as tuberculosis, with granuloma formation, are a classic association. A normal or relative-only interpretation ignores that the absolute count is what defines monocytosis.
  12. The correct answer is C (Nucleated red blood cells, because a dense round nucleus sits within hemoglobinized cytoplasm): Nucleated red cells have a round, dense nucleus and hemoglobinized pink to orange cytoplasm, and they are common in neonates and in hemolytic disease. Small lymphocytes have blue cytoplasm, Howell-Jolly bodies are small inclusions inside an otherwise mature red cell, and platelets lack a nucleus.
  13. The correct answer is D (Prussian blue stain, because potassium ferrocyanide in acid turns ferric iron granules a blue colour): Pappenheimer bodies contain iron, so they react with acid ferrocyanide to form blue ferric ferrocyanide on a Prussian blue stain. PAS detects glycogen, Sudan black B detects lipid in granules and a supravital stain highlights RNA in reticulocytes, none of which confirms iron.
  14. The correct answer is B (Stop reporting patient platelet results, because an uncontrolled system cannot be trusted by clinicians): When a control fails, the system is not demonstrated to be in control, so patient results for that parameter are held until the cause is found and corrected. Repeating until a value passes hides a real problem. Releasing results with a comment or recalibrating before investigating skips the troubleshooting that has to come first.
  15. The correct answer is C (Write the name and identifier in pencil on the frosted end, because pencil resists solvents): Graphite from a pencil is not dissolved by methanol or aqueous Romanowsky stains, so it remains readable. Ink and marker can wash off and a paper label over the smear area blocks the film. Bed location alone cannot identify a patient.
  16.
