The correct answer is B (About 50 percent, which is enough to correct the aPTT into the reference interval): Mixing equal volumes of a plasma with about 0 percent activity and one with 100 percent gives roughly 50 percent, and a clotting factor level of about 30 to 50 percent is enough to normalize the aPTT. A 100 percent result would require no dilution, and 25 percent or 1 percent are not what a one to one mix of these plasmas produces.
  17. The correct answer is A (Invert gently eight to ten times immediately after collection, avoiding shaking that damages red cells): Eight to ten gentle inversions spread the EDTA through the blood and prevent microclots without damaging cells. Hard shaking can cause hemolysis and foaming, while too little mixing leaves clots that falsely lower counts. EDTA does not mix reliably by diffusion alone.
  18. The correct answer is C (Hold the slides and have the physician confirm identity and relabel, because marrow is irreplaceable): Marrow is an irreplaceable specimen, so the slides are held and the physician who collected it confirms the patient identity and relabels them. Discarding the set is premature, staining only the legible slides risks a misidentified result, and relabelling from the tube label without verification is not acceptable practice.
  19. The correct answer is D (Platelet factor 4 neutralizes heparin and falsely shortens the aPTT, so reject and recollect): Platelets in an uncentrifuged tube release platelet factor 4, which binds and neutralizes heparin, so the aPTT is falsely shortened and could lead to an unsafe rate increase. The sample is no longer acceptable and a new one should be collected. Heparin does not degrade in the tube, and a comment cannot make an unreliable result usable.
  20. The correct answer is D (Rare cell types have large sampling error in a 100-cell count, because few are counted): When only 100 cells are counted, a cell type present at about 1 percent contributes one or two cells, so chance alone changes the percentage noticeably. Analyzers classify thousands of cells and therefore have less sampling error for rare types. Staining time and granule detection do not account for a one-cell difference.
  21. The correct answer is C (Objective facts of what happened, when it was found, and what immediate actions were taken): An incident form should record facts: what occurred, when and how it was discovered, and the immediate actions. Blame and speculation distort the review and discourage reporting under a just culture approach. The full chart is not needed and raises privacy concerns, since only information needed for the review should be included under provincial health privacy law.
  22. The correct answer is D (A supravital stain such as new methylene blue applied to fresh unfixed blood): Heinz bodies are denatured hemoglobin that is invisible on Romanowsky-stained films, so a supravital stain such as new methylene blue or crystal violet on fresh blood is needed. Prussian blue detects iron, which Heinz bodies do not contain. The Kleihauer-Betke method looks for fetal hemoglobin and is not used for this purpose.
  23. The correct answer is D (About pH 6.8 to 6.9, because it balances acidic and basic dye binding to cells): Romanowsky dyes combine acidic and basic components, and their binding to nuclei, cytoplasm and hemoglobin depends on pH. A buffer near 6.8 to 6.9 gives the expected pink red cells and blue-purple nuclei. A much lower pH shifts colour toward pink and a higher pH toward blue, so unbuffered water gives unreliable colour.
  24. The correct answer is C (Note the aspirate as hemodiluted and inform the hematologist, because the trephine may be needed): Pale smears without spicules and with a mature peripheral-type cell trail suggest the aspirate is diluted with blood. Cellularity cannot be judged from such a sample, so the hematologist must be told and may rely on the trephine or a repeat pull. Reporting hypocellularity would be a false conclusion, and discarding the slides removes information the hematologist can still use.
  25. The correct answer is C (It spins cells onto a small area of the slide, concentrating them for a readable differential): A cytocentrifuge spins a small volume of low cellularity fluid so the cells are deposited in a thin circle on the slide, which makes a differential practical. It does not layer cells by density, fix them with heat or lyse red cells. The slide is still air dried and stained afterward.
  26. The correct answer is A (Leakproof tube, absorbent material, secondary container and a rigid outer box marked UN3373): Diagnostic specimens shipped by road are normally treated as Biological Substance, Category B and need triple packaging: a leakproof primary container, absorbent material and a secondary container, all inside a rigid outer box marked UN3373. Bags, envelopes and unmarked coolers do not contain leaks or identify the hazard.
  27. The correct answer is D (The average is 93.6 fL, which exceeds the limit, so analyzer performance must be investigated): The mean is 1872 divided by 20, which equals 93.6 fL, a deviation of 3.6 fL from the 90 fL target and beyond the 3 fL limit. A shift appearing after a reagent change suggests a reagent or calibration problem that morning controls may not reveal. The group mean is judged against the moving average limit, not against individual reference limits.
  28. The correct answer is B (Acute toxicity, so swallowing or skin contact could be fatal, which calls for careful handling): A skull and crossbones is the acute toxicity pictogram, used for products that can cause serious harm or death after a small exposure by mouth, skin or inhalation. Corrosion is a different symbol showing liquid burning a hand and a metal bar. The label therefore signals strict care in handling and disposal, not a pressure or irritation hazard.
  29. The correct answer is C (Display each historical result with the reference interval that applied at its collection date): A hemoglobin that was normal for a nine-year-old can look low against an adult range, so each historical value must be shown with the interval used at the time. Replacing it with one adult range would misflag children's results. Removing flags or hiding earlier values loses the information the cumulative report exists to provide.
  30. The correct answer is C (Clean the eyepiece lens with lens paper, because debris on it rotates when it is turned): A spot that stays fixed when the slide moves and the objective changes, but turns with the eyepiece, is on the eyepiece. Dirt on the slide would move with the slide, and dirt on an objective would change with the objective used. Condenser dust is usually out of focus and does not rotate with an eyepiece.
  31. The correct answer is A (10.0 x10^9/L, calculated as 14.0 x 100 divided by 140 after the film review): Nucleated red cells are counted as white cells by most analyzers, so the count is corrected by dividing by 100 plus the nRBC per 100 white cells: 14.0 x 100 / 140 gives 10.0 x10^9/L. Multiplying by 1.4 inflates the count, and multiplying by 0.40 removes too much, because the nRBC fraction is 40 of 140 cells rather than 40 percent.
  32. The correct answer is C (Put all three into the rigid sharps container, because glass and lancets can puncture bags): Anything that can cut or puncture, including lancets, glass capillary tubes and broken slides, belongs in a rigid puncture-resistant sharps container, since bags and cardboard can be pierced. Splitting items by whether they are needles misses the injury risk of glass. Bleach rinsing does not make contaminated glass suitable for recycling.
  33. The correct answer is A (Factor VIII antibodies act slowly, so incubation reveals a time-dependent inhibitor the immediate mix misses): Antibodies against factor VIII neutralize the factor gradually at 37 C, so an immediate mix can look nearly corrected while the incubated mix becomes prolonged again. The extra step therefore detects time-dependent inhibitors. It does not activate factor XII, destroy heparin or generate platelet phospholipid.
  34. The correct answer is A (The laboratory medical director, because standard interpretive wording falls under medical oversight): Standard interpretive wording that will appear on many reports is reviewed and approved under the laboratory's medical oversight, usually by the medical director. One requesting physician cannot set policy for the whole laboratory. Seniority of a technologist or the manufacturer's software does not replace that approval.
  35. The correct answer is B (Describe the dysplasia and refer the film to the pathologist, because morphology alone cannot diagnose): A technologist describes what is seen and refers the film, because dysplasia may come from vitamin deficiency, drugs or a marrow disorder and morphology alone cannot decide which. Naming MDS on the report goes beyond technologist scope, and a B12 conclusion without testing is unsupported. Leaving the changes unreported hides information the pathologist needs.
  36. The correct answer is D (Direct observation of real tasks against defined criteria, recorded, because competence is shown by performance): Competency is shown by performance, so assessors watch the technologist do the actual work and score it against set criteria. Attendance, self-declaration and past employment show exposure but do not prove the person can perform this laboratory's procedures correctly.
  37. The correct answer is A (Residual heparin can mix with the sample and falsely prolong the clotting times): Heparin used to lock the lumen can remain in the line and mix with the sample, falsely prolonging the aPTT, thrombin time and sometimes the PT. The other concerns describe processes that do not occur in a catheter. Clinicians need to know that line draws are a recognized source of heparin contamination.
  38. The correct answer is C (As not interpretable, since donor cells mask the infant's own hemoglobins, with a later repeat): Donor red cells carry hemoglobin A, which hides any abnormal hemoglobin made by the infant, so a transfused sample cannot be interpreted. The laboratory reports it as not interpretable and asks for a repeat specimen after enough time has passed for the donor cells to clear. A clean-looking A pattern is not proof of health, and no calculation can separate the two cell populations.
  39. The correct answer is C (Use written notes and have him write his name and birth date to match the requisition): Written communication allows two identifiers to be confirmed accurately and respects the patient's access to care. A nod does not prove identity, and a bystander's translation breaches confidentiality. Turning him away places an unnecessary barrier on a patient who can be served.
  40. The correct answer is D (Negative, because the age-adjusted limit is 780 ng/mL FEU and 640 is below that limit): Age 78 x 10 gives an adjusted limit of 780 ng/mL FEU, and a result of 640 is below it, so it is negative under the approved low-probability protocol. The multiplier is 10, not 8, and nothing in the protocol limits it to outpatients or requires a repeat for older patients.
  41. The correct answer is C (Deficient G6PD activity, because little NADPH formed in the reaction to produce fluorescence): Active G6PD converts NADP+ to NADPH, which fluoresces under ultraviolet light, so a patient spot that stays dark has little enzyme activity. The normal control fluorescing shows that the reagents worked, so the run is valid. A dark spot is reported as deficient rather than borderline.
  42. The correct answer is B (Sealed end facing the outer rim against the gasket, because spinning force pushes cells toward it): The sealed end must point outward toward the rim so that centrifugal force packs the red cells against the clay plug and gasket. If the open end faced outward, blood would be thrown out of the tube. The orientation of the seal is therefore not interchangeable.
  43. The correct answer is D (Falling red cell counts across tubes suggest a traumatic tap rather than subarachnoid hemorrhage): Blood introduced by the needle clears as CSF continues to drip, so red cell counts fall from the first to the last tube. In true subarachnoid hemorrhage the counts stay similar across tubes. Clotting is not suggested by a declining count.
  44. The correct answer is D (MCH is hemoglobin mass per cell, while MCHC is hemoglobin concentration within the packed cells): MCH equals hemoglobin divided by RBC and expresses the average mass of hemoglobin in one cell in picograms. MCHC equals hemoglobin divided by hematocrit and expresses the concentration of hemoglobin in the packed red cells in g/L, so it corrects for cell size. The other statements swap the definitions or invent measurements.
  45. The correct answer is A (Hypochromic microcytes with pencil cells and mild anisopoikilocytosis, because iron-poor cells lack hemoglobin): Iron-deficient erythropoiesis produces small, pale cells with enlarged central pallor, and elongated pencil cells are characteristic. Macro-ovalocytes belong to megaloblastic anemia, spherocytes to hemolysis, and target cells with stippling to thalassemia or lead.
  46. The correct answer is C (Report nRBCs as a separate count per 100 WBC, outside the leukocyte percentages): The leukocyte differential is expressed as percentages that total 100 white cells. Nucleated red cells are counted alongside but reported as a number per 100 WBC so that they do not distort those percentages. Leaving them out loses a clinically useful finding in sickle cell crisis.
  47. The correct answer is B (It forms SLS-hemoglobin without cyanide, giving safer waste, and is calibrated to the reference method): The SLS method lyses the cells and converts hemoglobin to a stable SLS-hemoglobin complex, avoiding cyanide and its hazardous waste. Its results are calibrated so they agree with the cyanmethemoglobin reference method. It measures total hemoglobin from lysed cells, not free plasma hemoglobin, and it does need a lysing reagent.
  48. The correct answer is C (Pause, explain simply to the child, and use the parent's calming help before trying again): A frightened child should be given a simple explanation and calming support, with the parent helping, before another approach is tried. Forceful restraint without need causes distress and injury risk, and a quick surprise stick damages trust. Cancelling at the first protest is premature for a routine order, since most children cooperate once reassured; if distress continues, the clinician is contacted.
  49. The correct answer is B (Possible false positive, because factor Xa inhibitors prolong the dRVVT and mimic a lupus anticoagulant): Rivaroxaban inhibits factor Xa, the very factor that the venom activates in the dRVVT, so the clotting time is prolonged whether or not a lupus anticoagulant is present. The result should be flagged as possibly drug-related and retested off the drug or with a DOAC removal step. Rivaroxaban does not lower vitamin K dependent factors.
  50. The correct answer is B (Decline the add-on and request a new light blue citrate tube, because EDTA invalidates coagulation tests): Coagulation tests require citrated plasma, and EDTA binds the calcium that the clotting reactions need, so results from a lavender tube are invalid. Time since collection and physician acceptance do not change that. A new light blue tube must be collected, even though the patient will need to be called back.
  51. The correct answer is A (Rouleaux, caused by excess plasma proteins that reduce cell charge, and saline disperses it): Red cells stacked like coins are rouleaux, which form when excess immunoglobulin or fibrinogen reduces the negative surface charge that normally keeps cells apart. Saline replacement disperses rouleaux, whereas cold agglutination needs warming and forms irregular clumps. Thick areas show overlap, but a thin-area arrangement in orderly stacks is true rouleaux.
  52. The correct answer is C (Platelets settle on the grid and evaporation is prevented, so counting is consistent): Platelets are light and drift in the fluid, so a rest period in a humid dish lets them settle into one plane on the grid where they can be focused and counted. The damp paper prevents evaporation, which would change the fluid volume and the count. The waiting time is not needed for staining, hardening or dissolving other cells.
  53. The correct answer is B (The 10-x rule, ten consecutive results on one side of the mean, reflecting systematic error): By day 12 ten consecutive points sit above the mean, which violates the 10-x rule even though none is extreme. This rule is sensitive to small systematic error such as slow reagent or calibration change. The other rules need an extreme point or a wide spread, which this chart does not show.
  54. The correct answer is D (Remix the dilution, refill a clean chamber and recount, because the 24 cell spread exceeds policy): A spread of 24 cells across squares shows the cells are not evenly distributed, usually from poor mixing or a poorly filled chamber, so no square can be trusted to represent the whole. The sample is remixed and the chamber cleaned and refilled before recounting. Averaging, scaling or selecting squares only hides the problem.
  55. The correct answer is C (Clean the chamber, remix the specimen, recharge both sides, and repeat the counts before reporting): Counts from the two sides should agree closely, and a 40 percent gap with uneven distribution points to a mixing or charging fault. Both sides are recharged after cleaning and remixing. Averaging disagreeing counts hides the error, and choosing one side without cause is arbitrary.
  56. The correct answer is A (A rise in the reticulocyte count, because the marrow responds before hemoglobin increases measurably): A reticulocyte response appears within about a week of starting iron, then hemoglobin rises over the following weeks. The MCV and RDW lag for months because the old microcytic cells survive about 120 days. Ferritin recovers last, after hemoglobin has normalized.
  57. The correct answer is A (Neutrophils 58, lymphocytes 30, monocytes 7, eosinophils 4, basophils 1 percent): Each value in this differential lies inside its stated interval: 58 within 40 to 75, 30 within 20 to 45, 7 within 2 to 10, 4 within 1 to 6 and 1 within 0 to 2. The others each break at least one interval, with neutrophils at 38 and lymphocytes at 52 in one, eosinophils at 12 in another and monocytes at 15 in the third. Checking each cell line against its own interval is more reliable than judging the whole pattern by eye.
  58. The correct answer is B (3000 cells per microlitre, from 120 times dilution factor 10 divided by 0.4 microlitre): Four large squares at 0.1 mm depth hold 0.4 microlitre. The dilution is 2.0 mL total divided by 0.2 mL fluid, or 10. The count is 120 times 10 divided by 0.4, which gives 3000 cells per microlitre (3.0 x10^9/L). Using one microlitre for four squares, or a dilution factor of nine, gives the wrong totals.
  59. The correct answer is C (Collect a new, properly filled tube, because excess citrate prolongs the clotting time): Coagulation tubes depend on a nine to one ratio of blood to citrate. An underfilled tube contains excess citrate, which binds more calcium and falsely prolongs the INR. Neither a comment nor topping up from another tube restores the ratio, so a new full tube is needed.
  60. The correct answer is D (Decline the EDTA tube and request a citrate tube, because EDTA removes calcium that clotting needs): EDTA binds calcium, which the clotting cascade needs, so clotting tests on EDTA plasma are invalid and cannot be corrected with a factor or by adding calcium. Urgency does not change the science, so the technologist must explain the problem and ask for a citrate tube quickly to avoid a misleading INR.
  61. The correct answer is C (Mix the blood with albumin before spreading, because the added protein cushions fragile cells): Adding albumin, commonly bovine albumin, to the blood before spreading protects the fragile CLL lymphocytes, so fewer rupture and more intact cells can be classified. Pressure, heat or longer staining do not protect cells during the spreading step. Staining time affects colour, not cell fragility.
  62. The correct answer is C (Issue a corrected report with WBC 10.0 x10^9/L, notify the clinician, and document it): The corrected white count is 14.0 x 100 divided by 140, which equals 10.0 x10^9/L. A released result that was wrong is corrected openly with a new report and a notice to the clinician. Subtracting the percentage directly gives the wrong number, and leaving the report unchanged keeps an error in the chart.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Escalate the slide to the pathologist and phone the practitioner, because the finding needs urgent review): Many blast-like cells with severe cytopenias are a critical finding that needs same-day pathologist review and prompt notification of the practitioner. Naming a diagnosis is outside technologist scope, and delaying until the next day or until a recollection could harm the patient. Escalation and any repeat testing can proceed together.
Case CHB12

At 08:10 a laboratory released CBC results for two patients seated at the same collection station. At 10:30 the technologist finds the two tubes were labelled in the wrong order. Patient 1, a 58-year-old outpatient who has left, was reported with hemoglobin 78 g/L and platelets 41 x10^9/L, but the true results are hemoglobin 132 g/L and platelets 248 x10^9/L. Patient 2 is on the ward waiting for a transfusion decision and was reported with the normal values.

  1. The correct answer is D (Telephone both ordering providers now, state the reports are swapped, and give the true values): Patient 2 may receive or be denied a transfusion on the strength of a wrong normal result, so direct and immediate contact with both providers is the priority. Corrected reports and quality steps follow but must not delay the call. Recollecting first would add hours while the wrong values remain in use.
  2. The correct answer is D (A corrected label, the reason, the time, and both the original and corrected values): A corrected report is clearly marked as corrected, states why the change was made, shows when it was made, and keeps the original and new values visible so readers can see what changed. Hiding the earlier values or the reason breaks traceability. Naming the technologist alone does not tell clinicians what was wrong.
  3. The correct answer is B (Complete an occurrence report listing the error, time found, people notified, and corrective actions taken): An occurrence report records what happened, when it was found, who was told and what was done, so the quality system can look for the cause and prevent a repeat. A shift log note is not reviewed by the quality program. The original reports must stay linked to the corrected ones rather than being moved away.
  4. The correct answer is B (Count the cell tube, plate the culture and comment on refrigeration, because fragile organisms may die): The cell count tube was handled correctly and must be counted promptly before cells lyse. The culture can still be plated, but cold exposure may have killed fragile organisms such as meningococci, so a comment warns that a negative result may be unreliable. Rejecting both tubes loses valid information.
  5. The correct answer is C (Uniformly microcytic cells, because a narrow peak shifted left shows small, similar cell volumes): The horizontal axis of a red cell histogram is cell volume in fL, so a peak displaced to the left means the mean cell volume is reduced. A narrow peak means little variation in size, so the cells are uniformly small. Anisocytosis would widen the base of the curve and raise the RDW.
  6. The correct answer is D (Warm the specimen to 37 C and repeat, because cold agglutinins falsely lower the RBC): The MCHC of 407 g/L is physiologically implausible and the red cell count is spuriously low because clumps are counted as single cells, which also inflates the MCV. Cold agglutinins cause this, and warming the specimen to 37 C then reanalyzing gives reliable values. Megaloblastic change does not produce a high MCHC, and citrate does not prevent cold agglutination.
  7. The correct answer is A (Exertion and emotional stress release marginated neutrophils into circulation, transiently raising the count): Physical exertion and strong emotion raise catecholamines, which shift neutrophils from the vessel wall (the marginated pool) into the flowing blood. This causes a short-lived, mild leukocytosis that settles after rest. A symptom-free patient with a normal differential does not point to infection, and EDTA does not make neutrophils appear in the tube.
  8. The correct answer is C (Its retention time within a defined window, because each variant elutes at a characteristic time): Cation exchange HPLC separates hemoglobins by charge as they pass through a column, and each variant elutes in a reproducible time window. Retention time gives a presumptive name, and percent area gives the amount. Migration distance belongs to electrophoresis, and peak height by itself does not identify a variant.
  9. The correct answer is B (Plasma cells, because the eccentric nucleus, deep basophilic cytoplasm and perinuclear halo suit a myeloma population): Plasma cells have an eccentric round nucleus, deeply basophilic cytoplasm and a pale perinuclear zone where the Golgi apparatus sits. Large numbers of them, together with anemia and high calcium, fit multiple myeloma. Lymphoblasts and reactive lymphocytes lack the combination of eccentric nucleus and perinuclear clearing, and promyelocytes have visible granules.
  10. The correct answer is C (Introduce herself, use the patient's preferred name and explain each step in plain language): Respectful care starts with introducing yourself, using the name the patient prefers and describing each step in plain language, even when the patient has dementia. Pet names, commands and talking only to the nurse treat the patient as an object instead of a person.
  11. The correct answer is D (110 x10^9/L, from 22 times 100 divided by a counted volume of 0.02 microlitre): Five small squares of 0.04 mm2 give 0.2 mm2, and at a depth of 0.1 mm the counted volume is 0.02 microlitre. The count per microlitre is 22 times 100 divided by 0.02, or 110 000, which is 110 x10^9/L. Using a larger or smaller counted volume shifts the result by a factor of ten or more.
  12. The correct answer is D (Use a newborn interval, since hemoglobin and hematocrit are normally higher at birth than later): Fetal hemoglobin and a higher red cell mass make newborn hemoglobin and hematocrit higher than adult values, so 175 g/L and 0.54 L/L fit a typical newborn interval. Adult female limits would flag a normal infant, and the male range is not the right comparison either. Laboratories partition intervals by age so each result is judged against the right population.
  13. The correct answer is B (Red cells swell during prolonged storage in EDTA, which falsely raises the MCV and hematocrit): Red cells slowly take up water in EDTA at room temperature, so the MCV climbs and the calculated hematocrit rises with it. The effect is a storage artifact, not a patient change, which is why CBC samples should be tested within the stability limit of the laboratory. Platelet satellitism affects the platelet count, not the cell volume.
  14. The correct answer is B (Pink to salmon red cells, blue-purple nuclei, and orange-red granules in eosinophils): Correct Romanowsky staining gives pink to salmon red cells, blue-purple nuclei with visible chromatin, and orange-red eosinophil granules. A film that departs from these colours points to a problem with stain pH, timing or reagent quality. Nuclei that lack colour or cells that look blue-green or grey indicate faulty staining.
  15. The correct answer is A (Arrange an interpreter, and use writing and gestures meanwhile, because clear understanding is required): A qualified interpreter gives clear, accurate communication and meets the need for understanding before consent. Writing and gestures can bridge the gap until the interpreter arrives. A few signs, a hearing relative or silence do not ensure the patient understands.
  16. The correct answer is B (It compares reagent sensitivity with an international reference, and values nearer 1.0 are more sensitive): The ISI is a calibration factor that relates a reagent's response to an international reference thromboplastin whose ISI is 1.0. A lower value means a more sensitive reagent, and it is the exponent used to convert a prothrombin time ratio to an INR. It does not provide normal times, lot differences or warfarin dosing targets.
  17. The correct answer is B (15.1 x10^9/L, because 18.4 multiplied by 100 and divided by 122 removes the nucleated red cells): The analyzer counted nucleated red cells as leukocytes, so the corrected count is 18.4 multiplied by 100 and divided by 122, which equals 15.1 x10^9/L. Subtracting 22 percent directly or multiplying by 1.22 uses the wrong formula. Leaving the count uncorrected overstates the white cell count.
  18. The correct answer is A (Leave the room, warn others, close the door, and call the emergency number for help): A large spill with strong fumes and symptoms of exposure is beyond local clean-up, so the technologist leaves, warns others, closes the door and calls for emergency help. Staying in the fumes risks collapse, and towels cannot absorb that volume. Bleach does not neutralize methanol and fanning vapour spreads a flammable hazard.
  19. The correct answer is D (Crescent-shaped cells, confirmed by hemoglobin electrophoresis or HPLC showing hemoglobin S): Elongated crescent-shaped cells in a child with pain crises and hemolysis point to hemoglobin S, which is confirmed by electrophoresis or HPLC. The other pairings describe membrane, thalassemia or G6PD disorders, each of which has a different film and a different confirmatory test.
  20. The correct answer is C (Report a corrected WBC of 5.0 x10^9/L, and state the nRBC count per 100 WBC): The corrected WBC is 8.0 x 100 / 160 = 5.0 x10^9/L, and the nRBC count should accompany it. The flag only signals a problem and does not correct the number. Subtracting 60 percent or adding the nRBCs both use the wrong arithmetic.
  21. The correct answer is C (Warm to room temperature, mix, then run both, because the cold tube is within policy limits): Refrigeration extends the allowed time for the ESR to 24 hours, and the CBC is acceptable too. The tube must be brought to room temperature and mixed before testing because cold blood sediments more slowly. Rejecting either test would ignore the stated policy.
  22. The correct answer is D (Bernard-Soulier syndrome, because absent glycoprotein Ib-IX-V prevents von Willebrand factor from binding the platelet surface): Normal aggregation with ADP and collagen but none with ristocetin, large platelets, thrombocytopenia and no correction with normal plasma describe Bernard-Soulier syndrome, caused by absent GPIb-IX-V. Glanzmann shows the opposite pattern, type 2B shows an enhanced ristocetin response, and gray platelet syndrome has no ristocetin defect.
  23. The correct answer is B (40 degrees, because it gives a film length of about 41 mm, nearest the target): Reading the figure, the film length falls steadily as the angle rises, and 40 degrees gives about 41 mm, nearest the 40 mm target. At 30 degrees the film is about 50 mm long, while 50 and 60 degrees give 33 and 26 mm, which are too short.
  24. The correct answer is B (Prolonged PT and aPTT with low fibrinogen favour DIC, since TTP leaves clotting tests normal): DIC consumes clotting factors and fibrinogen, so PT and aPTT are prolonged and fibrinogen is low. TTP is a platelet and VWF disorder in which PT, aPTT and fibrinogen stay normal despite fragmented red cells. Sepsis commonly triggers DIC, and neither liver disease nor heparin contamination would explain the platelet fall and fragments together.
  25. The correct answer is D (1.25 mL platelet-rich plasma plus 0.75 mL platelet-poor plasma, giving 250 x10^9/L in 2.0 mL): The volume of platelet-rich plasma is 250 x 2.0 / 400 = 1.25 mL, and the remaining 0.75 mL is platelet-poor plasma. Equal volumes would give 200 x10^9/L, and the other two combinations give 150 and 320 x10^9/L, so neither reaches the target.
  26. The correct answer is C (Residual ribosomal RNA, because immature red cells keep RNA that mature erythrocytes have lost): Reticulocytes have extruded the nucleus but still contain ribosomal RNA, which the fluorescent dye binds, and fluorescence intensity rises with RNA content, allowing maturity fractions to be reported. Mature red cells have little or no RNA. Reticulocytes do not carry more hemoglobin, retain no nucleus and do not have extra membrane lipid.
  27. The correct answer is B (Count cells on the top and left lines only, so each shared cell is counted once): Each boundary line is shared by two squares, so a rule is needed to give every cell to one square only. Counting cells touching the top and left lines and skipping those on the bottom and right lines does that. Counting all or none of the boundary cells would inflate or deflate the total.
  28. The correct answer is A (Touch the pipette tip at the coverslip edge, letting capillary action draw the fluid in): Touching the tip to the edge of the seated coverslip lets capillary action draw fluid evenly into the 0.1 mm space without bubbles. Forcing fluid or overfilling floats the coverslip and increases the depth. Dropping fluid on the grid before the coverslip traps air and alters the chamber volume.
  29. The correct answer is A (Stop and have the discrepancy resolved and corrected by nursing staff before drawing any blood): A name mismatch, even a single letter, means the wristband or the requisition may belong to a different person, so the discrepancy must be resolved by the responsible nurse before any blood is drawn. Relabelling afterwards hides a possible mix-up, and bed location is not a patient identifier.
  30. The correct answer is D (To give even, glare-free light with best resolution, because the condenser focuses it on the film): Kohler illumination aligns the light path so the specimen is evenly lit, with the field and condenser diaphragms set for maximum contrast and resolution without glare. It does not change the magnification, replace immersion oil, or work by maximizing brightness, which actually washes out contrast.
  31. The correct answer is B (Retest retained samples from that window and correct significant differences, because results may be biased): A failed control means results since the last acceptable control may be wrong, so retained samples from that period are retested and compared against the original results. Where differences are clinically significant, corrected reports are issued. Passing controls after repair do not show what happened earlier, and a mathematical correction is not a valid substitute for retesting.
  32. The correct answer is D (Make fresh buffer at the specified pH, then stain and accept a control smear): Buffer pH controls the balance between acid and basic dyes, so an out-of-range buffer must be replaced and the new staining checked on a control smear before patient films are stained. More dye or longer staining does not restore the correct balance. Yesterday's slides say nothing about today's reagents.
  33. The correct answer is A (A raised value shows fibrin was formed then lysed, because crosslinking must precede the fragment): D-dimer antibodies recognize a fragment that exists only after factor XIIIa has crosslinked fibrin and plasmin has then digested it. A raised value therefore shows both clot formation and lysis. Serum FDP assays also detect fibrinogen breakdown products, so they cannot make this distinction.
  34. The correct answer is C (Absolute count 17.4 x10^9/L from 62 percent of 28.0, which exceeds the limit so refer): An absolute count equals the percentage times the total WBC divided by 100, so 62 x 28.0 / 100 gives 17.4 x10^9/L. That exceeds the stated 10.0 x10^9/L limit and calls for referral. Dividing the percent by the WBC or using the whole WBC gives wrong values, and smudge cells do not remove the film from the policy.
  35. The correct answer is C (About 94 fL, calculated as hematocrit times 1000 divided by the RBC count): MCV is 0.34 x 1000 / 3.60, which gives 94.4 fL, a normocytic value. Dividing hemoglobin by the RBC count gives the MCH (30 pg), and dividing hemoglobin by hematocrit gives the MCHC (318 g/L). Using a factor of 100 instead of 1000 shrinks the answer tenfold.
  36. The correct answer is A (Hold the pipette vertically, pre-wet the tip, and immerse it a few millimetres): A vertical pipette with a tip immersed only a few millimetres, after pre-wetting, draws a consistent volume without extra liquid clinging to the outside. Fast plunger release causes bubbles and splashing, and a deep tip carries excess sample. Reusing tips risks carryover between samples.
  37. The correct answer is C (Pseudo-Pelger-Huet cells, because hypolobated and hypogranular neutrophils suggest myelodysplastic dysplasia in the marrow): Neutrophils with two round lobes and reduced granules are pseudo-Pelger-Huet cells, an acquired dysplastic change seen in myelodysplastic syndromes. Hypersegmented neutrophils have five or more lobes and point toward B12 or folate deficiency. Bands have a curved, unsegmented nucleus, and toxic neutrophils show heavy dark granulation rather than hypogranulation.
  38. The correct answer is C (At 12:00, four hours after the dose, because LMWH reaches peak anti-Xa activity about then): Low molecular weight heparin reaches its peak anti-Xa activity about four hours after a subcutaneous dose, and monitoring is meaningful at steady state, after the third or fourth dose. A draw at 12:00 therefore captures the peak. Earlier draws miss the peak, and a draw at 20:00 would measure a trough value instead.
  39. The correct answer is B (At least 15 minutes with eyelids held open, because the chemical must be washed out): Eye flushing should last at least 15 minutes with the eyelids held open so that the chemical is diluted and washed out of every fold. Pain can ease before the chemical is fully removed, so relief is not a reliable stopping point. Short rinses of 2 or 5 minutes leave residue that can keep injuring the eye.
  40. The correct answer is B (The first low result is falsely higher than the second and third low results): Carryover is residue of the high sample contaminating the next measurement, so only the first low result is raised, and the second and third return toward the true low value. Random scatter indicates imprecision instead. Contamination adds material, so low results become higher, not lower.
  41. The correct answer is A (Use reduced citrate volume, because there is less plasma to neutralize the citrate): In polycythemia little plasma is present, so a standard volume of citrate is in excess for the plasma and falsely prolongs the PT and aPTT. Reducing the citrate volume restores the correct ratio. EDTA cannot be used because it binds calcium and ruins coagulation tests, and adding citrate or underfilling would make the problem worse.
Case CHB13

A 38-year-old patient with a draining abscess has a CBC showing WBC 52 x10^9/L, hemoglobin 118 g/L and platelets 410 x10^9/L. The film shows many band neutrophils, a few metamyelocytes and myelocytes, toxic granulation and Dohle bodies, with 1 percent basophils and no blasts. A leukocyte alkaline phosphatase (LAP) score is requested and returns 240 (laboratory reference 20 to 100).

  1. The correct answer is C (Leukemoid reaction, because a high LAP score and toxic change indicate reactive neutrophilia): A LAP score of 240 is high, which is typical of a reactive leukemoid reaction, whereas it is low in chronic myeloid leukemia. Toxic granulation, Dohle bodies and a clear infection source also point to a reactive response, and basophils are not increased. Blasts are absent, which argues against acute leukemia.
  2. The correct answer is D (Repeat the CBC after treatment, because a reactive count falls as the infection resolves): A leukemoid reaction resolves when the cause is treated, so a repeat CBC and film after treatment is the simplest confirmation. Neither BCR-ABL1 testing nor marrow biopsy is needed when the LAP score is high, toxic change is present and there is no basophilia. Infection raises, not lowers, LAP.
  3. The correct answer is C (Issue a corrected report and notify the physician, because later findings point to a reactive process): A released report that contains an unsupported interpretation must be corrected through the laboratory's amended report process, with the clinician informed and the change documented. A high LAP score and no basophilia point to a reactive process, not CML. Deleting records or waiting leaves the clinician with misleading information.
  4. The correct answer is D (Run them as patient specimens with routine staff and methods, and submit without outside consultation): Survey samples are treated as patient specimens so the grade reflects everyday performance: routine staff, the routine procedure, and one measurement unless the patient procedure calls for more. Extra maintenance, duplicates, or consultation with another laboratory would show performance better than patients receive, and providers prohibit sharing results with other laboratories before submission.
  5. The correct answer is A (126 x10^9/L, calculated as 4.2 percent of the automated RBC count, giving absolute reticulocytes): 42 per 1000 cells is 4.2 percent, and 0.042 x 3.00 x10^12/L equals 126 x10^9/L. Reporting the raw count ignores the RBC count, and the other values come from decimal-place errors. The absolute count is the preferred measure of marrow output.
  6. The correct answer is C (Clinical urgency, so STAT CBC goes first, since delay may harm patients): Laboratories rank work by clinical urgency, and a STAT request from an emergency patient with chest pain needs a result that can change care within minutes. Arrival time and test simplicity do not reflect risk to the patient. Ward location also does not set priority, since a routine inpatient order can wait behind a STAT request.
  7. The correct answer is C (Refer the film and call the clinician, because a negative rapid test cannot exclude infection): Rapid antigen tests can miss low parasitemia or some species, so a negative kit does not override suspicious morphology in a symptomatic traveller. The safe path is to alert the clinician and get the film confirmed by an expert. Repeating the same kit or waiting for another draw delays diagnosis of a potentially fatal infection.
  8. The correct answer is D (Osmotic fragility or eosin-5-maleimide binding, because small dense cells suggest membrane loss): Small dense round cells without central pallor are spherocytes, and with a negative antiglobulin test and a family history, an inherited membrane defect is likely. Osmotic fragility or eosin-5-maleimide binding tests the membrane directly. Electrophoresis, the G6PD screen and the solubility test look for hemoglobin or enzyme disorders that do not produce this film.
  9. The correct answer is C (200 x10^9/L, above the 25 to 100 x10^9/L interval, consistent with a marrow response): The absolute count is 0.080 x 2.50 x10^12/L = 200 x10^9/L, which is well above the interval and fits the expected reticulocyte peak about a week after B12 therapy begins. The other values come from misplaced decimals. A value of 2000 would be implausible, but 200 is not.
  10. The correct answer is B (Swirl gently to mix and avoid foam, because bubbles can disturb optical clot detection): Lyophilized reagents are reconstituted by gentle swirling, since foam and bubbles interfere with light scatter and may lead to erratic clot times. Vigorous shaking or vortexing creates exactly that foam. Overheating a reagent can damage its factor and phospholipid content.
  11. The correct answer is C (Lipemia, because lipid turbidity adds light absorbance, falsely raising hemoglobin but not the red cell count): Lipid particles scatter and absorb light in the hemoglobin measurement, so the hemoglobin is falsely high while the hematocrit and RBC are unaffected, which pushes the calculated MCHC above 360 g/L. The milky plasma and the absence of clumps on the film exclude cold agglutinins. Hemolysis would leave pink plasma.
  12. The correct answer is C (Product identifier, pictograms, signal word, hazard statements, precautionary statements, and supplier identifier on every container): A WHMIS 2015 supplier label carries the product identifier, pictograms, signal word, hazard statements, precautionary statements and the supplier identifier. Expiry dates, lot numbers and phone numbers are useful but are not the required hazard elements. The safety data sheet adds detail, but it does not replace the hazard text on the label.
  13. The correct answer is B (Stability limits run from collection, so the age of the specimen cannot be judged without it): Every stability limit counts from the moment of collection, so without a time the technologist cannot tell whether the sample is within its limit. The result itself does not depend on the clock hour, and the computer record can be completed once the collector supplies the time.
  14. The correct answer is A (Review inversion technique with the site's staff and keep monitoring clotting occurrences): The problem is limited to one site that changed its technique, while other sites use the same lot successfully, so inadequate mixing is the more likely cause. Reviewing technique and continuing to monitor tests that hypothesis. Blaming the lot or changing tube type would ignore the evidence and would be invalid for CBC testing.
  15. The correct answer is B (Hold results, correct the interface unit mapping, review released reports, and notify affected clinicians): The numerals are right but the unit label is wrong by a thousandfold, so the interface mapping must be fixed at the source and previously released reports reviewed and corrected. Hand edits leave the cause in place. Multiplying the numbers would create a second error.
  16. The correct answer is C (A normal variant known as benign ethnic neutropenia, because infection risk is unchanged): A persistent mild neutropenia near 1.2 x10^9/L in a healthy person of African descent, without infection or other cytopenias, fits benign ethnic neutropenia (also called Duffy-null associated neutrophil count). It is a normal variant with no extra risk of infection. Early marrow failure and autoimmune neutropenia would be expected to affect other lines or cause infections, and cyclic neutropenia needs repeated counts that swing widely.
  17. The correct answer is D (Phone the responsible clinician, obtain a read back, and document the recipient name and time): Critical values must be communicated directly to a responsible clinician, with a read back to confirm accuracy and a record of the recipient and time. Posting to a routine report or relaying through a clerk leaves no verified receipt. Holding the result for a batch review delays care for a patient at risk of spontaneous bleeding.
  18. The correct answer is D (Excess citrate for the small plasma volume, so recollect with less citrate for the hematocrit): Standard citrate tubes are balanced for a normal hematocrit. When the hematocrit is above about 0.55 L/L the plasma fraction is small, so the fixed citrate volume over-chelates calcium and falsely prolongs the PT and aPTT. The fix is recollection with the citrate volume reduced for the hematocrit, not reporting a factor deficiency or repeating the test on the same plasma.
